<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Under GR, SBS, and NT, the domain
$(\mathbf E,\mathbf g)$ is isometric to the exterior of a nonextremal Kerr spacetime.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{discrete:continuation}\label{prop:continuation}
Assume GR, SBS, and NT, with $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$.
There exist a neighborhood $\mathcal O'\subset\mathcal O$ of
$S_0$ and a connected stationary open set $U\subset\mathbf E$ such that
\[
 (\mathcal O'\cap\mathbf E)\cup\{p\in\mathbf E:\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p)>0\}\subset U,
\]
and $\mathbf Z|_{\mathcal O'\cap\mathbf E}$ extends to a Killing field on $U$
which commutes with $\mathbf T$ and has complete flow of effective
period $2\pi$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prep:quotient}
Under GR, the $\mathbf T$-action on $\mathbf E$ is free and proper, and
$Q=\mathbf E/\mathbb R$ is a smooth simply connected three-manifold.
There is $\vartheta\in C^\infty(\mathbf E)$ such that
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbf T\vartheta&=1,\\
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d\vartheta,d\vartheta)&<0,
 \end{aligned}
\]
and
\[
 (\vartheta,\pi):\mathbf E\longrightarrow\mathbb R\times Q
       \text{ is a diffeomorphism}.
\]
The map $\pi|_{\Sigma_1}:\Sigma_1\to Q$ is proper and
surjective, and $\mathbf E$ is simply connected.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Fix a future timelike stationary orbit
$\gamma(s)=\Phi_s^\mathbf T p_\infty$ in the end. We claim that the
stationary time between two points of a compact set is bounded.
The same comparison will subsequently control the projection of
$\Sigma_1$.
The definition $\mathbf E=I^-(\mathbf M^{(\mathrm{end})})\cap
I^+(\mathbf M^{(\mathrm{end})})$, followed by timelike travel within
the end, gives
\begin{equation}\label{prep:comparison}
 \text{for each compact }C\subset\mathbf E,\quad
 \gamma(a)\ll p\ll\gamma(b)\quad(p\in C)
\end{equation}
for some $a<b$. If $p,\Phi_t^\mathbf T p\in C$ and $t>b-a$,
\[
 \gamma(a+t)\ll\Phi_t^\mathbf T p\ll\gamma(b)\ll\gamma(a+t),
\]
contradicting chronology. The reversed chain excludes
$t<a-b$. Hence the inverse image of a compact set under
$(t,p)\mapsto(p,\Phi_t^\mathbf T p)$ is closed in a compact set
$[-(b-a),b-a]\times C$. This proves properness. A nonzero
period is also excluded,
since its integer multiples would violate the same bound.
The free action therefore has a smooth Hausdorff quotient $Q$.

To obtain a spacelike section of this action, we construct a
temporal function $\vartheta$ satisfying $\mathbf T\vartheta=1$.
We start with a future increasing Cauchy temporal function $\tau$,
as supplied by Bernal and S\'anchez~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{BS05}, and
average its transition between two fixed levels. Here temporal
means that $d\tau$ is timelike. Choose
$\chi'\ge0$, $\chi=0$ on $(-\infty,-1]$, and $\chi=1$
on $[1,\infty)$, with $\chi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$. Define
\[
 \vartheta(p)=\int_\mathbb R
       [\chi(\tau(\Phi_s^\mathbf T p))-\chi(s)]\,ds.
\]
The orbit $\gamma$ is inextendible by properness, so
$\tau(\gamma(s))\to\pm\infty$. Applying \eqref{prep:comparison}
after stationary translation gives
\[
 \tau(\gamma(a+s))<\tau(\Phi_s^\mathbf T p)<\tau(\gamma(b+s)).
\]
Thus the integrand and its derivatives in $p$ have locally
uniform compact support in $s$, and
\[
 \vartheta(\Phi_t^\mathbf T p)-\vartheta(p)
 =\int_\mathbb R[\chi(s)-\chi(s-t)]\,ds=t,
\]
and differentiation under the integral gives
\[
 d\vartheta_p
 =\int_\mathbb R\chi'(\tau(\Phi_s^\mathbf T p))
                     [(\Phi_s^\mathbf T)^{\ast}d\tau]_p\,ds .
\]
The nonzero integrands in the formula for $d\vartheta_p$ lie in one timelike
cone and are not all zero, since $\mathbf T\vartheta=1$.
Writing them as $(a(s),v(s))$ in an orthonormal coframe,
\[
 \int a(s)\,ds>\int|v(s)|\,ds
                     \ge\left|\int v(s)\,ds\right|.
\]
Therefore $d\vartheta$ is timelike. The product map
$\mathbb R\times\{\vartheta=0\}\to\mathbf E$ is
\[
 (t,p)\longmapsto\Phi_t^\mathbf T p,
\]
and its inverse is
\[
 q\longmapsto(\vartheta(q),\Phi_{-\vartheta(q)}^\mathbf T q).
\]

To prove properness of $\pi|_{\Sigma_1}$, we first observe that the edgeless
acausal hypersurface $\Sigma^0$ is closed, since
Minguzzi~\cite[Proposition~2.132 and Definition~3.35]{Minguzzi19}
gives $\overline S\setminus S\subset\operatorname{edge}(S)$ for
achronal $S$. Hence $\Sigma_1$ is closed in $\mathbf E$. To control its stationary
parameter, we use an observation that also applies to the deformed
section in Appendix~\ref{app:completion}. Let $\mathcal S\subset\mathbf E$
be any nonempty acausal set, fix $u\in\mathcal S$, and let
$C\subset\mathbf E$ be compact. Choose $a<b$ from
\eqref{prep:comparison} for $C\cup\{u\}$. If
$p\in C$, $\Phi_t^\mathbf T p\in\mathcal S$, and $t>b-a$, then
\[
 u\ll\gamma(b)\ll\gamma(a+t)\ll\Phi_t^\mathbf T p,
\]
contrary to acausality. Reversing the chain excludes $t<a-b$.
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{prep:acausal-time-bound}
 |t|\le b-a
 \qquad(p\in C,\ \Phi_t^\mathbf T p\in\mathcal S).
\end{equation}
For compact $A\subset Q$, the section $\vartheta=0$ supplies
compact representatives $C\subset\mathbf E$. Apply
\eqref{prep:acausal-time-bound} with $\mathcal S=\Sigma_1$:
\[
 (\pi|_{\Sigma_1})^{-1}(A)
 \subset\{\Phi_t^\mathbf T p:p\in C,\ |t|\le b-a\}.
\]
Closedness proves properness, and GR gives surjectivity.

We can now use the topology of $\Sigma_1$. Its projection has
degree one, and lifting that map to the universal cover of $Q$
will show that this cover has one sheet. Orient $Q$ by $\mathbf T$ and
the spacetime orientation, and $\Sigma_1$ positively at infinity. An asymptotic orbit has
one transverse intersection with $\Sigma_1$, by acausality,
so $\deg(\pi|_{\Sigma_1})=1$. Since $\Sigma_1$ is simply
connected, the map lifts to the connected universal cover
$\varpi:\widetilde Q\to Q$. The lift
$F:\Sigma_1\to\widetilde Q$, with
$\varpi\circ F=\pi|_{\Sigma_1}$, is proper because
\[
 F^{-1}(B)\subset(\pi|_{\Sigma_1})^{-1}(\varpi(B))
\]
is closed and the right side is compact for compact $B$.
Let $q_\infty$ be an asymptotic regular value and let
$q_0\in\varpi^{-1}(q_\infty)$ be the lift hit by $F$.
The unique asymptotic intersection has positive Jacobian, so
\[
 \deg F=\sum_{x\in F^{-1}(q_0)}\operatorname{sgn}\det dF_x=1.
\]
For any other $q_1\in\varpi^{-1}(q_\infty)$,
\[
 F^{-1}(q_1)=\varnothing
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \deg F=\sum_{x\in F^{-1}(q_1)}\operatorname{sgn}\det dF_x=0,
\]
contradicting constancy of degree on the connected manifold
$\widetilde Q$. The covering has one sheet, so $\pi_1(Q)=0$ and
\[
\pi_1(\mathbf E)=\pi_1(\mathbb R\times Q)=0.\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{prep:collar}
Let $\exp$ be the exponential map of $\mathbf g$.
There exist $\delta>0$ and a $\mathbf Z$-invariant outward unit
spacelike normal $e_1$ to $S_0$ such that
\[
 \Sigma_{\rm loc}=\{\exp_p(re_1(p)):p\in S_0,\ 0<r<\delta\}
\]
is an acausal spacelike hypersurface and
\[
 \mathbb R\times\Sigma_{\rm loc}\longrightarrow\mathcal U_{\mathrm{loc}}
       \subset\mathbf E,\qquad (t,p)\longmapsto\Phi_t^\mathbf T p
\]
is a diffeomorphism onto an open set. The field $\mathbf Z$ extends
from $\mathcal O\cap\mathcal U_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to a Killing field on
$\mathcal U_{\mathrm{loc}}$ with $[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$ and complete flow
of effective period $2\pi$.
The normal exponential parametrization extends smoothly to
$\{0\}\times S_0$, and, for every compact $K\subset Q$,
\[
 \pi(\exp_p(re_1(p)))\notin K
       \quad(p\in S_0,\ 0<r<\delta_K)
\]
for some $\delta_K>0$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let $\mathbf K_0$ denote the Hawking field constructed by
Alexakis, Ionescu, and Klainerman~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{AIK09}.
Their horizon normalization is
\[
 \mathbf K_0=\underline uL-u\underline L
       \quad\hbox{on }(\mathcal H^+\cup\mathcal H^-)\cap\mathcal O.
\]
In particular, $\mathbf K_0|_{S_0}=0$.
Their Proposition~3.5 proves that
$\mathbf g(\mathbf K_0,\mathbf K_0)<0$ in the nearby exterior.
Propositions~4.1--4.2 give $[\mathbf T,\mathbf K_0]=0$ and a constant
$\lambda_0$ for which $\mathbf T+\lambda_0\mathbf K_0$ generates a
periodic action. If $t_0>0$ is its effective period, our choice
of sign and normalization is
\[
 \mathbf Z=\frac{t_0}{2\pi}(\mathbf T+\lambda_0\mathbf K_0).
\]
Here $\lambda_0\ne0$, since otherwise $\mathbf T$ would have periodic
orbits in $\mathbf E$, contrary to Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}.
We rescale the Hawking field by setting
\[
 \mathbf K=-\lambda_0\mathbf K_0
\]
and define the horizon angular velocity relative to $\mathbf Z$ by
\[
 \Omega_H=-\frac{2\pi}{t_0}.
\]
Thus our $\mathbf K$ is a constant multiple of their Hawking
field, normalized so that the coefficient of $\mathbf T$ is one:
\[
 \mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z.
\]
It vanishes on $S_0$, commutes with $\mathbf T$ and $\mathbf Z$, and satisfies
\[
 \mathbf g(\mathbf K,\mathbf K)
 =\lambda_0^2\mathbf g(\mathbf K_0,\mathbf K_0)<0
       \quad\hbox{in }\mathcal O\cap\mathbf E.
\]
Shrinking around the compact
fixed set $S_0$ gives both flows for $|s|\le 2\pi/|\Omega_H|$, and
\[
 \Phi_{2\pi/|\Omega_H|}^\mathbf T=
 \Phi_{2\pi/|\Omega_H|}^{\mathbf K}\Phi_{-2\pi\operatorname{sgn}\Omega_H}^\mathbf Z
 =\Phi_{2\pi/|\Omega_H|}^{\mathbf K}.
\]
Fix an exterior point $p$ in this neighborhood and choose
$a<b$ with $\gamma(a)\ll p\ll\gamma(b)$ as in
\eqref{prep:comparison}, where $\gamma$ is the timelike
stationary orbit in the asymptotic end. If $\mathbf K$ were
past directed, repeated stationary translates of this timelike
segment would give, for $2\pi n/|\Omega_H|>b-a$,
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \gamma(a+2\pi n/|\Omega_H|)\ll\Phi_{2\pi n/|\Omega_H|}^\mathbf T p\ll p,\\
 p\ll\gamma(b)\ll\gamma(a+2\pi n/|\Omega_H|),
 \end{gathered}
\]
contradicting \eqref{prep:comparison}. Thus $\mathbf K$
is future directed.

We choose the normal coordinates equivariantly: average a
future timelike normal to $S_0$ under the circle action, normalize
it to $e_0$, and let $e_1$ be the orthogonal unit normal pointing
into the exterior. The differential of $\mathbf K$ acts as a
boost on their plane, with positive rate $\kappa$. To see that
the rate is constant along $S_0$, we use the Killing identity:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 X^\alpha\mathbf D_\alpha\mathbf D_\beta\mathbf K_\gamma
 &=X^\alpha\mathbf R_{\gamma\beta\alpha\delta}\mathbf K^\delta=0
                    \qquad(X\in TS_0),\\
 \kappa^2&=-\tfrac12(\mathbf D_\alpha\mathbf K_\beta)
                              (\mathbf D^\alpha\mathbf K^\beta),\\
 X(\kappa^2)&=-(\mathbf D^\beta\mathbf K^\gamma)
                       X^\alpha\mathbf D_\alpha\mathbf D_\beta\mathbf K_\gamma=0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
In fact $\mathbf D_{e_0}\mathbf K=\kappa e_1$ and
$\mathbf D_{e_1}\mathbf K=\kappa e_0$ at $S_0$, whereas
$\mathbf D_X\mathbf K=0$ for $X\in TS_0$. Exponentiating this
endomorphism on the normal plane gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 d\Phi_t^{\mathbf K}e_0&=\cosh(\kappa t)e_0+\sinh(\kappa t)e_1,\\
 d\Phi_t^{\mathbf K}e_1&=\sinh(\kappa t)e_0+\cosh(\kappa t)e_1,\\
 d\Phi_t^{\mathbf K}
 \left(\frac{V-U}{2}e_0+\frac{V+U}{2}e_1\right)
 &=\frac{e^{\kappa t}V-e^{-\kappa t}U}{2}e_0
   +\frac{e^{\kappa t}V+e^{-\kappa t}U}{2}e_1.
 \end{aligned}
\]
We apply the exponential map equivariantly to obtain the exact
coordinate representation
\begin{equation}\label{prep:normal-coordinates}
 \begin{gathered}
 (U,V,p)\longmapsto
 \exp_p\left(\frac{V-U}{2}e_0+\frac{V+U}{2}e_1\right),\\
 \mathbf K=\kappa(V\partial_V-U\partial_U),\\
 \mathbf Z U=\mathbf Z V=0,\\ \mathbf T=\mathbf K-\Omega_H\mathbf Z.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The normal-exponential coordinates $U,V$ identify the exterior
with $U,V>0$ and the future horizon with $U=0$.
The function $\tau_1=(V-U)/2$ is temporal near $S_0$,
since $d\tau_1(e_0)=1$ and $|d\tau_1|_\mathbf g^2=-1$ there.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering

  \node at (-.13,-1.42) {causal past};

        at (1.2,.17) {$\Sigma_{\rm loc}$};

        variable=\s] plot ({sqrt(.55*.55+\s*\s)},\s);

  \draw[black!65,line width=.45pt] (1.47,.82)--(1.09,.82);
  \node[anchor=west,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt]
        at (1.5,-.82) {$0<UV<\delta^2$};
  \draw[black!65,line width=.45pt] (1.48,-.82)--(1.02,-.82);

  \node[anchor=east] at (-.35,-.03) {$S_0$};
  \node at (.27,-2.9) {(b) Enlargement of $\mathcal O$};
 \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The horizon neighborhood and the exterior section, with angular
variables suppressed. In (a), $\Sigma_1$ runs from $S_0$ to the
asymptotic end and continues past the dashed large-radius cutoff.
Panel (b) enlarges the normal chart
\eqref{prep:normal-coordinates}. The Hawking field is first constructed
in the lightly shaded future and past regions, and local rigidity gives $\mathbf Z$ throughout
$\mathcal O$. The segment $\Sigma_{\rm loc}=\{U=V\in(0,\delta)\}$
sweeps out the darker exterior region $0<UV<\delta^2$ under the
stationary flow. Both $\mathbf T$ and $\mathbf K$ project to the displayed
hyperbolas.}
\label{fig:local-horizon}
\end{figure}

To make $\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ acausal, we extend its local temporal
function. Acausality will then exclude repeated stationary
intersections by the timelike curve obtained from the Hawking flow
and the periodic rotation. We write $D(\Sigma^0)$ for its domain
of dependence in $\mathbf M$ and match the local function to a temporal
function $\tau_0$ there with zero level $\Sigma^0$.
Since $\Sigma^0$ is acausal and edgeless,
Minguzzi~\cite[Corollary~3.36, Proposition~3.43, and
Theorem~3.45]{Minguzzi19} shows that $D(\Sigma^0)$ is open,
causally convex and globally hyperbolic. The hypersurface
$\Sigma^0$ is a spacelike Cauchy hypersurface of this domain,
so the theorem of Bernal and S\'anchez~\cite[Theorem~1.2]{BS06} supplies
$\tau_0$ there with $\tau_0^{-1}(0)=\Sigma^0$.
For the following cutoff calculation, choose a smooth Riemannian
metric near $S_0$, use $|\cdot|_+$ for its norm, and write
$\ell=\operatorname{dist}_+(\cdot,S_0)$ in a tubular neighborhood.
For $0<r_1<r_2$, choose
$\chi=1$ on $\ell\le r_1$, $\chi=0$ on $\ell\ge r_2$, with
\[
\begin{aligned}
|\tau_1-\tau_0|&\le C\ell,\\
|d\chi|_+&\le\frac{C}{\ell\log(r_2/r_1)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
For example, take
$\chi=\eta(\log(\ell/r_1)/\log(r_2/r_1))$, where $\eta$ is
smooth, equals one on $(-\infty,0]$, and vanishes on $[1,\infty)$.
The estimate for $\tau_1-\tau_0$ follows from their common
zero value on $S_0$ and bounded first derivatives. Consequently
\[
 |(\tau_1-\tau_0)d\chi|_+
 \le C\ell\,\frac{\|\eta'\|_\infty|d\ell|_+}
                   {\ell\log(r_2/r_1)}
 \le\frac{C}{\log(r_2/r_1)}.
\]
Set $\tau=(1-\chi)\tau_0+\chi\tau_1$. Its differential is
\[
 d\tau
 =(1-\chi)d\tau_0+\chi d\tau_1
       +(\tau_1-\tau_0)d\chi.
\]
Here $r_2$ is fixed so that $d\tau_0$ and $d\tau_1$ are future
increasing timelike covectors on the closed tubular neighborhood.
For $0\le a\le1$, convexity of this cone and compactness give
constants $\mu,C_0>0$, independent of $r_1$, such that
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbf g^{-1}((1-a)d\tau_0+a d\tau_1,
                       (1-a)d\tau_0+a d\tau_1)&\le-\mu,\\
 |(1-a)d\tau_0+a d\tau_1|_+&\le C_0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The norm of $\mathbf g^{-1}$ is bounded on this same neighborhood.
Expanding the quadratic form at the displayed convex combination,
and using $|(\tau_1-\tau_0)d\chi|_+\le C/\log(r_2/r_1)$, gives
\[
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d\tau,d\tau)
 \le-\mu+\frac{C C_0}{\log(r_2/r_1)}
              +\frac{C}{\log(r_2/r_1)^2}\le-\frac\mu2
\]
once $r_1$ is sufficiently small. Scaling the last summand in
$d\tau$ by any number in $[0,1]$ obeys the same estimate, so
$d\tau$ stays in the same time-oriented cone. Extend $\tau$ by
$\tau_0$ outside this neighborhood. Its zero level agrees near $S_0$ with the normal
section in the statement and is acausal, also in $\mathbf M$ by
causal convexity.

We can now prove uniqueness of the stationary intersection.
Suppose $p,\Phi_t^\mathbf T p\in\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ and write
$t=2\pi n/|\Omega_H|+s>0$, $0\le s<2\pi/|\Omega_H|$. If $s>0$, then
\[
 \Phi_{-s\Omega_H}^\mathbf Z p
 \ll\Phi_s^{\mathbf K}\Phi_{-s\Omega_H}^\mathbf Z p
 =\Phi_s^\mathbf T p.
\]
Append the $n$ stationary translates of the full timelike
segment. If $s=0$, use those segments alone. In either case
two points of $\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ are chronologically related,
a contradiction. Reversal handles $t<0$. The product map
is transverse because $\mathbf Z$ is tangent and $\mathbf K$ is
timelike, so it is a diffeomorphism onto its open image.
On this image define
\[
 \mathbf Z_{\Phi_t^\mathbf T p}=d\Phi_t^\mathbf T|_p\mathbf Z_p.
\]
The injectivity just proved makes this definition single valued.
We check agreement with the original field by choosing
the original normal neighborhood to have $0<U,V<\delta$.
For a point $q$ in this neighborhood, put
\[
 s=\frac{1}{2\kappa}\log\frac{V(q)}{U(q)}.
\]
The local stationary flow from $q$ to parameter $-s$ stays in
the normal neighborhood: both normal coordinates lie between
their values at $q$ and $\sqrt{U(q)V(q)}<\delta$.
Its endpoint $p'$ satisfies
\[
 U(p')=V(p')=\sqrt{U(q)V(q)},
 \qquad p'\in\Sigma_{\rm loc}.
\]
Thus $q=\Phi_s^\mathbf T p'$ by a flow segment entirely in the original
neighborhood. If also $q=\Phi_t^\mathbf T p$ with
$p\in\Sigma_{\rm loc}$, injectivity of the stationary product
gives $(t,p)=(s,p')$. Along this local segment, $[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$ gives
$d\Phi_t^\mathbf T\mathbf Z_p=\mathbf Z_{\Phi_t^\mathbf T p}$ by differentiating the
pushforward in $t$. For the metric and bracket,
\[
\begin{aligned}
(\Phi_t^\mathbf T)^{\ast}(\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf g)
       &=\mathcal L_{(\Phi_t^\mathbf T)^{\ast}\mathbf Z}(\Phi_t^\mathbf T)^{\ast}\mathbf g
       =\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf g=0,\\
[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]&=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Rotational equivariance of the normal exponential map makes
$\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ invariant. Hence the extended rotational
flow is given for every $s\in\mathbb R$ by
\begin{align*}
\Phi_s^\mathbf Z(\Phi_t^\mathbf T p)&=\Phi_t^\mathbf T(\Phi_s^\mathbf Z p),\\
\shortintertext{so that}
\Phi_{2\pi}^\mathbf Z(\Phi_t^\mathbf T p)&=\Phi_t^\mathbf T p.
\end{align*}
A smaller period on the image would restrict to a smaller
period on the original neighborhood, so the period remains effective.

It remains to show that the end approaching $S_0$ leaves every
compact subset of $Q$. If instead $p_j\to S_0$ in this section
and $\pi(p_j)$ stays in a compact subset of $Q$, write
$p_j=\Phi_{t_j}^\mathbf T q_j$ with compact representatives $q_j$.
Since $\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ is acausal,
\eqref{prep:acausal-time-bound} gives a uniform bound for $|t_j|$.
Then $p_j$ has an interior limit in $\mathbf E$, contradicting
$p_j\to S_0$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:ergoregion}
The ergoregion and ergosurface are, respectively,
\[
 \{p\in\mathbf E:\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p)>0\},
\]
and
\[
 \{p\in\mathbf E:\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p)=0\}.
\]
The same terms denote their images in $Q$, and
$E_0=\pi(\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0\})$.
Sets defined by these scalars are understood in the ambient
space specified in each statement.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{canon:regularity}
The surface $E_0\subset Q$ is smooth and embedded. On
$\pi^{-1}(E_0)$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf g^{-1}(d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)],d\psi)&=0,\\
|d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2&=|d\psi|_\mathbf g^2>0.
\end{aligned}
\]
In particular, $d(\psi|_{E_0})\ne0$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
We compute at a point $p\in\pi^{-1}(E_0)$ in a null frame
adapted to $\mathbf T$, which is nonzero by
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}. Write
$\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=d\operatorname{Vol}_\mathbf g$ and choose an oriented null frame at $p$
$L=\mathbf T,\underline L,E_1,E_2$, with
$\mathbf g(L,\underline L)=-1$, $E_1,E_2$ orthonormal, and
$\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(L,\underline L,E_1,E_2)=-1$.
Writing $\mathbf F_{LA}=\mathbf F(L,E_A)$, $A\in\{1,2\}$, the contractions are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf D_\alpha[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]&=2\mathbf T^\beta\mathbf F_{\alpha\beta},\\
 \mathbf D_\alpha\psi&=\mathbf T^\beta\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\beta\alpha}{}^{\gamma\delta}\mathbf F_{\gamma\delta}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The chosen orientation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
 (\ast\mathbf F)_{L1}&=-\mathbf F_{L2},\\
 (\ast\mathbf F)_{L2}&=\mathbf F_{L1}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Here raising a null index interchanges $L,\underline L$ and
introduces a minus sign. Hence the Killing equation gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](L)&=d\psi(L)=0,\\
 d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](E_A)&=-2\mathbf g(\mathbf D_L\mathbf T,E_A),\quad A=1,2,\\
 d\psi(E_1)&=d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](E_2),\\
 d\psi(E_2)&=-d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](E_1).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Because both covectors annihilate $L$, their metric contractions
reduce to the two spacelike frame components. We obtain
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)],d\psi)
 &=-d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](L)d\psi(\underline L)
   -d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](\underline L)d\psi(L)\\
 &\quad+(-2\mathbf F_{L1})(-2\mathbf F_{L2})
            +(-2\mathbf F_{L2})(2\mathbf F_{L1})=0,\\
 |d\psi|_\mathbf g^2&=(-2\mathbf F_{L2})^2+(2\mathbf F_{L1})^2
                         =|d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
We obtain
\begin{equation}\label{canon:twist}
 \begin{aligned}
 |d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2=|d\psi|_\mathbf g^2
 &=4\sum_{A=1}^2\mathbf g(\mathbf D_L\mathbf T,E_A)^2,\\
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)],d\psi)&=0
       \qquad(\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
If the common value were zero, then
$\mathbf D^\alpha[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]=\lambda\mathbf T^\alpha$ for some $\lambda\in\mathbb R$.
Stationarity preserves this identity, with the same $\lambda$,
along $\gamma(t)=\Phi_t^\mathbf T p$. Equation~\eqref{local:killing-acceleration}
gives $\mathbf D_\mathbf T\mathbf T=-\lambda\mathbf T/2$. With $ds/dt=e^{-\lambda t/2}$
and hence $\gamma'(s)=e^{\lambda t/2}\mathbf T$, we obtain
\[
 \mathbf D_{\gamma'(s)}\gamma'(s)
 =e^{\lambda t}\left(\mathbf D_\mathbf T\mathbf T+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbf T\right)=0.
\]
The stationary orbit is complete and proper, so it has no
endpoint in $\mathbf E$. After the displayed reparametrization it is
therefore a maximal zero-energy null geodesic whose stationary
projection is a point. This contradicts NT and proves strict
positivity in \eqref{canon:twist}. The two orthogonal spacelike
gradients are then independent, proving smoothness of $E_0$
and $d(\psi|_{E_0})\ne0$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:conditional-pseudoconvexity}
Let $f$ be smooth near $p\in\mathbf E$, with
$\mathbf T f=0$, $f(p)=0$, and $df(p)\ne0$.
It is $\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex at $p$ if
\begin{equation}\label{local:convex}
 \mathbf D^2f(X,X)<0
 \quad\text{for every }X\in T_p\mathbf E\setminus\{0\}
 \text{ with }df(X)=\mathbf g(X,X)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,X)=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{local:extension}
Let $p\in V\subset\mathbf E$, with $V$ open, and let
$f\in C^\infty(V)$ satisfy $\mathbf T f=0$, $f(p)=0$ and $df(p)\ne0$.
Assume $\mathbf T(p)\ne0$ and \eqref{local:convex}.
If $\mathbf Z$ is a smooth Killing field on $V\cap\{f<0\}$ with
$[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$, then, after shrinking $V$ about $p$, it has a
unique Killing extension to $V$ commuting with $\mathbf T$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:stationary-metrics}
On $\pi(\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ne0\})\subset Q$, define
\begin{equation}\label{local:stationary-metrics}
\begin{aligned}
 \pi^{\ast}\bar h
 &=\mathbf g-\frac{\mathbf T^\flat\otimes\mathbf T^\flat}{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)},\\
 h&=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\bar h\quad\hbox{where }\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The differential $d\pi$ identifies $\bar h$ with $\mathbf g|_{\mathbf T^\perp}$.
Thus $\bar h$ is Riemannian where $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)<0$ and Lorentzian
where $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$. On the latter region,
\[
\pi^{\ast}h=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\mathbf g-\mathbf T^\flat\otimes\mathbf T^\flat.
\]
The Lorentzian metrics $h,\bar h$ have the same unparametrized
null geodesics.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:orbit-area}
Following Chru\'sciel and Costa~\cite{CC08} and
Chru\'sciel, Costa, and Heusler~\cite{CCH12}, define
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 W=W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]
 &:=-\det\begin{pmatrix}
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)&\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)\\
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)&\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)
 \end{pmatrix}\\
 &=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)^2-\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z).
 \end{aligned}
\]
On $W>0$, put $\rho=\sqrt W$. Here $W$ is the area function,
and $\rho$ is its positive square root. If $[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$, their
flow parameters $(\tau,\phi)$ give the absolute orbit area density
$\rho\,|d\tau\,d\phi|$.
On this set, let $\sigma$ be the Riemannian metric induced by $\mathbf g$ on
$(\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\})^\perp$.
Where the fields commute and are Killing, $\sigma$ also denotes
the metric on the local orbit space, defined by orthogonal lifts.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{local:complete-flows}
Let $U_1,U_2$ be open subsets of a smooth manifold, and let $X_i$
be a smooth complete vector field on $U_i$, $i=1,2$. If
$X_1=X_2$ on $U_1\cap U_2$, then, for every $t\in\mathbb R$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi_t^{X_1}|_{U_1\cap U_2}&=\Phi_t^{X_2}|_{U_1\cap U_2},\\
 \Phi_t^{X_1}(U_1\cap U_2)&=U_1\cap U_2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The field $X$ defined by $X|_{U_i}=X_i$ is complete on
$U_1\cup U_2$. If $P>0$ and
$\Phi_P^{X_i}=\operatorname{Id}_{U_i}$ for $i=1,2$, then
$\Phi_P^X=\operatorname{Id}_{U_1\cup U_2}$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
For $p\in U_1\cap U_2$, uniqueness gives agreement of the two
integral curves near $t=0$. If their maximal common interval had a
finite upper endpoint $b$, completeness and continuity would give
\[
 \Phi_b^{X_1}(p)
 =\lim_{t\uparrow b}\Phi_t^{X_1}(p)
 =\lim_{t\uparrow b}\Phi_t^{X_2}(p)
 =\Phi_b^{X_2}(p)\in U_1\cap U_2.
\]
Local uniqueness at this point extends their agreement past $b$.
The lower endpoint is treated in the same way. The common flow
therefore preserves the intersection, with equality obtained by
applying the inverse flow. Each point of the union already has its
complete orbit in one $U_i$, so these orbits define the complete
flow of $X$. Its period follows by restriction to $U_1$ and $U_2$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{local:compatibility}
Let $U\subset\mathbf E$ be stationary and open, with a smooth field
$\mathbf Z$ satisfying $\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf g=0$ and $[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$ on $U$.
Let $C\subset\partial U\cap\{\vartheta=0\}$ be compact,
where $\vartheta$ is the stationary temporal function of
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}. Suppose every $p\in C$ has
an open neighborhood $V_p$ carrying a Killing field $\mathbf Z_p$ with
$[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z_p]=0$ and $\mathbf Z_p=\mathbf Z$ on $V_p\cap U$.
Then the fields $\mathbf Z_p$ glue, after restriction, to a stationary
Killing field $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ on a stationary neighborhood $V$
of $C$, with $[\mathbf T,\widetilde\mathbf Z]=0$ and
$\widetilde\mathbf Z=\mathbf Z$ on $V\cap U$.
If $\mathbf Z$ has complete period-$2\pi$ flow on $U$ and the projected
local flow of $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ preserves $\pi(C)$, then $V$ can
be chosen invariant, with complete flow and
$\Phi_{2\pi}^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}=\operatorname{Id}_V$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We first glue the local fields near $C$, then use the compact
period interval to obtain an invariant neighborhood. Choose
finitely many local extensions $\mathbf Z_i$ covering $C$, and set
$\mathcal J_i=(\mathbf Z_i^\flat,\mathbf D(\mathbf Z_i^\flat))$, regarded as sections
of $T^{\ast}\mathbf M\oplus\Lambda^2T^{\ast}\mathbf M$ with the connection
\eqref{local:killing-connection}. At a point $p\in C$
in their common domain, choose $p_j\in U$ tending to $p$ there.
Then
\[
 \mathcal J_i(p)-\mathcal J_k(p)
 =\lim_{j\to\infty}
      (\mathcal J_i(p_j)-\mathcal J_k(p_j))=0.
\]
Killing transport gives equality on every overlap component
meeting $C$. To exclude the other components, use the stationary
product coordinates $(\vartheta,x)$ and restrict the local
domains to $(-\epsilon_i,\epsilon_i)\times V_i$. Choose
$V_i'\Subset V_i$ covering $\pi(C)$. On $V_i\cap V_k$, the
set where $\mathcal J_i(0,x)=\mathcal J_k(0,x)$ is both open and closed.
Hence
\[
 \{x\in\overline{V_i'}\cap\overline{V_k'}:
             \mathcal J_i(0,x)\ne\mathcal J_k(0,x)\}
\]
is compact and disjoint from $\pi(C)$. Remove these finitely
many compact sets from $\bigcup_iV_i'$ and restrict to a
neighborhood $V_0$ of $\pi(C)$. The local fields agree there.
Their components and the metric coefficients are independent of
$\vartheta$, so they define one Killing field on $\mathbb R\times V_0$.
For $x\in V_0\cap\pi(U)$, we have
$(\widetilde\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\widetilde\mathbf Z)=(\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\mathbf Z)$ at $(0,x)$. Killing transport along $\mathbb R\times\{x\}\subset U$
proves equality on the entire old overlap.

We next obtain a common flow interval before taking a limit of
the period identity. Choose a cutoff supported in $V_0$ and
equal to one on a relatively compact neighborhood of $\pi(C)$.
Multiplying the projection of $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ by this cutoff
gives a compactly supported field on $Q$.
Its complete flow is denoted by $\widetilde\Phi_s$.
The compact invariant set $\pi(C)$ stays where the cutoff is
one. Choose $\eta>0$ smaller than its distance to the complement
of this region, using $g_+$. Smooth dependence of the complete
cut-off flow, uniformly on the compact interval $[-2\pi,2\pi]$,
gives a neighborhood $O_0$ of $\pi(C)$ such that
\[
 \sup_{|s|\le2\pi}
  \operatorname{dist}_{g_+}(\widetilde\Phi_s x,\pi(C))<\eta
                         \qquad(x\in O_0).
\]
Thus these trajectories solve the original projected equation
for the whole period interval. The spacetime lift is
\[
 \Phi_s^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(\vartheta,x)
 =\left(\vartheta+
       \int_0^s(\widetilde\mathbf Z\vartheta)(\widetilde\Phi_vx)\,dv,
                    \widetilde\Phi_sx\right).
\]
The integrand and its derivatives are bounded on this fixed
compact family of trajectories. Thus the lift exists on
$[-2\pi,2\pi]\times(\mathbb R\times O_0)$ and depends smoothly on
its initial point. For $p\in C$, choose $p_j\in U\cap\{\vartheta=0\}$ tending
to $p$. The new and old flows agree by ODE uniqueness, so their
period identities pass to the limit in $C^1$:
\begin{align*}
 \Phi_{2\pi}^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(p)
 &=\lim_j\Phi_{2\pi}^{\mathbf Z}(p_j)=p,\\
 d\Phi_{2\pi}^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}|_p
 &=\lim_jd\Phi_{2\pi}^{\mathbf Z}|_{p_j}=\operatorname{Id}.
\end{align*}
The new flow preserves $\mathbf g$, since
\[
 \frac d{ds}(\Phi_s^{\widetilde\mathbf Z})^{\ast}\mathbf g
       =(\Phi_s^{\widetilde\mathbf Z})^{\ast}(\mathcal L_{\widetilde\mathbf Z}\mathbf g)=0.
\]
Isometry uniqueness makes its time-$2\pi$ map the identity on
a stationary neighborhood $O$ of $C$. Choose a smaller
stationary neighborhood $O'$ whose projected closure is
compact in $\pi(O)$ and whose flow segments for
$0\le s\le2\pi$ remain in the constructed domain. Set
\[
 V=\bigcup_{0\le s\le2\pi}\Phi_s^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(O').
\]
Every orbit segment from $O'$ closes after time $2\pi$.
Repeating this segment gives an integral curve for all real
times, and uniqueness identifies it with the flow from every
point on that segment. Hence $V$ is invariant and the flow is
complete and period-$2\pi$. Commutation with $\mathbf T$ makes $V$
stationary.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{local:free-action}
Let $U\subset\mathbf E$ be connected, stationary and open, and let
$\mathbf Z$ be Killing on $U$, with $[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0$ and complete effective
period $2\pi$. Suppose $\Sigma\subset\pi(U)$ is a smooth
cooriented surface, possibly with boundary, preserved by the
projected action, and $W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]>0$ on $\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)$.
Then there is an invariant open neighborhood $O$ of $\Sigma$
in $\pi(U)$ such that
\[
 \{s\in\mathbb R:\Phi_s^\mathbf Z(q)=q\}=2\pi\mathbb Z
 \qquad(q\in O).
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We first show that an element fixing a projected point fixes
its spacetime representatives. For $q=\pi(p)\in\Sigma$, let
$H_q\subset\mathbb R/(2\pi\mathbb Z)$ be the projected stabilizer.
Freeness of the stationary action defines $a:H_q\to\mathbb R$ by
\[
 \Phi_s^\mathbf Z p=\Phi_{a(s)}^\mathbf T p.
\]
Commutation makes $a$ a continuous homomorphism, and compactness
gives
\[
 |na(s)|=|a(ns)|\le\max_{H_q}|a|\qquad(n\in\mathbb Z).
\]
Thus $a=0$: every projected stabilizer also fixes $p$.

The plane spanned by $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$ is Lorentzian. Let $N$ be the
cooriented unit normal to $\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)$, and let $E$ complete
it to an orthonormal basis of $\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}^{\perp_\mathbf g}$.
For $s\in H_q$, the flow differential fixes $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z,N$.
It sends $E$ to $\pm E$, and preservation of orientation fixes
the positive sign. Hence $d\Phi_s^\mathbf Z|_p=\operatorname{Id}$. Isometry uniqueness
on the connected domain $U$ and effectiveness give
$s=0\bmod2\pi$.

Freeness persists near $q$. Otherwise there are
$q_j\to q$ and nonzero stabilizers $s_j\in\mathbb R/(2\pi\mathbb Z)$.
A subsequence converges to a stabilizer of $q$, so $s_j\to0$.
The projected field is nonzero at $q$, because $W>0$, and a
flow box has a coordinate $z^1$ in which $\mathbf Z z^1=1$.
On a smaller neighborhood there is $\epsilon>0$ such that all
flow segments with $|s|<\epsilon$ stay in this box. A fixed
point there would satisfy
\[
 0=z^1(\Phi_s^\mathbf Z(q'))-z^1(q')=s,
\]
contradicting $s_j\ne0$. The circle saturation of the union
of these free neighborhoods is open, invariant and free.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prep:residual}
Let $\pi:\mathbf E\to Q=\mathbf E/\mathbb R$ send each point to its $\mathbf T$-orbit.
Let $\mathbf Z$ be the rotational Killing field and $e_1$ the invariant
outward unit spacelike normal to $S_0$ from Lemma~\ref{prep:collar},
with corresponding stationary Gaussian coordinate $r$.
For sufficiently small $0<\delta_-<\delta_+<\delta$, where $\delta$
is the normal-coordinate radius in Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}, define in $Q$
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 C_\pm&=\pi\{\exp_p(re_1(p)):p\in S_0,\ 0<r<\delta_\pm\},\\
 \mathcal D_0&=\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}\setminus C_-,\\
  H_0&=\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}\setminus C_+.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Then $C_\pm$ are open, $\overline{C_-}\subset C_+$, and
$\pi^{-1}(C_+)$ is invariant under the complete flow of $\mathbf Z$,
of effective period $2\pi$. The sets $\mathcal D_0,H_0$
are compact, and $\mathcal D_0$ is a manifold with corners.
The relative boundary of $H_0$ in $\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}$ is a
smooth annulus, transverse to $E_0=\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0\}\subset Q$,
whose quotient by the induced circle action of $\mathbf Z$ is one compact arc.
On a spacetime neighborhood of the inverse image of this annulus,
the stationary Gaussian coordinate $r$ satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
\{r<\delta_+\}&\subset\pi^{-1}(C_+),\\
|dr|_\mathbf g^2&=1,\\
\mathbf D^2r(X,X)&<0
 \quad\bigl(0\ne X\in T\mathbf E,\ Xr=\mathbf g(X,X)=0\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
For every component $D$ of $\mathcal D_0$, the set
$D\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ is path connected, and $D$ has a
nonempty relatively open boundary portion in $\partial C_-$ at positive distance
from $H_0$ in the fixed auxiliary Riemannian metric $g_+$ on $Q$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We compute $\mathbf D^2r(X,X)$ for null tangent vectors $X$ in
coordinates adapted to the Hawking field. Use the invariant normals of Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}
and propagate $\exp_p(re_1)$ by $\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$.
The translates are disjoint by rotational invariance and
Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}. Let $t$ be the $\mathbf K$ parameter,
let $y^A$, $A=1,2$, be coordinates on $S_0$, and write
$E_A=\partial_{y^A}$. We use $\mathbf g|_{TS_0}$ for the induced
sphere metric, whose components are $\mathbf g_{AB}(0)$, and write
$\mathbf D_r=\mathbf D_{\partial_r}$. Along the normal geodesics, identify
vectors by $\mathbf D$-parallel
transport and recall the boost data
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf K(0)&=0,\\
 \mathbf D_r\mathbf K(0)&=\kappa e_0.
\end{align*}
Smoothness gives $\mathbf K(r)=\kappa r e_0+O(r^2)$ and
$E_A(r)=E_A(0)+O(r)$. Since $e_0\perp T S_0$,
\[
 \left.\partial_r\mathbf g(\mathbf K,E_A)\right|_{r=0}
 =\mathbf g(\kappa e_0,E_A(0))=0.
\]
The metric coefficients and their radial derivatives therefore
satisfy, uniformly on a finite cover of $S_0$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf g_{tt}&=-\kappa^2r^2+O(r^3),\\
 \partial_r\mathbf g_{tt}&=-2\kappa^2r+O(r^2),\\
 \mathbf g_{tA}&=O(r^2),\\
 \partial_r\mathbf g_{tA}&=O(r),\\
 \mathbf g_{AB}&=\mathbf g_{AB}(0)+O(r),\\
 \partial_r\mathbf g_{AB}&=O(1).
\end{align*}
The radial coefficients are exact. Since $[\partial_r,E_A]=0$
and $\partial_r$ is the unit velocity of a geodesic,
\[
 \partial_r\mathbf g(\partial_r,E_A)
 =\mathbf g(\partial_r,\mathbf D_{E_A}\partial_r)
 =\tfrac12 E_A\mathbf g(\partial_r,\partial_r)=0.
\]
The Killing equation similarly gives
\[
 \partial_r\mathbf g(\mathbf K,\partial_r)
 =\mathbf g(\mathbf D_r\mathbf K,\partial_r)=0.
\]
Both inner products vanish at $r=0$, so
\[
 \mathbf g=dr^2+\mathbf g_{tt}\,dt^2+2\mathbf g_{tA}\,dt\,dy^A
                              +\mathbf g_{AB}\,dy^A dy^B.
\]
The coordinate $r$ is invariant under $\mathbf K,\mathbf Z$ and
hence under $\mathbf T$.

Let $X=X^t\mathbf K+Y^AE_A$ be null and tangent to a level of $r$,
and write $|Y|^2=\mathbf g_{AB}(0)Y^AY^B$. The coefficient bounds above
give
\[
 0=-\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2+|Y|^2
       +O(r^3(X^t)^2+r^2|X^t||Y|+r|Y|^2).
\]
The mixed term is bounded using
$2\kappa r|X^t||Y|\le\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2+|Y|^2$.
There is consequently a fixed constant $C_1$, depending only
on the metric in the chosen normal neighborhood and $\kappa$,
such that
\[
 \left|\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2-|Y|^2\right|
 \le C_1r\bigl(\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2+|Y|^2\bigr).
\]
For $C_1r\le1/2$, moving the first square to the left gives
\[
 \frac13|Y|^2\le\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2\le3|Y|^2,
 \qquad
 \left|\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2-|Y|^2\right|
                                      \le4C_1r|Y|^2.
\]
These bounds control the apparent singularity in $X^t$.
Since $\mathbf g_{rr}=1$ and $\mathbf g_{rt}=\mathbf g_{rA}=0$, the second
fundamental form of a level of $r$ is one half its radial
metric derivative. Hence
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbf D^2r(X,X)
 &=\tfrac12(\partial_r\mathbf g_{tt})(X^t)^2
       +(\partial_r\mathbf g_{tA})X^tY^A
       +\tfrac12(\partial_r\mathbf g_{AB})Y^AY^B,\\
 \left|\mathbf D^2r(X,X)+\kappa^2r(X^t)^2\right|
 &\le C\bigl(r^2(X^t)^2+r|X^t||Y|+|Y|^2\bigr)
 \le C_2|Y|^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Substituting the null equation cancels the leading time component:
\begin{equation}\label{prep:strict-calculation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \left|\mathbf D^2r(X,X)+\frac{|Y|^2}{r}\right|
 &\le\left|\mathbf D^2r(X,X)+\kappa^2r(X^t)^2\right|
       +\frac{\left|\kappa^2r^2(X^t)^2-|Y|^2\right|}{r}\\
 &\le(C_2+4C_1)|Y|^2,\\
 \mathbf D^2r(X,X)&\le-\frac{|Y|^2}{2r}<0
              \qquad(X\ne0).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For the last line choose $2(C_2+4C_1)r\le1$; a nonzero null
$X$ has $Y\ne0$ by the preceding comparison. Compactness of
$S_0$ and invariance under the Hawking flow make $C_1,C_2$,
and thus the allowed positive range of $r$, independent of the
point and the null tangent vector.
In particular, at the level $r=\delta_+$ in the statement,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf D^2(r-\delta_+)&=\mathbf D^2r,\\
|d(r-\delta_+)|_\mathbf g^2&=1,\\
\{r<\delta_+\}&\subset\pi^{-1}(C_+).
\end{aligned}
\]
We can therefore apply Proposition~\ref{local:extension} at
$r=\delta_+$: the strict inequality holds, and the field is defined
on the entire local side $r<\delta_+$.

The restriction of the circle action to $S_0\simeq S^2$ has
exactly two fixed points. Indeed, at a zero of $\mathbf Z|_{S_0}$ its
differential is a nonzero skew rotation, so the zero is isolated
with index one; the index theorem gives two zeros. The action on
the Lorentzian normal plane is trivial, since a compact connected
subgroup of its orientation-preserving Lorentz group is trivial.
On $T_pS_0$ the weight is $\pm1$: a larger weight would give a
nonidentity circle element fixing $p$ with identity differential
on all of $T_p\mathbf M$, contrary to effectiveness and isometry uniqueness.
Consequently, at either pole $p$ and for $V\in T_pS_0$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf D_V\mathbf T&=-\Omega_H\mathbf D_V\mathbf Z,\\
 \mathbf g(\mathbf D_V\mathbf Z,\mathbf D_V\mathbf Z)&=\mathbf g(V,V),\\
 \operatorname{Hess}_{S_0}[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]_p(V,V)
 &=2\mathbf g(\mathbf D_V\mathbf T,\mathbf D_V\mathbf T)=2\Omega_H^2\mathbf g(V,V).
\end{align*}
Here $\mathbf T(p)=0$, so the restriction of the spacetime Hessian
is the intrinsic Hessian. These local facts are all we need
from the axis geometry.

It remains to describe the part of this level in the ergoregion.
We show that it is an annulus meeting $E_0$ transversely. Identify
each small positive level of $r$ in $Q$ with $S_0$ by
$p\mapsto\pi(\exp_p(re_1(p)))$ and transport geodesic coordinates $y^1,y^2$
from either pole. At the pole on $r=0$,
\[
 \partial_{y^A}\partial_{y^B}[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]
 =2\Omega_H^2\delta_{AB}.
\]
Hence the coordinate Hessian remains bounded below by $c>0$
times the Euclidean metric on a fixed disk, for all sufficiently
small $r\ge0$.
Its center remains on the axis, so rotational invariance
gives $\partial_y[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](r,0)=0$, whereas
\[
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(r,0)=\mathbf g(\mathbf K,\mathbf K)(r,0)<0
 \qquad(r>0).
\]
The norm remains positive on the compact complement of the
two coordinate disks, including their boundaries, for small $r$.
For every Euclidean unit vector $\omega$ in the coordinate plane,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{d}{d\lambda}\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(r,\lambda\omega)
 &=\int_0^\lambda
   \partial_{y^A}\partial_{y^B}[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)](r,t\omega)
                     \omega^A\omega^B\,dt\\
 &\ge c\lambda.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Each radial segment therefore crosses the zero set exactly
once and transversely. The two zero sets are smooth circles,
and the part of this level where $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$ is the annulus
between the disks they enclose.
The stationary lift of each level has invariant radial
coorientation. On the closed annulus,
\[
 \mathbf g(\mathbf K,\mathbf K)<0\le\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T),
\]
with
\[
 \mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z.
\]
Thus $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$ are independent and span a Lorentzian plane.
Lemma~\ref{local:free-action}, applied
on the connected local stationary neighborhood, makes the projected
action free on this annulus.
The quotient of this compact connected annulus by the free
circle action is a compact connected one-dimensional manifold
with boundary. Each boundary circle is preserved: a continuous
circle action cannot interchange the two components. Its
orbits are open in that boundary circle by nonvanishing of the
generator and closed by compactness, so each boundary circle
is one orbit. The quotient therefore has two boundary points
and is a compact interval.
Choose $0<\delta_-<\delta_+$ sufficiently small and let $C_\pm$ be the
stationary images of $0<r<\delta_\pm$. These are the required nested
neighborhoods, and their boundary hypersurfaces satisfy
\eqref{prep:strict-calculation}.

For compactness of $\mathcal D_0$ and $H_0$, we examine their
two possible ends. At the
horizon end, the exact boost coordinates
\eqref{prep:normal-coordinates} move every sufficiently nearby
point into the neighborhood parametrized by $0<r<\delta_-$:
\begin{align*}
\Phi_t^{\mathbf K}(U,V,p)&=(e^{-\kappa t}U,e^{\kappa t}V,p),\\
\shortintertext{so that}
\Phi_{\frac1{2\kappa}\log(U/V)}^{\mathbf K}(U,V,p)
        &=(\sqrt{UV},\sqrt{UV},p).
\end{align*}
Throughout this segment both normal coordinates stay between
$\min(U,V)$ and $\max(U,V)$. The endpoint lies on
$\Sigma_{\rm loc}$ at $r=\sqrt{UV}$. Since the $\mathbf K$-
and $\mathbf T$-saturations coincide, every sequence in $\Sigma_1$
approaching $S_0$ eventually projects into $C_-$.
At infinity $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)<0$. Hence, for some $\epsilon>0$,
$R<\infty$,
\[
 \mathcal D_0,\ H_0\ \subset\
 \pi\bigl(\Phi_0(\{1+\epsilon\le|x|\le R\})\bigr).
\]
Both are closed and thus compact. The transverse
intersections with the smooth ergosurface make
$\mathcal D_0$ a manifold with corners.

A connected manifold with corners is path connected. Join two
points of $D\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ by a path in $D$.
Near its intersections with the face $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0$, use
$\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)$ as one boundary coordinate. At a corner, keep
the other boundary coordinate, $r-\delta_-$, fixed. A partition
of unity in finitely many such charts gives a smooth vector
field $V$ satisfying, on the two faces near this path,
\[
 V[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]>0\quad\hbox{on }\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0\},
 \qquad Vr=0\quad\hbox{on }\{r=\delta_-\}.
\]
Choose its cutoff to equal one near the intersections of the
path with $\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0\}$ and vanish near the two endpoints. Its flow
for a sufficiently small positive time preserves $D$ and moves
every point of the path where $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0$ into
$\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$. Points already in this set stay there:
a first crossing of the zero face would contradict the
displayed strict derivative.
It therefore supplies the required path in
$D\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ with the original endpoints.

Finally, every component $D$ of $\mathcal D_0$ meets
$\partial C_-$. Otherwise $\partial D$ is a nonempty compact
surface contained in $E_0$: it is nonempty because $D$ is compact
and $Q$ is connected and noncompact. At each point of $E_0$ the
regularity in Lemma~\ref{canon:regularity} gives a single positive
side, so each component of $\partial D$ is both open and closed
in $E_0$. On this compact surface $\psi$ attains a maximum,
contradicting $d(\psi|_{E_0})\ne0$.
At an intersection of $D$ with $\partial C_-$, transversality to
$E_0$ supplies points with $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$. A small relatively
open boundary portion there has closure in $C_+$ and hence
positive $g_+$-distance from $H_0$. Compactness of the manifold
with corners $\mathcal D_0$ gives finitely many components, so
these distances have a common positive lower bound.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{exit:regular-completion}
Assume GR and SBS. The spacetime $(\mathbf E,\mathbf g)$ embeds isometrically
as the domain of outer communications of a smooth stationary
vacuum spacetime which is $I^+$-regular in the sense of
Chru\'sciel and Costa~\cite[Definition~1.1]{CC08} and has one connected nondegenerate
future horizon. Its stationary field restricts to $\mathbf T$.
If $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$, the circle action generated by the field
$\mathbf Z$ of Lemma~\ref{prep:collar} extends to a neighborhood of this
horizon.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{action:area}
The circularity identities
\begin{equation}\label{action:circularity}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat&=0,\\
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d\mathbf Z^\flat&=0
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
hold on $U$. On $U\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}$ one has
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)&>0,\\
W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]&>0,
\end{aligned}
\]
and the distribution $\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}^{\perp_\mathbf g}$ is integrable.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We first derive circularity in a form that also applies at a
point where $\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}$ is degenerate.
For either Killing field $X=\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$,
the vacuum computation preceding Lemma~\ref{canon:regularity}
gives $d(\ast dX^\flat)=0$ and
$\mathcal L_X(\ast dX^\flat)=0$. In particular,
\[
 d\bigl(\iota_X(\ast dX^\flat)\bigr)
  =\mathcal L_X(\ast dX^\flat)-\iota_Xd(\ast dX^\flat)=0.
\]
The contraction identity
$\iota_X(\ast dX^\flat)=\ast(X^\flat\wedge dX^\flat)$ identifies
this with the twist one-form. Commutation gives
$\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf T^\flat=0$ and $\mathcal L_\mathbf T\mathbf Z^\flat=0$.
Applying Cartan's formula, we obtain
\[
 d\bigl(\iota_\mathbf Z\ast(\mathbf T^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat)\bigr)
 =\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\ast(\mathbf T^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat)
       -\iota_\mathbf Z d\ast(\mathbf T^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat)=0,
\]
and similarly
\[
 d\bigl(\iota_\mathbf T\ast(\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d\mathbf Z^\flat)\bigr)=0.
\]
On $S_0$ the relation $\mathbf T=-\Omega_H\mathbf Z$ gives
\[
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat=0,
\]
so both constant twist contractions have zero limit there.
They consequently vanish on the connected set $U$. This proves
\eqref{action:circularity}.
Where $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$ are independent, let $A,B$ be smooth local
sections of $\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}^{\perp_\mathbf g}$ and choose $E_1,E_2$ with
\[
 \det\begin{pmatrix}
 \mathbf T^\flat(E_1)&\mathbf T^\flat(E_2)\\
 \mathbf Z^\flat(E_1)&\mathbf Z^\flat(E_2)
 \end{pmatrix}\ne0.
\]
For $X=\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$, evaluation of the four-form gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=(\mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge dX^\flat)(E_1,E_2,A,B)\\
  &=\det\begin{pmatrix}
 \mathbf T^\flat(E_1)&\mathbf T^\flat(E_2)\\
 \mathbf Z^\flat(E_1)&\mathbf Z^\flat(E_2)
 \end{pmatrix}dX^\flat(A,B),\\
 \mathbf g(X,[A,B])
 &=A\mathbf g(X,B)-B\mathbf g(X,A)-dX^\flat(A,B)=0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The common orthogonal distribution is involutive wherever
the two fields are independent. Recalling the twist potential
$d\psi=\ast(\mathbf T^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat)$, we also obtain
$\mathbf Z\psi=0$ on $U$.

Where $\mathbf Z\ne0$, its periodic orbit is spacelike by causality:
$\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)$ is constant on the orbit, and a nonpositive value
would give a closed causal curve. The fields $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$ are
independent there, since commutation and dependence at a point
would give a periodic stationary orbit, contrary to
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}.
We claim that their plane is nondegenerate. If it were
degenerate at $p$, we could follow its null direction by fixing
\[
 \Omega=-\frac{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)}{\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}(p)
\]
and put $V=\mathbf T+\Omega\mathbf Z$. This nonzero Killing field
spans the null direction of that plane at $p$. Since $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$
are complete on $U$ and commute, $V$ is complete. Its flow
preserves both fields, so along the whole orbit through $p$,
\[
 \mathbf g(V,V)=\mathbf g(V,\mathbf Z)=\mathbf g(V,\mathbf T)=0.
\]
Circularity and the Killing equation give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 V^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge dV^\flat&=0,\\
 \iota_VdV^\flat&=-d[\mathbf g(V,V)],\\
 0=\iota_V(V^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge dV^\flat)
   &=-V^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d[\mathbf g(V,V)].
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus $d[\mathbf g(V,V)]$ is a linear combination of
$V^\flat,\mathbf Z^\flat$. Commutation shows that it annihilates
$\mathbf Z$. Since
$\mathbf g(V,\mathbf Z)=0$ and $\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)>0$, its $\mathbf Z^\flat$
coefficient must vanish. Along this orbit,
$d[\mathbf g(V,V)]=\lambda V^\flat$ for a smooth scalar $\lambda$,
so \eqref{local:killing-acceleration} gives
$\mathbf D_VV=-\lambda V/2$.
The orbit is a reparametrized null geodesic of zero stationary
energy. Recall the temporal coordinate $\vartheta$ of
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}, normalized by $\mathbf T\vartheta=1$.
It satisfies
\[
 \vartheta(\Phi_s^V p)
       =s+\vartheta(\Phi_{\Omega s}^{\mathbf Z}p).
\]
The second term is bounded by periodicity, so this geodesic
has no endpoint in $\mathbf E$ and is maximal as an unparametrized
geodesic. Its stationary projection lies in one compact
$\mathbf Z$-orbit, contrary to NT.

To determine the signature on the whole domain, we also need
the local structure of the axis. At a fixed point $p$, we average
a future timelike vector $V$:
\[
 \widetilde V=\int_0^{2\pi}d\Phi_s^\mathbf Z|_p V\,ds.
\]
The vector $\widetilde V$ is future timelike and fixed.
Normalize it and choose an orthonormal basis in
$\widetilde V^\perp$. The circle
representation has the form
\[
 d\Phi_s^\mathbf Z|_p=\operatorname{diag}
 \left(1,1,
 \begin{pmatrix}\cos(ms)&-\sin(ms)\\\sin(ms)&\cos(ms)\end{pmatrix}\right),
 \qquad m\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}.
\]
The case $m=0$ would make every isometry the identity by its
value and differential at $p$. The fixed space is therefore
a Lorentzian two-plane. Since
\[
 \Phi_s^\mathbf Z(\exp_p v)=\exp_p(d\Phi_s^\mathbf Z|_p v),
\]
the local fixed set is the exponential image of that plane,
so the axis is smooth of codimension two.
Perturbing paths off this submanifold shows that its complement
is connected. The determinant never vanishes there.
Near $S_0$, the span contains the timelike field
$\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$, so it is timelike everywhere
off the axis.

Suppose an axis point $p$ lies in the ergoregion. Choose a unit
vector $E_1$ in the rotated spacelike plane and let $E_2$ be its
orthogonal rotation, with weight $m\ne0$. Along
$\gamma(r)=\exp_p(rE_1)$, parallel transport gives
\[
 \mathbf Z(\gamma(r))=mrE_2+O(r^2).
\]
The stationary vector $\mathbf T(p)$ lies in the fixed plane. Therefore
\[
 \left.\frac d{dr}\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)(\gamma(r))\right|_{r=0}
 =\mathbf g(\mathbf D_{E_1}\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)(p)+\mathbf g(\mathbf T,mE_2)(p)=0.
\]
It follows that
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)(\gamma(r))&=O(r^2),\\
 \mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)(\gamma(r))&=m^2r^2+O(r^3),\\
 W(\gamma(r))&=-\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p)m^2r^2+O(r^3)<0
\end{align*}
for small $r>0$, contradicting positivity off the axis.
At an axis point on the ergosurface, both $\mathbf T$ and
$\bigl(d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]\bigr)^\sharp$ lie in the fixed Lorentzian two-plane.
Since $\mathbf T\ne0$ is null and annihilates $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)$,
$\bigl(d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]\bigr)^\sharp\in\mathbb R\mathbf T$, and hence
\[
|d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2=0,
\]
contrary to the strict positivity proved after
\eqref{canon:twist}. This excludes the axis throughout the
set in the statement.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{local:optical-curvature}
Let $\Sigma$ be a smooth $\mathbf Z$-invariant timelike surface in
$\pi(U\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\})$, represented locally by a curve
in a transverse surface with metric $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$. Let
$e_{\mathrm{opt}},N_{\mathrm{opt}}$ be the unit tangent and normal
of this curve for $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$, identified with their
$\phi$-independent lifts to the product chart. Define the signed
geodesic curvature by
\[
 \nabla^{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}_{e_{\mathrm{opt}}}e_{\mathrm{opt}}
       =\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}N_{\mathrm{opt}}.
\]
Define its $h$-unit normal and null tangents by
\begin{align*}
 n&=\rho^{-1}N_{\mathrm{opt}},\\
 e_4&=\frac{-\partial_\phi+e_{\mathrm{opt}}}{\sqrt2\rho},\\
 e_3&=\frac{-\partial_\phi-e_{\mathrm{opt}}}{\sqrt2\rho},
\end{align*}
and put $B(X,Y)=-h(\nabla_Xn,Y)$ for $X,Y\in T\Sigma$.
Writing $B_{ab}=B(e_a,e_b)$ for $a,b\in\{3,4\}$,
we have $h(e_3,e_4)=-1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{local:optical-null-curvature}
 B_{33}=B_{44}=\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}}{2\rho}.
\end{equation}
If $f$ is a smooth defining function for $\Sigma$, with $\mathbf Z f=0$
and $nf>0$, its pullback by $\pi$ satisfies on $\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)$
\begin{equation}\label{flow:null-extension}
 \mathbf D^2(f\circ\pi)(X,X)=-(nf)B(d\pi X,d\pi X)
 \quad\bigl(X(f\circ\pi)=\mathbf g(X,X)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,X)=0\bigr).
\end{equation}
Thus $\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}>0$ is equivalent to
\eqref{local:convex}. If $\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}\equiv0$, the integral curves of
$e_3$ and $e_4$ are null geodesics of $(Q,h)$ up to reparametrization.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
For this calculation write $\widehat h=\rho^{-2}h$ and let
$\widehat\nabla$ be its connection. The conformal connection
calculation in \eqref{local:conformal-connection}, now with
$h=\rho^2\widehat h$, gives
\[
 \nabla_XY
 =\widehat\nabla_XY+(X\log\rho)Y+(Y\log\rho)X
            -\widehat h(X,Y)\operatorname{grad}_{\widehat h}\log\rho.
\]
Extend $N_{\mathrm{opt}}$ independently of $\phi$. For $X\in T\Sigma$,
$\widehat h(X,N_{\mathrm{opt}})=0$, and differentiation of
$n=\rho^{-1}N_{\mathrm{opt}}$ gives the cancellation
\begin{align*}
 \nabla_Xn
 &=-\rho^{-1}(X\log\rho)N_{\mathrm{opt}}
     +\rho^{-1}\widehat\nabla_XN_{\mathrm{opt}}
     +\rho^{-1}(X\log\rho)N_{\mathrm{opt}}
     +\rho^{-1}(N_{\mathrm{opt}}\log\rho)X\\
 &=\rho^{-1}\bigl\{\widehat\nabla_XN_{\mathrm{opt}}
                     +(N_{\mathrm{opt}}\log\rho)X\bigr\}.
\end{align*}
Hence, for tangent $X,Y$,
\[
 B(X,Y)=-\rho\widehat h(\widehat\nabla_XN_{\mathrm{opt}},Y)
             -\rho(N_{\mathrm{opt}}\log\rho)\widehat h(X,Y).
\]
For $a=3,4$, the second term vanishes when $X=Y=e_a$.
The connection of $-d\phi^2+q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ and
$q_{\mathrm{opt}}(e_{\mathrm{opt}},N_{\mathrm{opt}})=0$ therefore give
\begin{align*}
 B(e_a,e_a)
 &=-\frac1{2\rho}q_{\mathrm{opt}}
        (\nabla^{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}_{e_{\mathrm{opt}}}N_{\mathrm{opt}},
                                                   e_{\mathrm{opt}})\\
 &=\frac1{2\rho}q_{\mathrm{opt}}
        (N_{\mathrm{opt}},\nabla^{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}_{e_{\mathrm{opt}}}
                                             e_{\mathrm{opt}})
 =\frac{\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}}{2\rho}.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{local:optical-null-curvature}. The normalization
$h(e_3,e_4)=-1$ agrees with the normalization of the horizon null
pair in Alexakis, Ionescu, and Klainerman~\cite{AIK09}.
Here $e_3,e_4$ are tangent to a timelike surface in $(Q,h)$,
and $B$ differentiates its spacelike normal. The horizon forms
$\chi_{AB},\underline\chi_{AB}$ of
\eqref{prep:null-second-forms} instead differentiate null normals
to spacelike two-surfaces in spacetime.

For the defining function in the statement, $\nabla f=(nf)n$
on $\Sigma$, where $nf=df(n)$. By
\eqref{local:stationary-hessian},
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf D^2(f\circ\pi)(X,X)
 &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf(d\pi X,d\pi X)\\
 &=h\bigl(\nabla_{d\pi X}((nf)n),d\pi X\bigr)\\
 &=(nf)h(\nabla_{d\pi X}n,d\pi X)
 =-(nf)B(d\pi X,d\pi X).
\end{align*}
The conormal is spacelike, since
\[
 \mathbf g^{-1}(\pi^{\ast}df,\pi^{\ast}df)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)h^{-1}(df,df)
              =\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(nf)^2>0.
\]
Every nonzero $h$-null vector tangent to $\Sigma$ is proportional
to $e_3$ or $e_4$, proving the equivalence with \eqref{local:convex}.

If $\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}\equiv0$, set
$Y=-\partial_\phi+e_{\mathrm{opt}}$. The product connection gives
\begin{align*}
 \widehat\nabla_YY
 &=\nabla^{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}_{e_{\mathrm{opt}}}e_{\mathrm{opt}}=0,\\
 \nabla_YY&=2(Y\log\rho)Y.
\end{align*}
Since $e_4=Y/(\sqrt2\rho)$, its acceleration is
\[
 \nabla_{e_4}e_4
 =\frac{\nabla_YY-(Y\log\rho)Y}{2\rho^2}
 =(e_4\log\rho)e_4.
\]
Replacing $e_{\mathrm{opt}}$ by $-e_{\mathrm{opt}}$ gives
$\nabla_{e_3}e_3=(e_3\log\rho)e_3$. Thus both normalized
null fields have integral curves that are geodesics up to
reparametrization.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{action:escape}
The equation
\begin{equation}\label{action:normalized-flow}
 \mathbf D_vv=-\tfrac12(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)v
\end{equation}
defines a smooth stationary local flow on the $\mathbf g_+$-unit
tangent bundle and preserves
\[
 \mathbf g(v,v)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,v)=0.
\]
A trajectory whose projection remains in a compact $K\subset Q$
has a limiting position and unit velocity modulo stationary
translations at every finite parameter endpoint, and its
spacetime lift extends through that endpoint.
Under NT, there is $C_K<\infty$, depending only on $K$, the
stationary geometry and $\mathbf g_+$, such that every zero-energy
null geodesic segment $\gamma:I\to\mathbf E$ satisfies
\[
 \pi(\gamma(I))\subset K\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
                 \int_I|\dot\gamma|_{\mathbf g_+}\,ds\le C_K.
\]
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We use the unit initial data to pass to limits of geodesics.
If an affinely parametrized geodesic is reparametrized by
$\mathbf g_+$-arclength, its velocity $v$ satisfies
$\mathbf D_vv\in\mathbb R v$. Differentiating $\mathbf g_+(v,v)=1$
then determines the coefficient in \eqref{action:normalized-flow}.
Conversely, a solution of that equation satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \frac d{ds}\mathbf g_+(v,v)
 &=(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)+2\mathbf g_+(\mathbf D_vv,v)\\
 &=(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)\bigl(1-\mathbf g_+(v,v)\bigr).
\end{align*}
Thus unit initial velocities remain unit. Metric compatibility
and the Killing equation for $\mathbf T$ give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac d{ds}\mathbf g(v,v)&=-(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)\mathbf g(v,v),\\
 \frac d{ds}\mathbf g(\mathbf T,v)
 &=-\tfrac12(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)\mathbf g(\mathbf T,v).
 \end{aligned}
\]
The normalized geodesic vector field preserves the closed set
$\mathbf g(v,v)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,v)=0$ in the unit tangent bundle. The quotient unit
bundle over $K$ is compact. For a fixed auxiliary distance on
this bundle, boundedness of the vector field gives
\[
 \operatorname{dist}\bigl([\gamma(s),v(s)],
                         [\gamma(s'),v(s')]\bigr)
       \le C_K|s-s'|.
\]
Thus $[\gamma(s),v(s)]$ has a limit at every finite endpoint. In the
stationary product coordinates supplied by
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}, its lift satisfies
\[
 |\vartheta(\gamma(s))-\vartheta(\gamma(s'))|
       \le C_K|s-s'|,
\]
because $d\vartheta$ is stationary and bounded on the compact
unit bundle. The position and velocity therefore have a
spacetime limit, and local existence for
\eqref{action:normalized-flow} continues the lift.

If the asserted length bound failed, we could take confined segments
with lengths tending to infinity. We center them at their
midpoints and orient them to the future, so that a limit of
their unit initial data will give a geodesic defined in both
directions.
After passing to a subsequence, their unit initial data satisfy
$\xi_j=[p_j,v_j]\to\xi$ in the compact quotient unit bundle
over $K$.
Cut off the induced normalized field on the quotient outside a
relatively compact neighborhood of this bundle.
Denote its complete cut-off flow by $\Phi_s$ and use the same
auxiliary Riemannian distance on the quotient bundle.
In a finite coordinate cover of this compact set, the smooth
vector field has bounded first derivatives. The variational
equation for its flow and Gronwall's inequality give, for
every fixed $R$,
\[
 \sup_{|s|\le R}\operatorname{dist}(\Phi_s\xi_j,\Phi_s\xi)
       \le C_R\operatorname{dist}(\xi_j,\xi)\longrightarrow0.
\]
For large $j$ the centered original segment contains $[-R,R]$,
so its limit stays in the closed quotient unit bundle over $K$ there.
As $R$ is arbitrary, the limiting trajectory solves the
original equation on $\mathbb R$ without entering the cutoff region.
On the compact set of stationary classes of future null vectors
with $\mathbf g_+(v,v)=1$ over $K$, the stationary covector
$d\vartheta$ satisfies
\[
 0<c_K\le d\vartheta(v)\le C_K.
\]
The spacetime lift exists on every finite interval and obeys
$\vartheta(\gamma(s))\to\pm\infty$. It is a maximal
zero-energy null geodesic confined over $K$, contrary to NT.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{action:localized-nontrapping}
We can locate more precisely the part of the exterior on which
NT imposes a restriction. The local horizon geometry already
excludes compactly trapped null geodesics near the horizon,
while a zero-energy null geodesic cannot enter a region where
$\mathbf T$ is timelike. We show below that it therefore suffices to
test NT on a single compact part of the closed ergoregion,
separated from the horizon. This gives exactly the length bound
used in Theorem~\ref{discrete:continuation}, where an obstruction
to extending the Killing field produces a zero-energy null
geodesic confined over that compact set.

Assume GR and SBS, with $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$. In the stationary
horizon coordinates of Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}, choose any sufficiently
small $\delta_+>0$. Let $C_+\subset Q$ be the image of
$0<r<\delta_+$ and put $H_0=\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}\setminus C_+$,
as in the definitions of Proposition~\ref{prep:residual}.
Every maximal unparametrized zero-energy null
geodesic whose stationary projection is contained in a compact
subset of $Q$ has its projection entirely in $H_0$.
Consequently NT is equivalent to the existence of $C<\infty$
such that every zero-energy null geodesic segment $\gamma:I\to\mathbf E$
satisfies
\[
 \pi(\gamma(I))\subset H_0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \int_I|\dot\gamma|_{\mathbf g_+}\,ds\le C.
\]
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We use the local horizon geometry to exclude a minimum of the
radial coordinate along a trapped geodesic. The construction of
$C_+$, compactness of $H_0$, and the calculation
\eqref{prep:strict-calculation} use GR and SBS. In particular,
throughout $\pi^{-1}(C_+)$,
\[
 \mathbf D^2r(X,X)<0
 \quad\bigl(X\ne0,\ \mathbf g(X,X)=0,\ Xr=0\bigr).
\]
The normalized-flow and finite-endpoint arguments in the preceding
proof use the stationary product and compactness. Hence a maximal
zero-energy null geodesic confined over a compact $K\subset Q$
can be parametrized by $\mathbf g_+$-arclength on all of $\mathbb R$.
Let $\gamma$ be such a parametrization and suppose that its
projection meets $C_+$. By the last assertion of
Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}, compact subsets of $Q$ stay away from
$r=0$. Therefore
\[
 0<r_{\ast}:=
 \inf\{r(\gamma(s)):\pi(\gamma(s))\in C_+\}<\delta_+.
\]
Choose times $s_j$ with $\pi(\gamma(s_j))\in C_+$ and
$r(\gamma(s_j))\to r_{\ast}$.
Using the stationary coordinate $\vartheta$ of
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient}, define
\[
 \gamma_j(s)=
 \Phi_{-\vartheta(\gamma(s_j))}^{\mathbf T}\bigl(\gamma(s_j+s)\bigr).
\]
The initial positions lie on $\vartheta=0$ over $K$, and their
velocities have $\mathbf g_+$-length one. After passing to a
subsequence, these initial data converge to $(p,v)$ with
$|v|_{\mathbf g_+}=1$. Since $r_{\ast}\in(0,\delta_+)$ and
$S_0$ is compact, the normal parametrization gives
$\pi(p)\in C_+$ and $r(p)=r_{\ast}$.
Smooth dependence for \eqref{action:normalized-flow} now gives a
limiting geodesic $\gamma_\infty$ with initial data $(p,v)$ on a
neighborhood of $s=0$.
For sufficiently small $|s|$, its projection and those of
$\gamma_j$ lie in $C_+$, so
\begin{align*}
 r(\gamma_\infty(s))&\ge r_{\ast},\\
 r(\gamma_\infty(0))&=r_{\ast},\\
 dr(v)&=0.
\end{align*}
The null and zero-energy constraints pass to the limit. At this
minimum the normalized geodesic equation gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0\le\left.\frac{d^2}{ds^2}r(\gamma_\infty(s))\right|_{s=0}
 &=\mathbf D^2r(v,v)
   -\tfrac12(\mathbf D_v\mathbf g_+)(v,v)\,dr(v)\\
 &=\mathbf D^2r(v,v)<0,
 \end{aligned}
\]
a contradiction. Thus $\pi(\gamma(\mathbb R))\cap C_+=\varnothing$.
Where $\mathbf T$ is timelike its orthogonal complement is positive
definite, so it contains no nonzero null vector. We conclude that
\[
 \pi(\gamma(\mathbb R))
 \subset\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}\setminus C_+=H_0.
\]
Under NT, Lemma~\ref{action:escape} applied to the compact set
$H_0$ gives the stated length bound. Conversely, if NT fails,
the preceding argument gives a normalized geodesic on $\mathbb R$
with projection in $H_0$. The bound would imply
\[
 2R=\int_{-R}^{R}|\dot\gamma(s)|_{\mathbf g_+}\,ds\le C
 \qquad(R>0),
\]
which is impossible.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{action:intrinsic-orbits}
Let $h$ be a smooth Lorentzian metric on a three-dimensional
manifold $Q$, let $g_+$ be a smooth Riemannian metric, and let
$K\subset Q$ be compact. Suppose every $h$-null geodesic segment
contained in $K$ has $g_+$-length at most $L_K$.
For every hypersurface-orthogonal timelike Killing field $\mathbf Z$
on an open subset of $Q$, put $\rho=\sqrt{-h(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}$.
On every periodic $\mathbf Z$-orbit contained in $K$,
\begin{equation}\label{action:intrinsic-bound}
 \left|\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right|_{g_+}
 +\left|\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right)\right|_{g_+}
 +|d\log\rho|_{g_+}\le C_K.
\end{equation}
Here $\nabla$ is the connection of $h$, and $g_+$ induces all
vector, covector, and tensor norms in the estimate. The constant depends
only on $K,h,g_+$ and $L_K$, independently of the field and
its domain.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Hypersurface orthogonality gives local coordinates in which
$\mathbf Z=\partial_\phi$ and $h=-\rho^2d\phi^2+q$, with $q$ Riemannian
and all coefficients independent of $\phi$. Thus the $h$-gradient
$\nabla\rho$ is the $q$-gradient on the transverse surface.
Let $x^A$, $A=1,2$, be coordinates on the transverse surface,
and let $\Gamma$ denote the Christoffel symbols of $h$ in
$(\phi,x^1,x^2)$. From $h_{\phi\phi}=-\rho^2$ and $h_{A\phi}=0$,
\begin{align*}
 \Gamma^\phi_{A\phi}
 &=\tfrac12(-\rho^{-2})(-2\rho\partial_A\rho)
   =\rho^{-1}\partial_A\rho,\\
 \Gamma^A_{\phi\phi}
 &=-\tfrac12q^{AB}\partial_B(-\rho^2)
   =\rho q^{AB}\partial_B\rho.
\end{align*}
Moreover $\Gamma^\phi_{AB}=\Gamma^A_{B\phi}=0$, since the coefficients
are independent of $\phi$. It follows that
\begin{align*}
 \nabla_\mathbf Z\mathbf Z&=\rho\nabla\rho,\\
 \nabla_\mathbf Z\nabla\rho
 &=\rho^{-1}q^{AB}(\partial_A\rho)(\partial_B\rho)\mathbf Z
   =\rho^{-1}|d\rho|_q^2\mathbf Z,\\
 \nabla_\mathbf Z(\nabla_\mathbf Z\mathbf Z)&=|d\rho|_q^2\mathbf Z.
\end{align*}
Both coefficients $\rho,|d\rho|_q$ are constant along an orbit.
We normalize the largest auxiliary speed on the orbit to one.
At a point attaining this maximum, the second derivative of the
speed will bound both the acceleration and the coefficient in
the orbit equation. Set
$m=\max_{\rm orbit}|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}$, start at a maximum, and
parametrize by $\gamma(t)=\Phi_{t/m}^\mathbf Z p$. Write
$V=\dot\gamma=\mathbf Z/m$ for its velocity and $A=\nabla_VV$ for its
acceleration, and set $\nu=|d\rho|_q/m$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{action:orbit-ode}
 \begin{aligned}
 \nabla_VV&=A,\\
 \nabla_VA&=\nu^2V,
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with $|V|_{g_+}\le1$ and $|V(0)|_{g_+}=1$.
At the maximum, differentiating the fixed auxiliary metric
first gives
\[
 \frac d{dt}g_+(V,V)=(\nabla_Vg_+)(V,V)+2g_+(A,V).
\]
We differentiate the metric and acceleration terms separately,
using $\nabla_VV=A$ and $\nabla_VA=\nu^2V$:
\begin{align*}
 \frac d{dt}\bigl[(\nabla_Vg_+)(V,V)\bigr]
 &=(\nabla^2g_+)(V,V,V,V)+(\nabla_Ag_+)(V,V)
       +2(\nabla_Vg_+)(A,V),\\
 \frac d{dt}\bigl[2g_+(A,V)\bigr]
 &=2(\nabla_Vg_+)(A,V)+2\nu^2g_+(V,V)+2g_+(A,A).
\end{align*}
At the maximum this yields
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&\ge\left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}g_+(V,V)\right|_{t=0}\\
 &=2|A(0)|_{g_+}^2+2\nu^2
   +(\nabla^2g_+)(V,V,V,V)
   +(\nabla_Ag_+)(V,V)+4(\nabla_Vg_+)(A,V).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $\nabla^2g_+$ is the second covariant derivative of the
auxiliary metric formed with the connection of $h$. The constants in this
proof depend only on $K,h,g_+$ and the assumed length bound $L_K$.
Boundedness of the metric derivatives on $K$ gives
\begin{align*}
2|A(0)|_{g_+}^2+2\nu^2
 &\le C+C|A(0)|_{g_+}\le |A(0)|_{g_+}^2+C,\\
\shortintertext{and therefore}
|A(0)|_{g_+}^2+\nu^2&\le C.
\end{align*}
Since $\nu=|d\rho|_q/m$, this also gives
\[
 |d\rho|_q\le Cm.
\]
Let $P_t:T_{\gamma(0)}Q\to T_{\gamma(t)}Q$ be $h$-parallel
transport. Its operator norm uses $g_+$ at the two endpoints. The
two equations in \eqref{action:orbit-ode} have the exact solution
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}P_t^{-1}V(t)\\P_t^{-1}A(t)\end{pmatrix}
 =\exp\left(t\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\\nu^2&0\end{pmatrix}\right)
   \begin{pmatrix}V(0)\\A(0)\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For $\nu>0$ the two entries are
\[
\begin{aligned}
P_t^{-1}V(t)&=\cosh(\nu t)V(0)+\frac{\sinh(\nu t)}\nu A(0),\\
P_t^{-1}A(t)&=\nu\sinh(\nu t)V(0)+\cosh(\nu t)A(0).
\end{aligned}
\]
At $\nu=0$ these expressions have continuous limits
$V(0)+tA(0)$ and $A(0)$, so the estimates are uniform
as $\nu\to0$.
For any nonzero parallel vector $Y$, differentiation gives
\[
 \left|\frac d{dt}\log|Y|_{g_+}\right|
 =\left|\frac{(\nabla_Vg_+)(Y,Y)}{2g_+(Y,Y)}\right|
 \le C|V|_{g_+}.
\]
Integration, with $|V|_{g_+}\le1$, gives
$\|P_t\|_{g_+}\le e^{C|t|}$. The initial bound and the matrix
formula therefore bound $V,A$ on every fixed time interval,
uniformly over all complete orbits.

We can now prove the bound for $m/\rho$. Suppose it fails and
take complete orbits over $K$ with $\rho_j/m_j\to0$, where the
subscript $j$ denotes the quantities just defined on the $j$th
orbit. Their normalized velocities approach the null cone,
while the preceding estimate bounds the accelerations and
coefficients needed to pass to the orbit equation. Pass to a subsequence of the bounded
initial data with $\nu_j\to\nu\ge0$. In a coordinate chart
\eqref{action:orbit-ode} is the fixed system
\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot\gamma^i&=V^i,\\
\dot V^i&=A^i-\Gamma^i_{k\ell}V^kV^\ell,\\
\dot A^i&=\nu^2V^i-\Gamma^i_{k\ell}V^kA^\ell,\\
\dot\nu&=0,
\end{aligned}
\]
where $i,k,\ell=1,2,3$ and the Christoffel symbols are those
of the fixed metric $h$.
For every fixed $R$, the preceding estimates put the solutions
on $[-R,R]$ in one compact subset of the bundle of
$(\gamma,V,A,\nu)$. Cut off the smooth system outside that set.
Continuous dependence on the convergent initial data gives
uniform convergence on $[-R,R]$. The limit stays over $K$ and
therefore solves the original system. Uniqueness identifies the
limits as $R$ increases, giving a solution on all of $\mathbb R$.
The identities
\[
\begin{aligned}
h(V_j,V_j)&=-\rho_j^2/m_j^2,\\
h(V_j,A_j)&=0,\\
h(A_j,A_j)&=\rho_j^2\nu_j^2/m_j^2
\end{aligned}
\]
pass to the limit. Since $|V(0)|_{g_+}=1$, the vector $V(0)$
is nonzero and null. Complete it to a null frame
$V(0),\underline V,E$ at $\gamma(0)$, with
$h(V(0),\underline V)=-1$ and $h(E,E)=1$.
For some $b\in\mathbb R$, the two limiting orthogonality relations give
\[
\begin{aligned}
 A(0)&=bV(0)+h(A(0),E)E,\\
 0&=h(A(0),A(0))=h(A(0),E)^2,
\end{aligned}
\]
so $A(0)=bV(0)$.
Reverse time if necessary
to have $b\ge0$. The matrix formula for $h$-parallel transport
$P_t:T_{\gamma(0)}Q\to T_{\gamma(t)}Q$ now gives
\[
 V(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 [\cosh(\nu t)+(b/\nu)\sinh(\nu t)]P_tV(0),&\nu>0,\\
 (1+bt)P_tV(0),&\nu=0.
 \end{cases}
\]
Both scalar factors are at least one for $t\ge0$, and
$\nabla_VV=A\in\mathbb R V$. We have therefore obtained a nonzero null curve on
$[0,\infty)$ which is a geodesic up to reparametrization. Its image stays in $K$, so the assumed length
bound gives
\[
 \ell(t)=\int_0^t|V(s)|_{g_+}\,ds\le L_K.
\]
On the other hand, integrating the logarithmic derivative
of the parallel vector $P_tV(0)$ gives
\[
 \log\frac{|P_tV(0)|_{g_+}}{|V(0)|_{g_+}}
 \ge -C\int_0^t|V(s)|_{g_+}\,ds=-C\ell(t).
\]
The displayed formula for $V$ therefore implies
\begin{align*}
|V(t)|_{g_+}&\ge|P_tV(0)|_{g_+}\ge e^{-C\ell(t)}\ge c_K,\\
\shortintertext{and hence}
\ell(t)&\ge c_Kt,
\end{align*}
a contradiction. Hence $m+|d\rho|_q\le C\rho$.

It remains to control the derivative of the normalized field.
For $X=X^\phi\partial_\phi+X^A\partial_{x^A}$, the Christoffel
symbols computed at the start of the proof give
\begin{align*}
 \nabla_X\mathbf Z
 &=X^A\Gamma^\phi_{A\phi}\partial_\phi
       +X^\phi\Gamma^A_{\phi\phi}\partial_{x^A}\\
 &=\rho^{-1}X(\rho)\mathbf Z+X^\phi\rho\nabla\rho\\
 &=(X\log\rho)\mathbf Z-h(X,\mathbf Z)\nabla\log\rho.
\end{align*}
Differentiating $\rho^{-1}\mathbf Z$ now cancels the first term:
\begin{align*}
 \nabla_X\left(\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right)
 &=-\rho^{-1}(X\log\rho)\mathbf Z+\rho^{-1}\nabla_X\mathbf Z\\
 &=-h\left(X,\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right)\nabla\log\rho.
\end{align*}
We have bounded $\mathbf Z/\rho$ in the auxiliary norm, with
$h(\mathbf Z/\rho,\mathbf Z/\rho)=-1$. To make the metric comparison
uniform in the field, observe that
\[
 \inf\left\{h(X,X):
 \begin{array}{l}
 p\in K,\ u,X\in T_pQ,\ h(u,u)=-1,\ |u|_{g_+}\le C,\\
 h(u,X)=0,\ |X|_{g_+}=1
 \end{array}\right\}>0.
\]
Indeed, the conditions define a compact set, and a nonzero
vector orthogonal to a timelike vector is spacelike. Since
$h(\nabla\log\rho,\mathbf Z/\rho)=\rho^{-1}\mathbf Z(\log\rho)=0$,
we can apply this lower bound to obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
 |\nabla\log\rho|_{g_+}^2
 &\le C h(\nabla\log\rho,\nabla\log\rho)
   =C|d\log\rho|_q^2\le C,\\
 |d\log\rho|_{g_+}
 &=|h(\nabla\log\rho,\cdot)|_{g_+}\le C.
\end{aligned}
\]
The derivative identity above yields
\[
 \left|\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right)\right|_{g_+}
 \le |h|_{g_+}\left|\frac{\mathbf Z}{\rho}\right|_{g_+}
                   |\nabla\log\rho|_{g_+}\le C,
\]
which proves \eqref{action:intrinsic-bound}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{action:relative}
For every compact $K\Subset\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}\subset Q$ there is
$C\ge1$ such that, along every complete $\mathbf Z$-orbit in $U$
whose stationary projection lies in $K$,
\begin{equation}\label{action:relative-est}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}}{\rho}
 +\left|\nabla(\mathbf Z/\rho)\right|_{g_+}
 +|d\log\rho|_{g_+}+|d\log\rho|_q&\le C,\\
C^{-1}\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)&\le W\le C\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z),\\
|\mathbf Z|_{\mathbf g_+}&\le C\rho.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here $W=\rho^2=-h(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)$ on $Q$, and $q$ is the Riemannian
metric in \eqref{action:reduction}. The constant depends only
on $K$, the stationary geometry, and $g_+,\mathbf g_+$, and is
independent of $U,\mathbf Z$. Thus $\mathbf Z/\rho$ on these projected orbits
lies in a compact subset of $\{v\in TQ:h(v,v)=-1\}$ over $K$
independent of $U,\mathbf Z$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{action:intrinsic-orbits} gives the quotient estimates,
including the derivative bound. We transfer them to spacetime.
The closed orbit has an extremum of $\vartheta$, where
$\mathbf Z\in\ker d\vartheta$, a spacelike plane whose projection
to $TQ$ is an isomorphism. After translating this point to
the fixed stationary section,
uniform metric comparison gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
c|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}^2&\le\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)\le C|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}^2,\\
\rho^2&=-h(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)\le C|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}^2,\\
|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}^2&\le C\rho^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
The last inequality is the bound just proved, and the middle
one uses only bounded coefficients of the fixed metric $h$.
Thus
\[
\begin{aligned}
C^{-1}\rho^2&\le\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)\le C\rho^2,\\
|\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)|&\le C|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}\le C\rho.
\end{aligned}
\]
These contractions and $\rho$ are constant along the orbit.
The $\mathbf T$-orthogonal decomposition of $\mathbf Z$ then yields
$|\mathbf Z|_{\mathbf g_+}\le C(|\mathbf Z|_{g_+}+|\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)|)\le C\rho$ everywhere.
Together with the intrinsic estimate, this proves
\eqref{action:relative-est}. The equalities
$h(\mathbf Z/\rho,\mathbf Z/\rho)=-1$ and $|\mathbf Z/\rho|_{g_+}\le C$ give the
last assertion.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{discrete:null-convexity}
Fix $\epsilon_0>0$. We impose the following condition on a
closed set $H\subset\mathcal D_0$: for every null geodesic segment
$\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbf E$ satisfying
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\dot\gamma)&=0\\
