 \int_a^b|\dot\gamma|_{\mathbf g_+}\,ds&<\epsilon_0
 \end{aligned}
\]
we have
\[
 \pi(\gamma(a)),\pi(\gamma(b))\in H
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \pi(\gamma([a,b]))\subset H.
\]
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{discrete:null-convex-cuts}
The set $H_0$ has the property in
Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity} for some $\epsilon_0>0$.
For this fixed $\epsilon_0$, the property is preserved by intersections
and by the following operation. Let $H$ have this property,
let $V\Subset Q$ be open, and let $C\subset V$ be relatively closed. Suppose every zero-energy null
segment contained over $V$, of $\mathbf g_+$-length less than $\epsilon_0$
and with projected endpoints in $C$, projects into $C$. If
\[
 \overline{H\cap(V\setminus C)}\Subset V,
\]
then $H'=H\setminus(V\setminus C)$ has the same property.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We first verify the property for the initial set. Near its relative
boundary we use the Gaussian coordinate $r$ of
Proposition~\ref{prep:residual}, whose Hessian satisfies \eqref{local:convex}
on a neighborhood of the compact annulus $r=\delta_+$ and its
nearby levels. If a zero-energy null segment
with both projected endpoints in $H_0$ enters $\{r<\delta_+\}$ and
remains in this neighborhood, then at an interior minimum below
$\delta_+$ we have
\begin{equation}\label{discrete:null-minimum}
 \begin{aligned}
 (r\circ\pi\circ\gamma)'&=0,\\
 0\le(r\circ\pi\circ\gamma)''
   &=\mathbf D^2(r\circ\pi)(\dot\gamma,\dot\gamma)<0.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This contradiction uses an affine parameter.
The sign of the second derivative at a critical point is unchanged
by reparametrization.
To make the length restriction uniform, let $A$ be the compact
initial annulus and choose a relatively compact neighborhood $V_0$ of
$A$ on which $r$ is defined and satisfies \eqref{local:convex}.
Stationarity and compactness give $L<\infty$ with
\[
 |d\pi(X)|_{g_+}\le L|X|_{\mathbf g_+}
 \quad\text{over }\overline{V_0}.
\]
Choose $\epsilon_0>0$ so that
$L\epsilon_0<\operatorname{dist}_{g_+}(A,Q\setminus V_0)$.
If the projected segment meets $A$, its first exit from $V_0$ would give
\[
 \operatorname{dist}_{g_+}(A,Q\setminus V_0)
 \le\int|d\pi(\dot\gamma)|_{g_+}\,ds
 <L\epsilon_0,
\]
a contradiction. The preceding minimum argument therefore applies to
every segment of the required length that crosses the initial annulus.
A nonzero null vector orthogonal to $\mathbf T$ cannot occur where $\mathbf T$ is
timelike. Thus such a segment stays in $\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\ge0\}$,
and crossing the annulus is the only way it could leave $H_0$.

We next check that the removal preserves the condition of
Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity}. Let $\gamma$
have projected endpoints in $H'$, so that its entire projection lies
in $H$. If its projection enters $V\setminus C$, let $(a_0,b_0)$
be a component of $(\pi\circ\gamma)^{-1}(V\setminus C)$.
Compact containment of the removed part excludes an endpoint on
$\partial V$, and consequently
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \pi(\gamma(a_0)),\pi(\gamma(b_0))&\in C\\
 \pi(\gamma([a_0,b_0]))&\subset V.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The subsegment has length less than $\epsilon_0$. Its assumed convexity
contradicts $\pi(\gamma((a_0,b_0)))\subset V\setminus C$.
An intersection has the property by applying its defining implication
to each of the intersected sets.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{discrete:admissible}
A pair $(H,\mathbf Z)$ is required to satisfy the following conditions.
The set $H\subset H_0$ is compact, $U_H$ in
\eqref{discrete:exact-domain} is connected, and $\mathbf Z$ is a Killing
field on $U_H$ whose flow is complete with effective period $2\pi$.
It agrees on $\pi^{-1}(C_+)$ with the field of
Proposition~\ref{prep:residual} and satisfies
\[
 [\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]=0.
\]
We require that $H$ satisfy
Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity}, with the fixed
$\epsilon_0$ of Lemma~\ref{discrete:null-convex-cuts}, and that
\[
 H_0\cap E_0\subset H,
\]
where $E_0=\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0\}\subset Q$ is the projected ergosurface.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{discrete:admissible-update}
Let $(H,\mathbf Z)$ satisfy Definition~\ref{discrete:admissible} and let
$V\Subset\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal D_0)\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$
be open. Suppose a Killing field $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ on $\pi^{-1}(V)$
commutes with $\mathbf T$, has complete period-$2\pi$ flow there, and
\[
 \widetilde\mathbf Z=\mathbf Z\quad\text{on }\pi^{-1}(V)\cap U_H.
\]
Let $f\in C^\infty(V)$ satisfy $\widetilde\mathbf Z f=0$ on $V$,
where the field is projected to $Q$. Suppose its stationary
pullback satisfies $\mathbf g^{-1}(\pi^{\ast}df,\pi^{\ast}df)>0$ and
\[
 \mathbf D^2(f\circ\pi)(X,X)<0
 \quad\bigl(X\ne0,\ X(f\circ\pi)=\mathbf g(X,X)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,X)=0\bigr)
\]
for $X\in T\pi^{-1}(V)$.
For $\varepsilon\in\mathbb R$, assume
\[
 \overline{H\cap V\cap\{f<\varepsilon\}}\Subset V
\]
and that every component of $V\cap\{f<\varepsilon\}$ meets
$\pi(U_H)$. Then
\[
 H'=H\setminus\bigl(V\cap\{f<\varepsilon\}\bigr)
\]
and the glued field on $U_{H'}$ satisfy Definition~\ref{discrete:admissible}.
Under the same assumptions on $V,\widetilde\mathbf Z$, the conclusion
also holds for $H'=H\setminus V$ when $H\cap\partial V=\varnothing$
and every component of $V$ meets $\pi(U_H)$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $A=V\cap\{f<\varepsilon\}$. It is open, and
$H'=H\setminus A$ is compact. Since $\overline V$ lies in
$\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$, we retain $H_0\cap E_0\subset H'$.
For a zero-energy null segment contained over $V$, with projected
endpoints in $\{f\ge\varepsilon\}$, an interior minimum below
$\varepsilon$ contradicts \eqref{discrete:null-minimum}, applied with
$r$ replaced by $f$. Thus every such segment projects into
$\{f\ge\varepsilon\}$. Lemma~\ref{discrete:null-convex-cuts}
and the compact-containment hypothesis give
the condition of Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity} for $H'$.

Write $\Phi_t$ and $\widetilde\Phi_t$ for the complete flows
on $U_H$ and $\pi^{-1}(V)$. By Lemma~\ref{local:complete-flows},
\begin{align*}
 \widetilde\Phi_t(U_H\cap\pi^{-1}(V))
     &=U_H\cap\pi^{-1}(V),\\
 \widetilde\Phi_t(\pi^{-1}(H\cap V))
     &=\pi^{-1}(H\cap V).
\end{align*}
The second equality follows by taking the complement inside
$\pi^{-1}(V)$. Since
\[
 \frac d{dt}(f\circ\pi\circ\widetilde\Phi_t)
       =(\widetilde\mathbf Z f)\circ\pi\circ\widetilde\Phi_t=0,
\]
the set $\pi^{-1}(A)$ is also invariant. Consequently the fields
glue on the enlarged domain
\[
 U_{H'}=U_H\cup\pi^{-1}(A).
\]
Each component of $A$ meets $\pi(U_H)$, so this domain is
connected. On $U_H$ its flow is $\Phi_t$, and on $\pi^{-1}(A)$
it is $\widetilde\Phi_t$. The overlap identity therefore gives
a complete flow satisfying
\[
 \Phi_{2\pi}^{\mathbf Z}=\operatorname{Id}_{U_{H'}}.
\]
A smaller global period would restrict to a smaller period on
$\pi^{-1}(C_+)$, contrary to the initial normalization.
The glued field is Killing, commutes with $\mathbf T$, and agrees
with the initial field, proving all the required conditions.

For $H'=H\setminus V$, compactness and the inclusion
$H_0\cap E_0\subset H'$ follow in the same way. A null segment with projected
endpoints in $H'$ already projects into $H$. If its projection
entered $V$, it would meet $H\cap\partial V$, contrary to the
hypothesis. Its projection therefore stays in $H'$. Now take $A=V$ in the preceding flow
and domain argument.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{discrete:maximal-action}
The pairs of Definition~\ref{discrete:admissible} have a maximal
element under $\preceq$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The initial pair exists by Proposition~\ref{prep:residual} and
Lemma~\ref{discrete:null-convex-cuts}. For a nonempty chain
$\mathcal C$, put
\[
 H_{\mathcal C}=\bigcap_{(H',\mathbf Z')\in\mathcal C}H'.
\]
This compact set contains $H_0\cap E_0$ and retains the condition
of Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity}.
Formula~\eqref{discrete:exact-domain} gives
\[
 U_{H_{\mathcal C}}=\bigcup_{(H',\mathbf Z')\in\mathcal C}U_{H'}.
\]
The union is connected, since its members are nested and contain
$\pi^{-1}(C_+)$. The order identifies the fields on their overlaps,
so they define a smooth commuting Killing field $\mathbf Z_{\mathcal C}$
on this union. For $p\in U_{H'}$, its entire old orbit stays in
$U_{H'}$, and uniqueness of integral curves gives
\[
 \Phi_t^{\mathbf Z_{\mathcal C}}(p)=\Phi_t^{\mathbf Z'}(p)
 \qquad(t\in\mathbb R).
\]
Consequently the glued flow is complete and has period $2\pi$.
A smaller effective period would restrict to a smaller period on
$\pi^{-1}(C_+)$, contrary to the initial normalization. Thus
$(H_{\mathcal C},\mathbf Z_{\mathcal C})$ is an upper bound for the chain.
Zorn's lemma gives a maximal pair.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{discrete:stalled-boundary}
For the maximal pair of Lemma~\ref{discrete:maximal-action}, every
point of
\[
 \partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}
\]
has a neighborhood in which this boundary is a smooth timelike
hypersurface of $(Q,h)$. For either unit spacelike normal $n$, its
second fundamental form $B(X,Y)=-h(\nabla_Xn,Y)$ satisfies
\[
 B(X,X)=0
 \qquad(X\in T\partial H,\ h(X,X)=0).
\]
Every smooth null curve with nowhere zero tangent in this hypersurface is an $h$-geodesic
up to reparametrization. Locally the remaining set is either one side
of the hypersurface or the hypersurface itself.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:reach}
For a closed set $H\subset Q$, its reach in $g_+$ is the
supremum of $R\ge0$ for which every $x$ with
$d_{g_+}(x,H)<R$ has a unique nearest point in $H$.
Positive reach near $q\in\partial H$ means that this property
holds for all such $x$ in a neighborhood of $q$, for some $R>0$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:proximal-conormal}
Let $H\subset Q$ be closed and $q\in\partial H$.
We use the proximal normal cone of
Bernicot and Venel~\cite[Definition~3.6]{BV15} and denote its
$g_+$-metric dual by $N_H^P(q)$. Thus $N_H^P(q)$ consists of the covectors $\alpha\in T_q^{\ast}Q$ for which there is $s_0>0$ such that
\[
 d_{g_+}\bigl(\exp_q^{g_+}(s\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}}),H\bigr)
       =s|\alpha|_+\qquad(0<s<s_0).
\]
Here $\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}}$ is the metric dual of $\alpha$ in $g_+$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{closure:null-reach}
Let $K\subset O\Subset\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$, where $K$ is compact
and $O\subset Q$ is open, and let $H\subset Q$ be closed.
For some $r>0$, assume that every $h$-null geodesic segment
contained in $O$, of $g_+$-length less than $r$, with endpoints
in $H$ lies in $H$.
Suppose an open set $U\subset\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}\setminus H$
contains $O\setminus H$ and carries a complete smooth field $V$
such that, for some $C<\infty$, on $O\setminus H$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 h(V,V)&=-1,\\
 |V|_{g_+}+|\nabla V|_{g_+}&\le C.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Then $H$ has a uniform positive reach radius near $K$, depending
only on $K,O,r,C$ and the fixed metrics.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Suppose that an exterior point has two nearest points in $H$. We first
extend the exterior flow to these points by taking limits from the
ball, then move them in opposite timelike directions until the
connecting geodesic becomes null. The first-order displacements
cancel at its midpoint, while their difference changes the causal
character of the connecting geodesic. The remaining error will
be smaller than the difference between the ball radius and the
distance of its chord midpoint from the center.

Choose a fixed smooth $h$-unit timelike field $V_0$ locally and put
$\widehat g_+=h+2h(V_0,\,\cdot\,)\otimes h(V_0,\,\cdot\,)$.
It suffices to prove
a reach bound in this metric, by the change-of-metric calculation following
Definition~\ref{def:proximal-conormal}.
The field $V_0$ and all coordinate constants below depend only on
the fixed geometry.
Suppose an exterior point $c$ has distinct nearest points $p,q$ at
the same sufficiently small distance $R$. In $\widehat g_+$-normal coordinates
centered at $c$, choose the initial basis with first vector $V_0(c)$.
Then $\widehat g_+(c)=I$, $h(c)=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,1)$, and the
coordinate ball $B_R(c)$ is the geodesic ball disjoint from $H$.
In this proof $|\cdot|$ and $\cdot$ denote the Euclidean coordinate
norm and inner product, and $d=|q-p|$.
The open coordinate chord from $p$ to $q$ lies in $B_R(c)$,
where there is one smooth exterior field. The bound on $\nabla V$
and the fixed connection coefficients give $|\partial V|\le C$ there.
The boundary values are its limits from this ball; hence
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 V(q)-V(p)&=\int_0^1\partial V(p+s(q-p))(q-p)\,ds,\\
 |V(q)-V(p)|&\le Cd.
 \end{aligned}
\]
All values $V(p),V(q)$ in this proof denote limits from the
same ball $B_R(c)$. Write $\Phi_t$ for the complete flow on $U$.
Choose $K\subset O_1\Subset O$ and then $t_0>0$ with
\[
 Ct_0<\tfrac12\operatorname{dist}_{g_+}(\overline{O_1},Q\setminus O).
\]
The speed bound keeps every trajectory starting in $O_1\setminus H$
inside $O$ for $|t|\le t_0$. Restrict all the small balls to $O_1$.
In coordinates, the variational equation and Gronwall's inequality
give
\begin{align*}
 \frac d{dt}d\Phi_t&=(\partial V)_{\Phi_t}d\Phi_t,\\
 |d\Phi_t|&\le C e^{C|t|}.
\end{align*}
For $x,y$ in the ball, apply this bound along their coordinate
chord, whose image remains in the exterior:
\begin{align*}
 d_{g_+}(\Phi_t(x),\Phi_t(y))
 &\le\int_0^1\bigl|(d\Phi_t)_{x+s(y-x)}(y-x)\bigr|_{g_+}\,ds\\
 &\le C e^{C|t|}|x-y|.
\end{align*}
Thus $\Phi_t$ has a continuous limit from the ball at $p$ and $q$,
uniformly for these times. Denote the resulting curves by $p(t)$
and $q(t)$. They lie in $\partial H$. Indeed they are limits of
exterior points, and if, for example, $p(t)$ were exterior, the
inverse flow would give
\[
 p=\lim_{x\to p,\ x\in B_R(c)}\Phi_{-t}(\Phi_t(x))
   =\Phi_{-t}(p(t))\notin H.
\]
The coordinate acceleration of every exterior trajectory satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\Phi_t^i(x)
   &=\partial_jV^i(\Phi_t(x))V^j(\Phi_t(x)),\\
 \left|\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\Phi_t(x)\right|&\le C.
\end{align*}
Taking limits in its Taylor formula proves that $p'(0)=V(p)$
and $q'(0)=V(q)$, with uniform quadratic remainders.
The distance to $c$ has a minimum along these two-sided boundary
curves. Gauss' lemma therefore gives
\[
 V(p)\cdot(c-p)=V(q)\cdot(c-q)=0,
\]
so
\[
 |(q-p)\cdot V(p)|
 =|(c-q)\cdot(V(q)-V(p))|\le CRd.
\]
Write $V(p)=(v^0,v')$ in these coordinates. The hypotheses and
$h(p)-h(c)=O(R)$ imply
$(v^0)^2-|v'|^2\ge1/2$ and $|V(p)|\le C$ for small $R$.
If $w\cdot V(p)=0$, then $w^0=-w'\cdot v'/v^0$ and
\[
 h(c)(w,w)\ge\frac{|w'|^2}{2(v^0)^2}\ge c_0|w|^2.
\]
Apply this inequality to
\[
 w=q-p-\frac{(q-p)\cdot V(p)}{|V(p)|^2}V(p).
\]
The bound $|(q-p)\cdot V(p)|\le CRd$ gives
\begin{align*}
 |w-(q-p)|&\le CRd,\\
 h(c)(q-p,q-p)&\ge c_0|w|^2-CRd^2
                       \ge\tfrac12c_0d^2
\end{align*}
after reducing $R$.
The boundary curves just constructed satisfy
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 p(-t)&=p-tV(p)+O(t^2)\\
 q(t)&=q+tV(q)+O(t^2).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Work inside one convex-normal neighborhood for $h$. For
$0\le t\le Md$, let $\gamma_t:[0,1]\to Q$ be the geodesic from
$p(-t)$ to $q(t)$, with affine parameter on $[0,1]$.
To estimate the connecting geodesics, integrate
their coordinate equation twice with the two endpoint values fixed:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &\gamma_t^i(s)-(1-s)p(-t)^i-sq(t)^i\\
 &\quad=\int_0^1\min\{s,u\}\bigl(1-\max\{s,u\}\bigr)
       \Gamma^i_{kl}(\gamma_t(u))\dot\gamma_t^k(u)
                                      \dot\gamma_t^l(u)\,du.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $\Gamma^i_{kl}$ are the Christoffel symbols of $h$ in these
coordinates. The endpoint distance is $O_M(d)$; smoothness of the exponential
map and its inverse gives $|\dot\gamma_t|\le C_Md$.
The integral and its first $s$-derivative are consequently
$O_M(d^2)$. Together with the orbit expansions, this yields
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \dot\gamma_t&=q-p+t\bigl(V(p)+V(q)\bigr)+O_M(d^2),\\
 h(\dot\gamma_t,\dot\gamma_t)
 &=h(c)\bigl(q-p+t(V(p)+V(q)),q-p+t(V(p)+V(q))\bigr)
       +O_M(Rd^2).
 \end{aligned}
\]
For the second equality, $h-h(c)=O_M(R)$, and $d\le2R$
absorbs the velocity error $O_M(d^3)$.
At $t=0$ the constant squared speed is at least $c_0d^2/4$.
Since $V(p)+V(q)=2V(p)+O(d)$, we have
$h(c)(V(p)+V(q),V(p)+V(q))\le-2$ for small $R$. Hence
\[
 h(\dot\gamma_{Md},\dot\gamma_{Md})
 \le \bigl(C+CM-2M^2+C_MR\bigr)d^2<0
\]
if $M$ is fixed sufficiently large and then $R$ sufficiently small.
Choose $R$ also so that $2MR<t_0$, hence $Md<t_0$.
To exclude a constant connecting geodesic, let $P$ be the
Euclidean orthogonal projection onto $V(p)^\perp$. The vector
$w$ above equals $P(q-p)$, so, uniformly for $0\le t\le Md$,
\begin{align*}
 P(q(t)-p(-t))
   &=w+tP(V(q)-V(p))+O(t^2)=w+O_M(d^2),\\
 |P(q(t)-p(-t))|
   &\ge (1-CR)d-C_Md^2\ge d/2>0
\end{align*}
after reducing $R$, since $d\le2R$.
Continuity therefore gives a nonconstant null connecting
geodesic for some $0<t_{\ast}<Md$.
Its endpoints belong to $H$, it stays in the prescribed larger
neighborhood, and its auxiliary length is at most $C_Md<r$.

We now compare the midpoint of this null geodesic with the original
chord midpoint. Write $z$ for its affine midpoint and $m=(p+q)/2$.
Using the integral formula at $s=1/2$ and the two orbit expansions,
we obtain
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 z-m&=\tfrac12t_{\ast}(V(q)-V(p))+O(t_{\ast}^2+d^2),\\
 |z-m|&\le C(t_{\ast}d+t_{\ast}^2+d^2)\le C_Md^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
But
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |m-c|^2&=R^2-d^2/4,\\
 |z-c|^2
 &\le |m-c|^2+2|m-c||z-m|+|z-m|^2\\
 &\le R^2-\tfrac14d^2+2RC_Md^2+C_M^2d^4\\
 &\le R^2-\bigl(\tfrac14-2C_MR-4C_M^2R^2\bigr)d^2
 \le R^2-\tfrac18d^2<R^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here we used $d\le2R$ and reduced $R$ so that
$2C_MR+4C_M^2R^2\le1/8$, after fixing $M$.
No lower bound for $d/R$ is needed: both the chord deficit and
the error are compared at order $d^2$.
The midpoint lies in $B_R(c)$, contrary to the required inclusion
of the null segment in $H$. All sufficiently near exterior points
therefore have a unique nearest point, with a uniform radius.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{normal:parallel-boundary}
Let $H\subset Q$ be closed with positive reach near
$q\in\partial H$. Let $\mathcal W\subset Q$ be an open neighborhood
of $q$, put $U=\mathcal W\setminus H$, and let $\mathcal D$ be a smooth
connection on a vector bundle over $\mathcal W$.
There exist a neighborhood $V\Subset \mathcal W$ of $q$ and a compact set
$K\Subset U$ such that every smooth section $\mathcal J$ on $U$ with
$\mathcal D \mathcal J=0$ satisfies, in a fixed local bundle frame,
\[
 \|\mathcal J\|_{C^m(U\cap V)}\le C_m\sup_K|\mathcal J|\qquad(m\ge0),
\]
where the norms use the chosen coordinates, bundle frame and a fixed
positive bundle metric, and $C_m$ is independent of $\mathcal J$.
If $N_H^P(q)$ is not a line, the limit $\mathcal J(q)=\lim_{U\ni x\to q}\mathcal J(x)$
exists. These limits are continuous at boundary points with
this property. If a smooth parallel section $\widetilde{\mathcal J}$
near $q$ satisfies $\widetilde{\mathcal J}(q)=\mathcal J(q)$, then
$\widetilde{\mathcal J}=\mathcal J$ on $U\cap V$ after shrinking $V$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We first use the normal segments to transport the section from
points at a fixed distance outside $H$. Fix a smooth positive bundle metric for
$|\mathcal J|$ and a local bundle frame in a coordinate neighborhood with compact closure
in $\mathcal W$, where the nearest-point property holds. Take $R>0$
below the local reach and injectivity radii. After reducing the
boundary neighborhood, all its normal segments of length $R$
stay in this coordinate neighborhood. For $p\in\partial H$
and a unit $\alpha\in N_H^P(p)$, put
\[
 x_R=\exp_p^{g_+}(R\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}}).
\]
As $p$ ranges over a smaller closed neighborhood, the unit normal
directions form a closed set, so the corresponding endpoints $x_R$
form a compact set $K$. The preceding extension of normal segments
gives $d_{g_+}(x_R,H)=R$, so $K$ is compactly contained in $U$.
For $x=\exp_p^{g_+}(s\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}})$, $0<s<R$,
$\mathcal D$-parallel transport $P$ along this segment gives
\begin{equation}\label{normal:radial-transport}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal J(x)&=P_{x_R\to x}\mathcal J(x_R),\\
|\mathcal J(x)|&\le e^{CR}\sup_K|\mathcal J|,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the bound follows from \eqref{local:parallel-transport-bound}.
Every sufficiently near exterior point has this form.
Successive coordinate derivatives of
$\partial_i\mathcal J=-\Gamma_i^{\mathcal D}\mathcal J$ give the asserted
$C^m$ bounds from this zeroth-order bound. Transport along each
normal segment extends smoothly to its boundary endpoint and gives,
in the fixed bundle frame,
\[
 \bigl|\mathcal J(\exp_p^{g_+}(s\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}}))
            -P_{x_R\to p}\mathcal J(x_R)\bigr|
 \le Cs\sup_K|\mathcal J|.
\]

To prove that the radial limits give one boundary value, fix $q$
where $N_H^P(q)$ is not a line. Any two unit directions in this convex cone can
be joined within its unit section by spherical arcs of total
length at most $2\pi$. If the directions are opposite, use
any third direction not on their line. At distance $s>0$,
the normal exponential map sends these arcs to a path in $U$
of length at most $Cs$. If $\eta_s$ is such a path, the coordinate
parallel equation and the bound already proved give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal J(\eta_s(1))-\mathcal J(\eta_s(0))
 &=-\int_0^1\Gamma_i^\mathcal D(\eta_s(t))\dot\eta_s^i(t)
                                      \mathcal J(\eta_s(t))\,dt,\\
 |\mathcal J(\eta_s(1))-\mathcal J(\eta_s(0))|
 &\le C\operatorname{Length}_{g_+}(\eta_s)\sup_K|\mathcal J|.
 \end{aligned}
\]
In particular,
\[
 \left|\mathcal J(\exp_q^{g_+}(s\alpha_1^{\sharp_{g_+}}))
       -\mathcal J(\exp_q^{g_+}(s\alpha_2^{\sharp_{g_+}}))\right|
       \le Cs\sup_K|\mathcal J|.
\]
The limit along each normal segment exists as $s\downarrow0$.
By this inequality it is independent of the direction, and we
denote it by $\mathcal J(q)$.

If $x_j\in U$ tends to $q$, its nearest points $p_j$ tend
to $q$. Pass to a subsequence of its unit normal covectors
converging to $\alpha\in N_H^P(q)$, using the chosen coordinates
to identify nearby cotangent spaces. The extended endpoints
satisfy
\begin{align*}
x_{R,j}&\longrightarrow\exp_q^{g_+}(R\alpha^{\sharp_{g_+}}),\\
\mathcal J(x_j)&=P_{x_{R,j}\to x_j}\mathcal J(x_{R,j})\longrightarrow \mathcal J(q).
\end{align*}
This proves convergence as $x\in U$ tends to $q$. At a sequence
of boundary points tending to $q$, use their radial expressions
$P_{x_R\to p}\mathcal J(x_R)$ and pass to a convergent subsequence
of unit normal covectors. The same limit proves continuity of the boundary
values wherever they are defined.

It remains to compare a smooth extension with the section on the
entire nearby exterior. The equality
$\widetilde{\mathcal J}(q)=\mathcal J(q)$ and uniqueness of parallel
transport first give $\widetilde{\mathcal J}=\mathcal J$ on each short normal segment
from $q$. The endpoints at a fixed length $R$ form a compact
subset of $U$. At each endpoint, transport within a coordinate
ball extends the equality to that ball. If equality failed at $x_j\in U$ tending to $q$,
extend its normal segment to length $R$. A subsequence of
the extended endpoints converges to the preceding compact
set, where equality holds on an open neighborhood. Transport along such a segment gives
\[
 |\mathcal J-\widetilde{\mathcal J}|(x_j)
 \le e^{CR}|\mathcal J-\widetilde{\mathcal J}|(x_{R,j})=0,
\]
which is a contradiction. All these segments lie
in the domain of $\widetilde{\mathcal J}$ after initially reducing
$R$ and the neighborhood.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{closure:normal-limits}
For the maximal pair $(H,\mathbf Z)$ fixed above,
put $U=U_{H}$. The set $H$ has locally
uniform positive reach in the ergoregion.
For every compact set
\[
 K\subset\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\},
\]
there are a
neighborhood $O\subset Q$ and constants $C_m,c>0$ such that,
on the section $\vartheta=0$,
\[
 \|(\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\mathbf Z)\|_{C^m(\{\vartheta=0\}\cap U\cap\pi^{-1}(O))}
       \le C_m\qquad(m\ge0),
\]
where the $C^m$ norm uses coordinate derivatives on the stationary
section and the positive tensor norm induced by $\mathbf g_+$.
Also $c\le\rho\le c^{-1}$ there, where
$\rho=\sqrt{W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]}=\sqrt{-h(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}$.
For every $p\in K$ and every $\alpha\in N_H^P(p)$, we have
\[
 h^{-1}(\alpha,\alpha)\ge c|\alpha|_{g_+}^2.
\]
For $p\in K$, if $N^P_{H}(p)$ is not a line, the pair
$(\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\mathbf Z)$ has one limit as $x\in\pi(U)$ tends to $p$,
with values evaluated on $\vartheta=0$. The projected limit of $\mathbf Z$ is timelike for
$h$ and annihilated by every $\alpha\in N^P_{H}(p)$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Having obtained positive reach for $H$, we apply the
boundedness part of Lemma~\ref{normal:parallel-boundary} to the
pullback of $\mathcal D$ to $\vartheta=0$. Recall from
\eqref{local:killing-connection} that its parallel section is
$\mathcal J=(\mathbf Z^\flat,\mathbf D(\mathbf Z^\flat))$, with second component
$(\mathbf D(\mathbf Z^\flat))_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbf D_\alpha\mathbf Z_\beta$.
Let $K_R\Subset\pi(U)$ be the compact set of exterior normal
endpoints at the fixed distance $R$ supplied by that lemma. Equation~\eqref{normal:radial-transport} bounds the section from
its values on $K_R$; the coordinate parallel equation then gives
the asserted $C^m$ bounds.
For the lower bound, Proposition~\ref{action:relative} gives
$|d\log\rho|_{g_+}\le C$ along the same exterior normal segments.
If $x_R\in K_R$ is the endpoint corresponding to $x$, then
\[
 |\log\rho(x)-\log\rho(x_R)|
 \le\int_x^{x_R}|d\log\rho|_{g_+}\,ds\le CR.
\]
Since $K_R$ is compactly contained in the old domain and $\rho>0$
there, we obtain
\[
 e^{-CR}\min_{K_R}\rho\le\rho(x)\le e^{CR}\max_{K_R}\rho.
\]
For the normal covectors we use the exterior flow of
$V=\mathbf Z/\rho$, where $h(V,V)=-1$, as in the reach proof.
Fix a sufficiently small $g_+$-ball disjoint from $H$
and tangent to it at $p$. Limits of the normalized
flow from this ball give a two-sided boundary curve $p(t)$ with
\begin{align*}
 p(t)&=p+tV(p)+O(t^2),\\
 h(V(p),V(p))&=-1,\\
 |V(p)|_{g_+}&\le C.
\end{align*}
Here $V(p)$ is the limit from this ball. For every unit
$\alpha\in N^P_{H}(p)$, the quadratic inequality
\eqref{normal:conormal-inequality} gives, for both signs of $t$,
\begin{align*}
 \alpha(p(t)-p)&\le C|p(t)-p|^2,\\
 t\alpha(V(p))&\le C t^2.
\end{align*}
It follows that $\alpha(V(p))=0$. In a local $h$-orthonormal
frame, write the components of $V(p)$ as $V^0,V^1,V^2$ and
the dual components of $\alpha$ as $\alpha_0,\alpha_1,\alpha_2$.
The identities $h(V(p),V(p))=-1$ and $\alpha(V(p))=0$ give
\begin{align*}
 (V^0)^2&=1+(V^1)^2+(V^2)^2,\\
 \alpha_0&=-\frac{\alpha_1V^1+\alpha_2V^2}{V^0}.
\end{align*}
Therefore
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 h^{-1}(\alpha,\alpha)
 &=\alpha_1^2+\alpha_2^2
     -\frac{(\alpha_1V^1+\alpha_2V^2)^2}{(V^0)^2}\\
 &\ge\frac{\alpha_1^2+\alpha_2^2}{(V^0)^2}
 \ge c|\alpha|_{g_+}^2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
For the last inequality we used the uniform bound for $|V(p)|_{g_+}$,
$\alpha_0^2\le\alpha_1^2+\alpha_2^2$, and equivalence of the
coordinate and $g_+$ norms on a finite frame cover of $K$.
The constant is independent of the exterior ball.
If $p_j\to p\in K$ and $\alpha_j\in N_H^P(p_j)$ converge to
$\alpha$, closedness gives $\alpha\in N_H^P(p)$, so the same
inequality also holds for every such limit.

If $N^P_{H}(p)$ is not a line,
Lemma~\ref{normal:parallel-boundary} gives one limit
$\mathcal J(p)$ of $(\mathbf Z^\flat,\mathbf D(\mathbf Z^\flat))$ as exterior points tend to $p$.
The positive lower bound for $\rho$ shows that every preceding
limit from a ball disjoint from $H$ is the projection of this same field divided by
$\rho(p)$. Hence
\begin{align*}
 \alpha(\mathbf Z(p))&=0
                 \quad(\alpha\in N^P_{H}(p)),\\
 h(\mathbf Z(p),\mathbf Z(p))&=-\rho(p)^2<0.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{closure:graph-band}
Let $p\in\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$.
If $N^P_{H}(p)$ is pointed, there are local coordinates
$(x,s)$, with $x\in\mathbb R^2$, in which
\[
 H=\{s\ge v(x)\},
\]
with $v$ Lipschitz and semiconvex. If this cone is a line, there
are coordinates of the same form, centered at $p$, in which
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H&=\{a(x)\le s\le b(x)\},\\
 a(0)&=b(0)=0,\\
 Da(0)&=Db(0)=0,
 \end{aligned}
\]
where $a$ is semiconvex and $b$ semiconcave.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We use the Euclidean metric of these coordinates to identify
vectors and covectors, and normalize the normal covectors in this metric. The change-of-metric calculation following
Definition~\ref{def:proximal-conormal} gives
\eqref{normal:conormal-inequality} with a uniform constant $C$ for
these unit covectors. Its closedness consequence makes the unit normal
cones upper semicontinuous: if $q_k\to p$ and unit
$\alpha_k\in N_{H}^P(q_k)$ converge to $\alpha$, then
$\alpha\in N_{H}^P(p)$.

In the pointed case choose a unit vector $E$ and $c>0$ with
$\alpha(E)>2c$ for every unit normal covector at $p$. After reducing the
neighborhood, every unit normal covector there satisfies $\alpha(E)>c$.
For a boundary point $q_0$, put
$z=q_0-tE+w$, where $|w|\le\epsilon t$ and $\epsilon<c/2$.
If $z\notin H$, let $q$ be its nearest point and
$\alpha=(z-q)/d$, where $d=|z-q|\le t+|w|$. Then
\[
 d+ct-|w|\le\alpha(q_0-q)
       \le C|q_0-q|^2
       \le C(d+t+|w|)^2.
\]
The left side is at least $ct/2$ and the right side is $O(t^2)$.
Thus this cone lies in $H$ for a uniform small range of $t$.
For $z=q_0+tE+w$, any unit normal covector at $q_0$ instead gives
$\alpha(z-q_0)\ge ct/2>C|z-q_0|^2$, so this opposite cone lies
outside $H$.

Choose a coordinate product of a disk with an interval whose end disks lie in these two cones at $p$.
The inner disk lies in $H$ and the outer disk lies outside $H$.
Every line segment parallel to $E$ therefore meets the boundary. Along
this segment, the two cones put an interval of $H$ below every boundary point and an exterior interval above it.
Two boundary crossings would require a reentry with the opposite
orientation. Thus each segment has exactly one crossing. The same
cones imply that its height difference between two such segments is at
most $\epsilon^{-1}$ times their separation: a larger difference
would place one boundary point in the other's interior or exterior
cone. Reverse $E$ to obtain the inward coordinate $s$ and the graph
$H=\{s\ge v(x)\}$. A unit normal covector at $(x,v(x))$ has
the decomposition $\alpha=\alpha_i\,dx^i+\alpha_s\,ds$, with
$i\in\{1,2\}$ and $\alpha_x=(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$.
Its height component satisfies $\alpha_s\le-c$, and hence
\[
 v(z)\ge v(x)-\frac{\alpha_x}{\alpha_s}\cdot(z-x)
       -\frac{C(1+\epsilon^{-2})}{c}|z-x|^2.
\]
After adding a fixed positive quadratic function to $v$, this
inequality gives an affine function touching it from below at
every point. This proves semiconvexity.

Suppose now that the cone at $p=0$ is $\mathbb R\,ds$.
Upper semicontinuity puts every nearby unit normal covector in small
neighborhoods of $ds$ and $-ds$. In particular,
\[
 |\alpha_s|\ge c>0.
\]
The two normal covectors at $p$ give
$|s|\le C(|x|^2+s^2)$ on $H$. Choose a closed cylinder
$\overline{B_R^2}\times[-\delta,\delta]$ so small that the regions near its two ends
$|s|\ge\delta/2$ miss $H$.

Every vertical segment with $|x_0|<R/4$ meets $H$ in this cylinder.
Otherwise minimize $|x-x_0|^2$ over its compact intersection
with $H$. Since $0\in H$, the minimum is at most $|x_0|^2$;
the minimizer $q=(x_q,s_q)$ cannot lie on a lateral face, and
the empty end strips exclude the other faces. If that segment missed $H$, $\nu=(x_0-x_q,0)$ would be nonzero and
\[
 \nu(z-q)\le\tfrac12|x_z-x_q|^2\le\tfrac12|z-q|^2
 \qquad(z\in H\text{ near }q).
\]
The quadratic characterization puts $\nu$ in $N_{H}^P(q)$. Since
$\nu_s=0$, this contradicts $|\alpha_s|\ge c$.

We now show that each vertical segment meets $H$ in one interval. For this we
first determine the orientation of a normal covector at an endpoint of a
possible gap. Take $q\in H$ with $q+t\partial_s\notin H$ for small
$t>0$. Let $q_t$ be the nearest point of $q+t\partial_s$,
put $d_t=|q+t\partial_s-q_t|$, and let
$\alpha_t=(q+t\partial_s-q_t)/d_t$. Since $|q-q_t|\le2t$,
\[
 t\alpha_t(\partial_s)
 =d_t-\alpha_t(q-q_t)\ge-4Ct^2.
\]
A limiting unit normal covector at $q$ therefore has nonnegative
$s$-component, and that component is at least $c$.
For a gap of length $d$ above $q$, evaluation at its upper endpoint
would give $cd\le Cd^2$. Take $2\delta<c/C$ to exclude every gap.
Thus its intersection with $H$ is one closed interval $[a(x),b(x)]$.
The same projection argument, with the sign of $t$ reversed,
gives a normal covector with $\alpha_s\le-c$ at its lower endpoint;
at its upper endpoint there is one with $\alpha_s\ge c$.

Compare the lower endpoints over $x$ and $\eta$.
Use a normal covector with $\alpha_s<0$ at the higher of the two lower endpoints.
The quadratic inequality \eqref{normal:conormal-inequality} gives
\[
 c|a(\eta)-a(x)|\le |\eta-x|
                  +C\bigl(|\eta-x|^2+|a(\eta)-a(x)|^2\bigr).
\]
Since $|a(\eta)-a(x)|\le2\delta$, reducing $\delta$ absorbs the last
term and proves a uniform Lipschitz bound. The upper endpoints
satisfy the same estimate, using normal covectors with positive $s$-component. If $L$ is a
common Lipschitz constant, the original inequalities now give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 a(\eta)&\ge a(x)-\frac{\alpha_x}{\alpha_s}\cdot(\eta-x)
                  -\frac{C(1+L^2)}c|\eta-x|^2,
                       &&\alpha_s\le-c,\\
 b(\eta)&\le b(x)-\frac{\beta_x}{\beta_s}\cdot(\eta-x)
                  +\frac{C(1+L^2)}c|\eta-x|^2,
                       &&\beta_s\ge c.
 \end{aligned}
\]
These are the asserted semiconvexity and semiconcavity estimates.
For $H$, the two normal covectors at $p$ moreover give
$|a(x)|+|b(x)|\le C'|x|^2$, so $Da(0)=Db(0)=0$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{local:optical-reduction}
Near a point of $\Sigma$ there is a smooth stationary field
$\widetilde\mathbf Z$, equal to $\mathbf Z$ on the chosen exterior, whose
projection is timelike for $h$ and whose local flow preserves
$\Sigma$. A transverse disk $S$ has coordinates $(y,s)$ in which
\[
 \Sigma\cap S=\{s=v(y)\},
\]
with $v$ Lipschitz and semiconvex and the chosen exterior given by
$s<v(y)$. Put $\rho^2=-h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,\widetilde\mathbf Z)$. The metric
\[
 q_{\mathrm{opt}}(X,Y)
 =\rho^{-2}h(X,Y)
       +\rho^{-4}h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,X)h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,Y),
 \qquad X,Y\in TS,
\]
is smooth and positive definite on $S$. It equals the optical metric on the chosen exterior. The Killing
and circularity identities for $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ hold on that exterior
and on $\Sigma$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Choose graph coordinates $(t,y,s)$ on $Q$, centered at the point
under consideration. After reversing $s$ for an upper exterior
component, the chosen exterior is $s<a(t,y)$ with $a$ Lipschitz
and semiconvex. On the fixed stationary section
put $\mathcal J=(\mathbf Z^\flat,\mathbf D(\mathbf Z^\flat))$ and choose a smooth
bundle frame. Write $\Gamma_s^\mathcal D$ for the matrix in this frame
of the Killing transport connection $\mathcal D$, pulled back to the stationary
section, in the $s$ direction.
Apply the transport construction \eqref{local:graph-transport}
from a strip about $s=s_-$ in the chosen exterior, solving
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \partial_s\widetilde{\mathcal J}(t,y,s)
    &=-\Gamma_s^\mathcal D(t,y,s)\widetilde{\mathcal J}(t,y,s),\\
 \widetilde{\mathcal J}(t,y,s_-)&=\mathcal J(t,y,s_-).
 \end{aligned}
\]
The smooth coefficients and strip data
give a smooth solution on the whole coordinate rectangle. Below
the boundary graph, each vertical segment stays in the exterior,
so uniqueness gives $\widetilde{\mathcal J}=\mathcal J$ there.
We take $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ to be the metric dual of the first
component and extend it stationarily. It is timelike for $h$ near
the boundary by Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits}. In the rectangle
we use the smooth field supplied by this transport equation. On the
chosen exterior and its boundary we also have the Killing and circularity identities,
by equality with $\mathbf Z$ and continuity.

We claim that the local flow of $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ preserves this boundary.
Reduce the cylinder and the time interval so that the trajectories
remain in the original cylinder. On the chosen exterior component this flow equals the
complete flow of $\mathbf Z$. A trajectory cannot change exterior
components without meeting $H$. For exterior points
$p_k\to p\in\partial H$, smooth dependence gives
\[
 \Phi_\tau^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(p)
     =\lim_k\Phi_\tau^\mathbf Z(p_k)
       \in\overline{Q\setminus H}.
\]
If this limit were exterior, the inverse flow would give
$p=\Phi_{-\tau}^\mathbf Z(\Phi_\tau^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(p))\notin H$,
a contradiction. Thus the flow preserves the boundary graph.

Writing $\widetilde\mathbf Z^i$ for the projected components in $(t,y,s)$,
the Lipschitz bound for $a$ along a boundary trajectory gives
\[
 |\widetilde\mathbf Z^s|
 \le C\bigl(|\widetilde\mathbf Z^t|+|\widetilde\mathbf Z^y|\bigr).
\]
Since $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ is timelike, its two base components cannot
both vanish. After a linear change of $(t,y)$ and a restriction of the chart,
we have $\widetilde\mathbf Z^t\ne0$. Take the transverse disk $S=\{t=0\}$, with coordinates $(y,s)$. The flow map
\[
 (\tau,y,s)\longmapsto\Phi_\tau^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(0,y,s)
\]
has, at $\tau=0$, the nonzero differential determinant
\[
 \det\bigl(\widetilde\mathbf Z,\partial_y,\partial_s\bigr)|_S
                 =\widetilde\mathbf Z^t|_S\ne0.
\]
It therefore gives a smooth local coordinate system. On the disk,
put $v(y)=a(0,y)$, which is Lipschitz and semiconvex by restriction.
Boundary invariance and the inverse flow then give $s=v(y)$ for
$\Sigma$ in these coordinates, with the chosen exterior $s<v(y)$.
For the metric in the statement we have
\[
 q_{\mathrm{opt}}(X,X)
 =\rho^{-2}h\bigl(X+\rho^{-2}h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,X)\widetilde\mathbf Z,
                  X+\rho^{-2}h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,X)\widetilde\mathbf Z\bigr).
\]
The vector inside the pairing is the $h$-orthogonal projection of
$X$ onto $\widetilde\mathbf Z^{\perp_h}$. This plane is spacelike, and
that projection is injective on the transverse plane $TS$.
Thus $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ is positive definite. On the exterior its
formula is exactly orthogonal projection to $\mathbf Z^{\perp_h}$ followed
by rescaling by $\rho^{-2}$, as in \eqref{action:reduction}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{local:optical-convexity}
In the transverse disk of Lemma~\ref{local:optical-reduction}, the
closed epigraph
\[
 C=\{(y,s):s\ge v(y)\}
\]
is locally geodesically convex for $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Use the local flow of $\widetilde\mathbf Z$ to give coordinates
$(\phi,y,s)$. On the chosen exterior, the Killing equation and
circularity give
\[
 h=\rho^2\bigl(-(d\phi+\beta)^2+q_{\mathrm{opt}}\bigr),
\]
where the coefficients are independent of $\phi$ and
$\beta=-\rho^{-2}h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,\cdot)|_{TS}$. To check its
closedness on the exterior, circularity gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=h(\mathbf Z,\cdot)\wedge d[h(\mathbf Z,\cdot)]\\
  &=\rho^4(d\phi+\beta)\wedge d\beta
   =\rho^4d\phi\wedge d\beta.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The last equality uses the two-dimensional disk, so $d\beta=0$.
We retain $\beta$, since the chosen disk need not be orthogonal to
the rotation.

Let $c$ be a sufficiently short affinely parametrized
$q_{\mathrm{opt}}$-geodesic with endpoints in $C$. If it leaves
$C$, take a component $(u_-,u_+)$ of its exterior excursion. Its
endpoints lie on $s=v(y)$. Write $k=|\dot c|_{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}$,
which is constant, and lift this excursion by
\[
 \dot\phi=k-\beta(\dot c).
\]
Locally choose $\chi$ with $d\chi=\beta$ and put
$\theta=\phi+\chi(c)$. Then $\dot\theta=k$, so
\eqref{local:optical-geodesics} identifies the lift as a null geodesic
of $-d\theta^2+q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ and hence, after reparametrization,
of $h$. These local descriptions agree on overlaps,
since the equation for $\phi$ is fixed.

There is no loss of the geodesic equation at the two endpoints of
the excursion. Let $\widehat c=(\phi,c)$ denote the lift just
constructed and $X$ the velocity of its stationary horizontal
spacetime lift, with the original parameter $u$. The conformal
connection formula and \eqref{local:null-lift} give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \nabla_{\dot{\widehat c}}\dot{\widehat c}
     &=2\frac d{du}\log\rho\,\dot{\widehat c},\\
 \mathbf D_XX
     &=\frac d{du}\log\!\left(\frac{\rho^2}{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)}\right)X.
 \end{aligned}
\]
In the second line both sides are horizontal, so the quotient
identity determines the spacetime identity. Therefore the change
of parameter
\[
 \frac{d\lambda}{du}
       =\frac{\rho^2}{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)}>0
\]
makes the spacetime lift affinely parametrized. On a smaller
flowbox this derivative and its reciprocal are bounded. The
coefficients of the optical metric, $\beta$, and the horizontal
lifting equation are smooth up to the boundary, and
$|\dot\phi|\le Ck$, $|X|_{\mathbf g_+}\le Ck$.
The lift and its velocity thus extend to both endpoints; the
affine geodesic equation holds there by continuity. Its auxiliary
length is at most $C\operatorname{Length}_{q_{\mathrm{opt}}}(c)$.
Choose the chord short enough that its entire lift stays in the
flowbox and this length is less than $\epsilon_0$.
Boundary invariance gives
\[
 (\phi(u_-),c(u_-)),\ (\phi(u_+),c(u_+))\in\Sigma\subset H,
\]
whereas the open arc lies in the chosen exterior. This contradicts
Definition~\ref{discrete:null-convexity}. Every short chord therefore
stays in $C$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{local:weak-cap}
Let $v,q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ be as in
Lemma~\ref{local:optical-reduction}. If a smooth curve $s=w(y)$
touches $v$ from below and has $\kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}>0$ toward
increasing $s$ at contact, its images under the local flow of
$\widetilde\mathbf Z$ form a supporting hypersurface for $H$ whose
stationary lift is strictly $\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Let $f=s-w(y)$ in the auxiliary flow coordinates. Since $w\le v$,
its entire negative side lies in the chosen exterior. At contact, the Killing and circularity identities follow by
continuity from that exterior. Let $n$ point toward increasing $s$,
and take the normalized null tangents $e_3,e_4$ of
Lemma~\ref{local:optical-curvature}. Since $nf>0$, equations
\eqref{local:optical-null-curvature} and \eqref{flow:null-extension} give
\[
 \mathbf D^2(f\circ\pi)(X,X)
  =-(nf)B(d\pi X,d\pi X)<0
 \quad\bigl(0\ne X,\ X(f\circ\pi)=\mathbf g(X,X)=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,X)=0\bigr).
\]
The same calculation holds if the touching curve meets the boundary
only at the contact point: shift it into the exterior, apply the
smooth identity there, and take the limit. Its first two derivatives
and the smooth metric coefficients converge at contact.
Since $df$ annihilates the timelike vector $\widetilde\mathbf Z$, its metric
dual is spacelike. The stationary conormal is therefore spacelike as well. This verifies
the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{local:extension}.

The extension agrees with $\mathbf Z$ on the entire chosen exterior in a
smaller neighborhood. Indeed, after applying the local theorem,
choose a rectangular flowbox inside the extension neighborhood and
a strip below both $w$ and $v$. The two fields and their first
derivatives agree on this strip. Every point below $v$ joins it by
a vertical segment in that exterior, and uniqueness for
\eqref{local:killing-connection} gives agreement along the segment.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{discrete:weak-null-tests}
Suppose locally
\[
 H=\{(t,y,s):a(t,y)\le s\le b(t,y)\},
\]
with the boundary graphs of Lemma~\ref{closure:graph-band}.
If $a(p)=b(p)$, no smooth strictly $\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex
hypersurface supports $H$ at $p$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Center the graph coordinates at $p$, with common height zero.
Semiconvexity of $a$ and semiconcavity of $b$ supply linear functions
$\ell_a,\ell_b$ with
\[
 \ell_a(x)-C|x|^2\le a(x)\le b(x)
                         \le\ell_b(x)+C|x|^2.
\]
Taking $x=rX$ with both signs of $r$ shows $\ell_a=\ell_b$.
Both graphs therefore have one common differential and a quadratic
remainder at $p$. Any smooth supporting hypersurface has this tangent
plane. Suppose such a hypersurface is strictly pseudoconvex and,
after reversing $s$ if necessary, take its defining function $f$
with $f\ge0$ on the upper graph and $df_p$ pointing toward increasing $s$. We use the auxiliary
field of Lemma~\ref{local:optical-reduction} constructed from the
upper exterior. In this proof all occurrences of $\widetilde\mathbf Z$
and $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ refer to this choice. The upper graph is
invariant under its local flow. As $f$ has a minimum along the
boundary orbit through $p$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \widetilde\mathbf Z f(p)&=0,\\
 \widetilde\mathbf Z^2f(p)&\ge0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Take a transverse disk $S$ tangent at $p$ to
$\widetilde\mathbf Z^{\perp_h}$, and extend $f|_S$ constantly by this
local flow to a function $\widetilde f$. At $p$ the two differentials
agree. We calculate the connection terms from the upper exterior
before taking their boundary limits. For fields
$A,B\perp_h\widetilde\mathbf Z$, Killing's equation and circularity give
\[
 2h(\nabla_A\widetilde\mathbf Z,B)
 =h(\nabla_A\widetilde\mathbf Z,B)-h(\nabla_B\widetilde\mathbf Z,A)
 =-h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,[A,B])=0.
\]
Together with
$h(\nabla_A\widetilde\mathbf Z,\widetilde\mathbf Z)=-\rho A\rho$,
this gives
$\nabla_A\widetilde\mathbf Z=(A\log\rho)\widetilde\mathbf Z$.
Killing's equation also gives
$\nabla_{\widetilde\mathbf Z}\widetilde\mathbf Z
=-\tfrac12\operatorname{grad}_h h(\widetilde\mathbf Z,\widetilde\mathbf Z)
=\rho\operatorname{grad}_h\rho$.
These first-derivative identities pass to $p$ by smoothness.
Put $e_0=-\widetilde\mathbf Z/\rho$ and choose
$E\in T_pS\cap\ker df_p$ with $h(E,E)=1$, extending $E$
by the auxiliary flow. At $p$ we consequently have
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \nabla_{e_0}e_0&=\rho^{-1}\operatorname{grad}_h\rho,\\
 \nabla_{e_0}E&=(E\log\rho)e_0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Since $[\widetilde\mathbf Z,E]=0$,
$\widetilde\mathbf Z\widetilde f=0$, and $df_p=d\widetilde f_p$,
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{Hess}_h\widetilde f(e_0,e_0)
   &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_0,e_0)-\rho^{-2}\widetilde\mathbf Z^2f,\\
 \operatorname{Hess}_h\widetilde f(e_0,E)&=0,\\
 \operatorname{Hess}_h\widetilde f(E,E)&=\operatorname{Hess}_hf(E,E).
\end{align*}
For $e_4=(e_0+E)/\sqrt2$ and $e_3=(e_0-E)/\sqrt2$, we have
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 2\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_4,e_4)
 &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_0,e_0)+2\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_0,E)+\operatorname{Hess}_hf(E,E),\\
 2\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_3,e_3)
 &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_0,e_0)-2\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_0,E)+\operatorname{Hess}_hf(E,E).
 \end{aligned}
\]
The mixed terms cancel in their sum. Thus each null Hessian of the
invariant function is
\begin{equation}\label{local:invariant-hessian}
 \operatorname{Hess}_h\widetilde f(e_a,e_a)
 =\frac{\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_3,e_3)+\operatorname{Hess}_hf(e_4,e_4)}2
             -\frac{\widetilde\mathbf Z^2f}{2\rho^2}<0,
 \qquad a=3,4.
\end{equation}
Boundary invariance gives $\widetilde f\ge0$ on the upper graph.
Its zero curve in $S$ therefore touches that graph from below, and
\eqref{local:invariant-hessian} makes its geodesic curvature strictly
positive toward increasing $s$. On the other hand,
Lemma~\ref{local:optical-convexity}, applied from the upper exterior,
says that the hypograph of this boundary is geodesically convex.
We verify the contradiction directly.

Use $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$-normal coordinates $(y,s)$ at contact,
with common tangent $s=0$. Write the upper boundary as $s=b(y)$
and the smooth lower curve as $s=w(y)$. The common differential
and quadratic remainder proved above give
\[
 b(y)=b(0)+w'(0)y+O(y^2)=O(y^2).
\]
Positive curvature gives $w''(0)>0$, so
\[
 \frac{b(\eta)+b(-\eta)}2
 \ge\frac{w(\eta)+w(-\eta)}2
 =\tfrac12w''(0)\eta^2+o(\eta^2).
\]
The short geodesic joining $(\pm\eta,b(\pm\eta))$ has coordinate
velocity $O(\eta)$ and Christoffel symbols $O(\eta)$. Its affine
midpoint $(y_m,s_m)$ consequently satisfies
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 y_m&=O(\eta^3),\\
 s_m&=\tfrac12\{b(\eta)+b(-\eta)\}+O(\eta^3).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Since $b(y_m)=O(\eta^6)$, this midpoint lies strictly above the
upper boundary for small $\eta$. This contradicts geodesic
convexity of its hypograph. Reversing $s$ treats a hypersurface
supporting from the upper exterior.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{local:geodesic-stalemate}
Let $\Sigma,v,q_{\mathrm{opt}}$ be as in
Lemma~\ref{local:optical-reduction}. If no point of $\Sigma$ admits
a smooth strictly $\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex supporting
hypersurface from the chosen exterior, then $\Sigma\cap S$ is a
smooth $q_{\mathrm{opt}}$-geodesic and
\[
 B_{33}=B_{44}=0\quad\text{on }\Sigma.
\]
The null curves tangent to $\Sigma$ are $h$-geodesics after
reparametrization.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{local:optical-convexity}, the epigraph of $v$ is
locally geodesically convex. We first exclude a corner. If the two
one-sided slopes at $y_0$ differ, choose a slope $\mu$ strictly between
them. Semiconvexity gives $c>0$ and $C<\infty$ such that
\[
 v(y)-v(y_0)-\mu(y-y_0)
       \ge c|y-y_0|-C|y-y_0|^2.
\]
For any fixed sufficiently large $A$, the smooth curve
\[
 w(y)=v(y_0)+\mu(y-y_0)+A(y-y_0)^2
\]
lies below $v$ near $y_0$ and has positive geodesic curvature at
contact. Lemma~\ref{local:weak-cap} contradicts the hypothesis.
Thus $v$ is differentiable. The subdifferential of the convex
function $v+C y^2$ is a singleton at every point and is upper
semicontinuous, so $v'$ is continuous.

Join two sufficiently close boundary points by their short
$q_{\mathrm{opt}}$-geodesic. Convexity puts the chord in the
closed epigraph. Since the boundary is $C^1$, Fermi coordinates
$(y,n)$ about this chord express the intervening boundary as
$n=v(y)$, with
\[
 v(y_-) = v(y_+) = 0 \quad\text{and}\quad v\le0.
\]
In these coordinates, with $y$ the arclength on the chord,
\[
 q_{\mathrm{opt}}=dn^2+J(y,n)^2dy^2.
\]
The geodesic equation of the chord gives
\[
 J(y,0)=1 \quad\text{and}\quad J_n(y,0)=0.
\]
The only possibly nonzero Christoffel symbols are
\[
 \Gamma^n_{yy}=-JJ_n,\qquad
 \Gamma^y_{yy}=J_y/J,\qquad
 \Gamma^y_{yn}=\Gamma^y_{ny}=J_n/J.
\]
They vanish on $n=0$, since $J_y(y,0)=0$, and satisfy
$|\Gamma^k_{ij}(y,n)|\le C|n|$ on a smaller strip.
The constant is uniform for these short chords: their initial
points and unit tangents lie in a compact set, and the normal
exponential map depends smoothly on these data.

Suppose that $v<0$ somewhere. Let $d=y_+-y_-$, let $y_c$ be the
midpoint, and fix $K>2C$, where $C$ bounds the coefficients in
the curvature expression below. After reducing $d$, the function
$\eta(y)=1-K(y-y_c)^2$ satisfies $1/2\le\eta\le1$. Put
\[
 \lambda=\max_{[y_-,y_+]}\frac{-v}{\eta}>0.
\]
The curve $w=-\lambda\eta$ lies below $v$, with an interior contact. The local Lipschitz bound
for the boundary gives $\lambda\le 2\|v\|_\infty\le Cd$, and
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |w|&\le\lambda,\\
 |w'|&\le\lambda Kd,\\
 w''&=2K\lambda.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Write $Y=\partial_y+w'\partial_n$. The unit normal pointing
toward increasing $n$ has metric dual
$J(dn-w'\,dy)/(J^2+(w')^2)^{1/2}$. Using the three Christoffel
symbols above, the signed geodesic curvature is
\[
 \kappa_{\mathrm{opt}}
 =\frac{J}{(J^2+(w')^2)^{3/2}}
       \bigl\{w''-JJ_n-(J_y/J)w'-2(J_n/J)(w')^2\bigr\}.
\]
All coefficients here are evaluated at $(y,w(y))$.
Reduce $d$ further, keeping $K$ fixed, so that
$|w'|\le\lambda Kd\le1$. Since $|w|\le\lambda$, the expression
in braces is bounded below by
\[
 2K\lambda-C\lambda\{1+|w'|+(w')^2\}
       \ge (2K-3C)\lambda>0.
\]
This proves strict positivity with the required orientation.
Lemma~\ref{local:weak-cap} again gives a forbidden supporting
hypersurface. Therefore $v=0$: every sufficiently short chord
between boundary points lies in the boundary, and the boundary
is the smooth geodesic determined by those endpoints.

The optical curvature identity now gives $B_{33}=B_{44}=0$.
We use the null-tangent argument of Foertsch, Hasse, and
Perlick~\cite{FHP03}. For a nonzero tangent null field $X$ on $\Sigma$, the Gauss formula
and the induced connection $\nabla^\Sigma$ give
\begin{equation}\label{local:null-surface-acceleration}
 \nabla_X X=\nabla_X^\Sigma X+B(X,X)n
                   =\nabla_X^\Sigma X.
\end{equation}
Furthermore
$h(\nabla_X^\Sigma X,X)=\tfrac12Xh(X,X)=0$.
The orthogonal complement of $X$ in the Lorentzian tangent plane
is $\mathbb R X$, so $\nabla_XX\in\mathbb R X$, as required.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{closure:corner-cap}
If $p\in\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ and
$\dim N^P_{H}(p)=2$, there are a neighborhood $V$ of $p$
and $f\in C^\infty(V)$ with spacelike nonzero differential such that
\begin{align*}
 f(p)&=0,\\
 H\cap V&\subset\{f\ge0\}
\end{align*}
and
\[
 \operatorname{Hess}_hf(X,X)<0
 \qquad\bigl(0\ne X\in\ker df(p),\ h(X,X)=0\bigr).
\]
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits}, $N^P_{H}(p)$ lies in the
plane of covectors annihilating the common timelike field, on which
$h^{-1}$ is positive definite. Take independent normal covectors $\alpha,\beta$ and
$h$-normal coordinates $x$ centered at $p$. Regard the covectors
as linear functions in these coordinates. Equation~\eqref{normal:conormal-inequality} gives
\[
 \max\{\alpha(x),\beta(x)\}\le C|x|^2
 \qquad(x\in H).
\]
The line $\ker\alpha\cap\ker\beta$ is timelike. Consequently
\[
 \min_{\substack{|X|=1,\ h(X,X)=0\\(\alpha+\beta)(X)=0}}
                 |(\alpha-\beta)(X)|>0.
\]
Choose $M$ sufficiently large and put
\[
 f(x)=-(\alpha+\beta)(x)+(2C+1)|x|^2
                                  -M(\alpha-\beta)(x)^2.
\]
The differential at $p$ is $-(\alpha+\beta)\ne0$, and is spacelike.
Since the Christoffel symbols vanish at $p$,
\[
 \operatorname{Hess}_hf(X,X)=2(2C+1)|X|^2
                         -2M\bigl((\alpha-\beta)(X)\bigr)^2<0
\]
on its nonzero null tangents.

For $x\in H$, the same two normal inequalities give
\begin{align*}
 |(\alpha-\beta)(x)|
 &\le-(\alpha+\beta)(x)+2C|x|^2,\\
 M(\alpha-\beta)(x)^2
 &\le MC_1|x|\bigl(-(\alpha+\beta)(x)+2C|x|^2\bigr).
\end{align*}
Here $C_1$ bounds the norm of $\alpha-\beta$.
Reduce the coordinate ball so that $MC_1|x|\le1/2$. Then
\begin{align*}
 f(x)&\ge(1-MC_1|x|)(-(\alpha+\beta)(x))
           +(2C+1-2CMC_1|x|)|x|^2\\
 &\ge\bigl[-2C(1-MC_1|x|)+2C+1-2CMC_1|x|\bigr]|x|^2
 =|x|^2.
\end{align*}
Thus its entire negative side is in the existing domain.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{closure:orbit-tube}
Let $p\in\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ admit a
smooth strictly $\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex supporting
hypersurface. There is a connected stationary invariant neighborhood
$V\subset\mathbf E$ of $\pi^{-1}(p)$ carrying a commuting Killing extension
$\widetilde\mathbf Z$ of complete effective period $2\pi$, with
\[
 \widetilde\mathbf Z=\mathbf Z\quad\text{on }V\cap U_{H}.
\]
Its flow preserves $H\cap\pi(V)$.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Put $U=U_{H}$. Let $f$ define the supporting hypersurface,
with $\{f<0\}\subset\pi(U)$.
Since $f(p)=0$ and $f\ge0$ on $H$ near $p$, Taylor's
formula in a fixed coordinate chart gives
\[
 -df_p(x-p)\le C|x-p|^2
 \qquad(x\in H\text{ near }p).
\]
Thus $-df_p\in N^P_{H}(p)\setminus\{0\}$.
Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits} and
Definition~\ref{def:stationary-metrics} give
\[
 \mathbf g^{-1}(\pi^{\ast}df,\pi^{\ast}df)
   =\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)h^{-1}(df,df)>0.
\]
Thus the conormal is spacelike, as required in
Proposition~\ref{local:extension}. If the normal cone $N_H^P(p)$
were a line, Lemma~\ref{closure:graph-band} would
give $H=\{a\le s\le b\}$ with $a(p)=b(p)$, and
Lemma~\ref{discrete:weak-null-tests} would exclude the strict
support. Thus Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits} gives one limit
of $(\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\mathbf Z)$ from all nearby exterior components.

To carry the supporting hypersurface around the orbit, we first extend the old flow
maps smoothly to the boundary. We work on $Q$ and write $\Phi_t$
for the projected old flow. The complete orbits stay in a fixed compact subset of the ergoregion, by the confinement argument following
Lemma~\ref{closure:null-reach}. A finite cover of its boundary part
by Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits} bounds every derivative of $\mathbf Z$
there. The remaining exterior part has compact closure in $\pi(U)$,
where $\mathbf Z$ is smooth. We thus have uniform coefficient bounds
along the full orbit interval in the variational equations.
Here $D$ denotes coordinate differentiation of the projected field
and its flow:
\begin{align*}
 \frac d{dt}D\Phi_t&=(D\mathbf Z)_{\Phi_t}D\Phi_t,\\
 \frac d{dt}D^2\Phi_t
 &=(D^2\mathbf Z)_{\Phi_t}(D\Phi_t,D\Phi_t)
                         +(D\mathbf Z)_{\Phi_t}D^2\Phi_t.
\end{align*}
Gronwall's inequality and successive differentiation therefore give
\[
 \sup_{|t|\le2\pi,\ x\in\{f<0\}}
       \bigl(|D^k\Phi_t(x)|+|(D\Phi_t(x))^{-1}|\bigr)\le C_k
 \qquad(k\ge1)
\]
on a smaller smooth half-neighborhood of $p$.
In coordinates $(z,r)$ flattening $f=0$, these bounds imply
\[
 |D^k\Phi_t(z,-r)-D^k\Phi_t(z,-s)|\le C_{k+1}|r-s|.
\]
Thus the maps and all their derivatives have smooth one-sided
limits on $f=0$, jointly with $t$. We use $\Phi_t(x)$ and
$d\Phi_t|_x$ also for these limits.

Let $n$ be the spacelike unit normal toward $f>0$, and let
$\exp^h$ be the exponential map of $h$. Define a map on a full
neighborhood of $f=0$ by
\begin{equation}\label{closure:extended-flow-map}
 I_t\bigl(\exp_x^h(rn_x)\bigr)
   =\exp_{\Phi_t(x)}^h\bigl(r\,d\Phi_t|_x n_x\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $r<0$, isometry invariance of the geodesic equation in
\eqref{closure:extended-flow-map} gives
\[
 I_t=\Phi_t\quad\Longrightarrow\quad I_t^{\ast}h=h.
\]
At $r=0$ we have $dI_t=d\Phi_t$, which is invertible.
The inverse function theorem gives a uniform smaller neighborhood
on which $I_t$ is a diffeomorphism for $|t|\le2\pi$.
Set $\gamma(t)=\Phi_t(p)$ and $f_t=f\circ I_t^{-1}$.
We use the isometry identity on the negative side to compute the
second derivatives of $I_t$ at the boundary, where we must preserve the
strict Hessian inequality. Write $\Gamma^k_{ij}$ for the Christoffel
symbols of $h$. Preservation of the connection on the negative side
reads
\[
 \partial_i\partial_j I_t^k
 +\Gamma^k_{ab}(I_t)\partial_iI_t^a\partial_jI_t^b
 -\Gamma^a_{ij}\partial_aI_t^k=0.
\]
Smoothness gives the same equation at $p$, or
$(\nabla dI_t)_p=0$, where the covariant derivative uses $h$
on both the source and target. Differentiating $f=f_t\circ I_t$ twice,
we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{Hess}_hf(X,X)|_p
 &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf_t(dI_tX,dI_tX)|_{\gamma(t)}
       +df_t\bigl((\nabla dI_t)(X,X)\bigr)|_p\\
 &=\operatorname{Hess}_hf_t(dI_tX,dI_tX)|_{\gamma(t)}.
\end{align*}
Moreover, $dI_t$ preserves $h$ at $p$ and
\[
 \{f_t<0\}=I_t(\{f<0\})\subset\pi(U).
\]
Thus the null tangents and their strict Hessian inequality are
transported together, and $\{f_t=0\}$ is a strictly pseudoconvex supporting
hypersurface at $\gamma(t)$.

For $p_j\in\{f<0\}$ approaching $p$, we have
$\Phi_t(p_j)\to\gamma(t)$.
If $\gamma(t)\in\pi(U)$, continuity of the old inverse flow gives
\[
 p=\lim_j\Phi_{-t}(\Phi_t(p_j))
                       =\Phi_{-t}(\gamma(t))\in\pi(U),
\]
a contradiction. Thus
\begin{align*}
 \gamma([0,2\pi])&\subset\partial H,\\
 \gamma(2\pi)&=\gamma(0)=p.
\end{align*}
We can now apply the local extension theorem all along the compact
curve. Each point has such a supporting hypersurface, so
$N^P_{H}(\gamma(t))$ is not a line. Combining the local theorem with
Lemma~\ref{normal:parallel-boundary}, we obtain local Killing fields with
\[
 (\widetilde\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\widetilde\mathbf Z)|_{\gamma(t)}
   =\lim_{\substack{x\to\gamma(t)\\x\in\pi(U)}}(\mathbf Z,\mathbf D\mathbf Z)(x),
\]
where the limits of the spacetime field and its covariant derivative
are taken on the fixed stationary section.
Each field agrees with $\mathbf Z$ throughout its intersection with $U$.
Apply the first part of Lemma~\ref{local:compatibility} to the
compact set on $\vartheta=0$ above $\gamma([0,2\pi])$.
It gives one stationary Killing extension near that set, agreeing
with $\mathbf Z$ on the whole old overlap. The curve $\gamma$ is an
integral curve of its projection, since differentiation of the
one-sided flow limits gives
\[
 \dot\gamma(t)=\lim_{x\to p,\ f(x)<0}\mathbf Z(\Phi_t(x))
                           =\widetilde\mathbf Z(\gamma(t)).
\]
Together with $\gamma(2\pi)=\gamma(0)$, this proves invariance
of the compact projected curve. The second part of
Lemma~\ref{local:compatibility} therefore provides a stationary
invariant neighborhood $V$ with complete period $2\pi$.
A smaller effective period would be the identity on a nonempty
open subset of the connected old domain and, by isometry
uniqueness, throughout it, contrary to the initial normalization.

It remains to show that the complete circle action preserves the remaining
set. Lemma~\ref{local:complete-flows}, applied to the complete
flows on $U$ and $V$, gives
\[
 \Phi_t^{\widetilde\mathbf Z}(U\cap V)=U\cap V.
\]
Choose $V$ over $\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal D_0)$ in the ergoregion. Since
\[
 V\setminus U=V\cap\pi^{-1}(H),
\]
the inverse maps prove the asserted preservation of $H$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{local:invariant-cut}
If $p\in\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ admits a smooth strictly
$\mathbf T$-conditionally pseudoconvex supporting hypersurface, there is
an open set $A\ni p$, with $\overline A$ contained in the open
ergoregion, for which $H'=H\setminus A$ and a field extending
$\mathbf Z$ on $U_H$ satisfy Definition~\ref{discrete:admissible}.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
We first make the defining function invariant under the extended
circle action, and then localize the removal in a fixed neighborhood.
By Proposition~\ref{closure:orbit-tube}, the projected orbit $O\subset Q$
through $p$ has a complete invariant neighborhood whose inverse image
carries a Killing extension of $\mathbf Z$. The union of this spacetime
neighborhood with $U_{H}$ is connected, so we may apply
Lemma~\ref{action:area} to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat&=0,\\
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge\mathbf Z^\flat\wedge d\mathbf Z^\flat&=0.
\end{align*}
Thus the spacelike distribution $\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}^{\perp_\mathbf g}$ is
integrable throughout the extension neighborhood. We work on its
stationary quotient and write $\Psi_t$ for the projected flow.
The generator is timelike for $h$, so its stabilizer is finite.
If $t$ has order $m$ in this stabilizer, then for a spacetime point
$q$ above $p$ there is $a\in\mathbb R$ with
\[
 \Phi_t^\mathbf Z(q)=\Phi_a^\mathbf T(q)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 q=\Phi_{mt}^\mathbf Z(q)=\Phi_{ma}^\mathbf T(q).
\]
The stationary action is free, so $a=0$.
Thus the stabilizer fixes $q$. Its differential fixes $\mathbf T_q,\mathbf Z_q$
and preserves the spacetime orientation, so it acts by rotations on
the spacelike plane $\{\mathbf T,\mathbf Z\}^{\perp_\mathbf g}$.
The representation is faithful: a trivial rotation fixes this plane
as well as $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$, so the isometry fixes $q$ and has identity differential there. It
would give a smaller period on the old domain.

The stabilizer preserves $N^P_{H}(p)$ in the dual of
the transverse plane.
A nontrivial finite rotation cannot preserve a nonzero proper convex
cone unless that cone is a line. The line case was excluded in the
proof of Proposition~\ref{closure:orbit-tube}. Thus either the orbit
is free or the cone is the whole dual of the transverse plane.

Suppose first that this cone is proper, so the orbit is free.
Let $f$ define the supporting hypersurface at $p$. We shall make
$f$ invariant while preserving its strict Hessian inequality on
null tangent vectors.
Since $O\subset H$ and $f\ge0$ there near $p$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf Z f(p)&=0,\\
 \mathbf Z^2f(p)&\ge0.
\end{align*}
Take a horizontal disk $S$ through $p$ for the static expression
$h=-\rho^2d\phi^2+q$, where $q=h|_{TS}$ and
$\rho^2=-h(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)$.
A neighborhood of the entire orbit is $S^1\times S$.
Extend $f|_S$ constantly along the circles and call the resulting
function $\widetilde f$. Then
\begin{align*}
 d\widetilde f(p)&=df(p),\\
 \widetilde f|_{H}&\ge0.
\end{align*}

Equation~\eqref{local:invariant-hessian}, applied with the extended
Killing field $\mathbf Z$, gives
$\operatorname{Hess}_h\widetilde f(e_a,e_a)<0$ for $a=3,4$, since
$\mathbf Z^2f(p)\ge0$. The circle isometries transport this inequality
around $O$.
Thus circularity and the minimum of $f$ on the orbit give an
invariant supporting hypersurface satisfying the strict Hessian inequality. We now localize the portion to be removed,
keeping it away from the edge of one fixed orbit neighborhood.
Choose coordinates $(s,y)$ on $S$ with $\widetilde f=s$ and
$p=(0,0)$. For $\delta>0$ sufficiently small put
\[
 f_\delta=s+\delta(s^2+y^2).
\]
On $O$ its differential is unchanged, and its Hessian differs from that
in \eqref{local:invariant-hessian} by
$\delta\operatorname{Hess}_h(s^2+y^2)$. To make the choice uniform, consider
the compact set
\[
 \{(x,X):x\in O,\ |X|_{g_+}=1,\ h(X,X)=0,\ ds(X)=0\}.
\]
On this set $-\operatorname{Hess}_hs(X,X)$ has a positive minimum $\mu$,
and $|\operatorname{Hess}_h(s^2+y^2)(X,X)|$ has a finite maximum $C_0$.
Choose $\delta C_0<\mu/4$. Since $df_\delta=ds$ on $O$,
\[
 -\operatorname{Hess}_h f_\delta(X,X)\ge 3\mu/4
       \qquad(x\in O,\ |X|_{g_+}=1,\ h(X,X)=df_\delta(X)=0).
\]
The conormal remains spacelike near $O$. Continuity of the two
null tangent lines then gives, on a smaller invariant neighborhood,
\[
 -\operatorname{Hess}_h f_\delta(X,X)\ge(\mu/2)|X|_{g_+}^2
       \qquad(h(X,X)=df_\delta(X)=0).
\]
Choose this neighborhood as
\[
 V=S^1\times\{|s|<R,\ |y|<R\},\qquad \delta R<1/8,
\]
with closure in the extension neighborhood. Since $s\ge0$ on
$H\cap V$,
\begin{equation}\label{local:cut-margin}
 f_\delta\ge\delta(s^2+y^2)
                         \quad\text{on }H\cap\overline V.
\end{equation}
Choose $0<\epsilon<\delta R^2/64$ and set
\[
 A=V\cap\{f_\delta<\epsilon\}.
\]
Its boundary inside $V$ is a graph, since
\begin{align*}
 \partial_s f_\delta&=1+2\delta s>1/2,\\
 f_\delta(-R,y)&<0<\epsilon<f_\delta(R,y).
\end{align*}
Thus $A$ is connected and meets the old domain, since its strip
$s=-R/2$, $|y|<R/2$ lies below the supporting graph and satisfies
\[
 f_\delta\le-R/2+\delta R^2/2<-R/4<\epsilon.
\]
On the remaining set, \eqref{local:cut-margin} gives directly
\[
 \overline{H\cap A}
 \subset\{s^2+y^2\le\epsilon/\delta\}\Subset V.
\]
The field on the invariant neighborhood agrees with the old field
on its entire overlap by Proposition~\ref{closure:orbit-tube}.
The invariant function $f_\delta$ and the preceding compact
containment therefore satisfy every hypothesis of
Lemma~\ref{discrete:admissible-update}. We obtain the required
pair on $U_{H\setminus A}$.

We finally treat the case where $N^P_{H}(p)$ is the
whole dual of the transverse plane. Here we will remove a tube around the entire
orbit, including the case of a finite stabilizer. Take a small
transverse disk through $p$, with coordinates $x$ and tangent space
$\mathbf Z_p^{\perp_h}$. Use the Euclidean coordinate norm on this disk and
its dual norm on covectors. Taking the supremum in its normal inequalities
and absorbing the quadratic coordinate error gives
\[
 |x|=\sup_{|\alpha|=1}\alpha(x)\le C|x|^2
                  \qquad(x\in H\cap S).
\]
Thus $H\cap S=\{p\}$ after shrinking. Taking the images
of the disk under the circle action
gives a connected invariant neighborhood $V$ with
\begin{align*}
 H\cap\overline V&=O,\\
 H\cap\partial V&=\varnothing.
\end{align*}
The transverse punctured disk lies in the old domain, so $V$
meets $\pi(U_H)$. The entire-component case of
Lemma~\ref{discrete:admissible-update}, with $A=V$, completes the
construction.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{closure:line-stalemate}
Let $\Sigma,\widetilde\Sigma$ be smooth timelike surfaces in
$(Q,h)$ whose second fundamental forms vanish on null tangents.
If they are locally ordered graphs tangent at $p$, they coincide
near $p$.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Write the graph heights as $v\le\widetilde v$. We use the
induced connections on the two surfaces and either choice of their
unit spacelike normals in the Gauss formula. We first follow a
common nonzero null tangent $L_p$ at $p$. By \eqref{local:null-surface-acceleration}, an affinely parametrized
null geodesic for either induced metric is also an ambient affinely
parametrized geodesic. Uniqueness therefore puts $\gamma(s)=\exp_p^h(sL_p)$ in both
surfaces. Along this curve,
\[
 \widetilde v-v=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad D(\widetilde v-v)=0,
\]
because the difference is nonnegative. Their tangent planes agree.
Set $L(s)=\dot\gamma(s)$, and let $\underline L(s)$ be the
other common null vector, normalized by $h(L,\underline L)=-1$. The same application of \eqref{local:null-surface-acceleration}
shows that
\[
 \Psi(s,t)=\exp_{\gamma(s)}^h(t\underline L(s))
                        \in\Sigma\cap\widetilde\Sigma.
\]
At $(0,0)$ the differential columns are $L_p$ and $\underline L(0)$.
Their pairing $h(L_p,\underline L(0))=-1$ makes them independent,
so $\Psi$ has rank two and its image is an open surface in both
$\Sigma$ and $\widetilde\Sigma$. This proves local coincidence.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
A strict supporting hypersurface at
$p\in\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ would give, by
Lemma~\ref{local:invariant-cut}, a pair $(H',\mathbf Z')$ with
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H'&=H\setminus A\subsetneq H,\\
 \mathbf Z'|_{U_H}&=\mathbf Z.
 \end{aligned}
\]
This contradicts maximality. Thus no boundary point in the open
ergoregion admits such a hypersurface.

If $N_H^P(p)$ is not a line, Lemma~\ref{closure:normal-limits}
puts it in the spacelike plane annihilating one timelike vector.
Lemma~\ref{closure:corner-cap} excludes dimension two. Since every
boundary point has a nonzero normal covector, its normal cone is
a ray or a line.

In the ray case, Lemma~\ref{closure:graph-band} gives a one-sided
boundary graph. Extend the optical metric smoothly from its exterior by
Lemma~\ref{local:optical-reduction}. The absence of strict supports
and Lemma~\ref{local:geodesic-stalemate} make its transverse curve
a smooth geodesic of the optical metric. Its rotational images are therefore a
smooth timelike hypersurface with
\[
 B_{33}=B_{44}=0.
\]

In the line case, write $H=\{a\le s\le b\}$ as in
Lemma~\ref{closure:graph-band}. We use the lower and upper exterior
components separately. Where the graphs differ, a supporting
hypersurface for either face is a supporting hypersurface for $H$
in a smaller neighborhood. At a point where they meet, the
calculation from the opposite exterior in Lemma~\ref{discrete:weak-null-tests}
excludes a strict support. Thus
Lemma~\ref{local:geodesic-stalemate} applies from each exterior,
with its own optical metric, and makes both faces smooth timelike
photon surfaces. They are ordered and tangent at $p$, so
Lemma~\ref{closure:line-stalemate} identifies them near $p$.

Every null tangent in a Lorentzian two-plane is a multiple of
$e_3$ or $e_4$. The displayed vanishing therefore holds for every
null tangent, proving the proposition.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{local:brake-reflection}
Let $(H,\mathbf Z)$ be the maximal pair above. Suppose that the
zero-energy null geodesic $\gamma:(s_-,s_0)\to\mathbf E$ is parametrized
by $\mathbf g_+$-arclength, that $s_0<\infty$, and that
\[
 \pi(\gamma(s))\in\partial H.
\]
If
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \lim_{s\uparrow s_0}(\gamma(s),\dot\gamma(s))&=(p_0,v_0),\\
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p_0)&=0,
 \end{aligned}
\]
then $\gamma$ continues through $s_0$ with projection in
$\partial H$. Moreover, $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(\gamma(s))>0$ for all
sufficiently small $0<|s-s_0|$. In an affine parameter $\tau$
with $\tau(s_0)=0$, its transverse coordinates satisfy
\[
 (\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T),\psi)(\gamma(\tau))
       =(\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T),\psi)(\gamma(-\tau)).
\]
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Equation~\eqref{action:normalized-flow} continues the geodesic
through $(p_0,v_0)$. Since $\mathbf T$ is nonzero and null at $p_0$,
we have $v_0=\lambda\mathbf T_{p_0}$ for a nonzero $\lambda$.
Since $\mathbf T[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]=0$ and
$\mathbf D_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma$ is proportional to $\dot\gamma$,
\eqref{local:ergosurface-convexity} gives
\begin{align*}
 \frac{d}{ds}\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(\gamma(s))\big|_{s_0}&=0,\\
 \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(\gamma(s))\big|_{s_0}
 &=\frac{\lambda^2}{2}|d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2\big|_{p_0}>0.
\end{align*}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{local:isolated-contact}
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(\gamma(s))
 =\frac{\lambda^2}{4}|d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]|_\mathbf g^2\big|_{p_0}
       (s-s_0)^2+o((s-s_0)^2).
\end{equation}
In particular, the final incoming interval lies in the open
ergoregion. Choose an affine parameter $\tau$ with $\tau(s_0)=0$ and
$(d\tau/ds)(s_0)=1$, so the contact velocity remains $v_0$.
Below a dot denotes $d/d\tau$.

By Lemma~\ref{canon:regularity}, the gradients of
$x^1=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)$ and $x^2=\psi$ span a spacelike plane in a
neighborhood of $p_0$. Their common level surfaces are therefore
Lorentzian. On any one of these surfaces, $\mathbf T$ is Killing and
has constant length. If $\nabla^C$ is its intrinsic connection,
then $A(X)=\nabla^C_X\mathbf T$ is skew-adjoint and
\[
 \mathbf g(AX,\mathbf T)=\tfrac12X\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)=0.
\]
Skew-adjointness then gives
\[
 A\mathbf T=0.
\]
A nonzero skew-adjoint endomorphism of a Lorentzian two-plane
is invertible. Hence $A=0$. Since the intrinsic curvature
annihilates the nonzero parallel field $\mathbf T$, the curvature
of the surface vanishes as well. Along a smooth transversal,
choose a second tangent vector to each level surface, independent
of $\mathbf T$, and extend it by intrinsic parallel transport. On a small
simply connected level-surface patch this gives a smooth field
$Y$, independent of $\mathbf T$, with
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \nabla^C Y&=0,\\
 [\mathbf T,Y]&=0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
We thus obtain local coordinates $(x^1,x^2,\theta,\phi)$ with
$\mathbf T=\partial_\theta$ and $Y=\partial_\phi$. Their coordinate
domain is a product $B\times P$, where $B$ is a rectangle in the
$x$ variables and $P$ is a rectangle about $(0,0)$ in $(\theta,\phi)$, with $\phi(p_0)=0$.

On the part of $U_H$ with $x^1>0$, the actual field $\mathbf Z$
preserves $x^1,x^2$ by circularity and is independent of $\mathbf T$
by Lemma~\ref{action:area}. Its restriction to a level surface
is Killing. Commutation with the parallel field $\mathbf T$ implies
that its intrinsic skew derivative annihilates $\mathbf T$, so the
same two-dimensional argument gives
\[
 \mathbf Z=a(x)Y+b(x)\mathbf T,
 \qquad a(x)\ne0
\]
on each connected level-surface patch in $U_H$. Here and below the
coefficients are defined only where $\mathbf Z$ is already known.
Completeness of the action on $U_H$ makes each such patch
contain the whole coordinate rectangle $P$. Indeed, at a
point with coefficients $a_0,b_0$, the complete field
$(\mathbf Z-b_0\mathbf T)/a_0$ agrees with $Y$ along that level surface,
and its flow is explicitly
\[
 \Phi_t^{(\mathbf Z-b_0\mathbf T)/a_0}(q)
 =\Phi_{t/a_0}^{\mathbf Z}\circ\Phi_{-b_0t/a_0}^{\mathbf T}(q)
                   \qquad(t\in\mathbb R).
\]
The right-hand side belongs to $U_H$ for every $t$.
A finite segment of its flow cannot leave $U_H$ while
remaining in the coordinate patch: at a first exit, completeness
would give a point of $U_H$ with the same limit, contrary to local
uniqueness. Applying this to $Y$ and to $\mathbf T$ proves that, for an
open set $O\subset B\cap\{x^1>0\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{local:plaque-invariance}
 U_H\cap\{x^1>0\}\cap(B\times P)=O\times P.
\end{equation}
In particular, $a,b$ are smooth functions on $O$. Taking boundaries
in $B\cap\{x^1>0\}$ and using \eqref{discrete:exact-domain} gives
\[
 \pi^{-1}(\partial H)\cap\{x^1>0\}\cap(B\times P)
 =\partial_{B\cap\{x^1>0\}}O\times P.
\]
Thus, if one point over $x$ lies in this boundary, every point
of $\{x\}\times P$ does. This is the property needed to reconstruct
the outgoing spacetime curve from its transverse coordinates.

We now compute the geodesic equation in this smooth local frame.
Use capital indices $A,B\in\{\theta,\phi\}$ for the level-surface
coordinates $y=(\theta,\phi)$ and lower-case indices $i,j\in\{1,2\}$
for $x$. Completing the square gives
\begin{equation}\label{local:regular-metric}
 \mathbf g=G_{AB}(x)(dy^A+\omega_i^A dx^i)(dy^B+\omega_j^B dx^j)
                  +\sigma_{ij}(x,\phi)dx^i dx^j.
\end{equation}
The matrix $G$ is the metric of the parallel frame $(\mathbf T,Y)$,
so it depends only on $x$. It is Lorentzian, and
\[
 W[\mathbf T,Y]=-\det G=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,Y)^2-x^1\mathbf g(Y,Y)>0.
\]
All the coefficients are smooth across $x^1=0$. With
$(G^{AB})=G^{-1}$, the remaining coefficients are determined by
\begin{align*}
 \omega_i^A&=G^{AB}\mathbf g(\partial_{y^B},\partial_{x^i}),\\
 \sigma_{ij}&=\mathbf g(\partial_{x^i},\partial_{x^j})
                         -G_{AB}\omega_i^A\omega_j^B.
\end{align*}
The matrix $\sigma$ is positive definite. Stationarity removes
all $\theta$ dependence, but $Y$ need not be an ambient Killing
field.

On $O\times P$, the isometry generated by $\mathbf Z$ preserves the
horizontal fields $E_i=\partial_{x^i}-\omega_i^A\partial_{y^A}$.
Expanding their commutator with $aY+b\mathbf T$ gives
\begin{align*}
 0=[\mathbf Z,E_i]
 &=-\bigl(a\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi+\partial_i a\bigr)Y
   -\bigl(a\partial_\phi\omega_i^\theta+\partial_i b\bigr)\mathbf T,\\
 0=(\mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf g)(E_i,E_j)&=a\partial_\phi\sigma_{ij}.
\end{align*}
Circularity, in the form \eqref{action:circularity}, says that
the horizontal distribution is integrable. Thus
\begin{align*}
 [E_i,E_j]&=-\Omega_{ij}^A\partial_{y^A}=0,\\
 \Omega_{ij}^A
 &=\partial_i\omega_j^A-\partial_j\omega_i^A
   +\omega_j^\phi\partial_\phi\omega_i^A
   -\omega_i^\phi\partial_\phi\omega_j^A.
\end{align*}
Define a Riemannian metric $\sigma^0$ and a one-form $\alpha$ on
$B$ by evaluation on the fixed section $\phi=0$:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sigma^0_{ij}(x)&=\sigma_{ij}(x,0),\\
 \alpha_i(x)&=-\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi(x,0).
 \end{aligned}
\]
The commutator identity gives
$\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi=-\alpha_i$ on $O\times P$.
Since the metric coefficients are smooth on $B\times P$, the
identities on $O\times P$ and their $x$ derivatives pass to
its closure. In particular, on that closure,
\begin{equation}\label{local:boundary-coefficients}
