 \begin{aligned}
 \sigma_{ij}&=\sigma^0_{ij},\\
 \partial_k\sigma_{ij}&=\partial_k\sigma^0_{ij},\\
 \partial_\phi\sigma_{ij}&=0,\\
 \partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi&=-\alpha_i,\\
 \Omega_{ij}^A&=0.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These identities apply along the incoming boundary curve and
along every level-surface patch above it.

For the metric \eqref{local:regular-metric}, let $v^i=\dot x^i$ and put
\[
 p_A=\mathbf g(\partial_{y^A},\dot\gamma)
     =G_{AB}(\dot y^B+\omega_i^Bv^i).
\]
The stationary momentum is zero. Writing
$\ell=p_\phi=\mathbf g(Y,\dot\gamma)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{local:brake-lift}
 \begin{aligned}
 \dot y^A&=G^{A\phi}\ell-\omega_i^Av^i,\\
 G^{\phi\phi}&=-\frac{x^1}{W[\mathbf T,Y]}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The $\phi$ Euler--Lagrange equation for
$\mathcal L=\frac12\mathbf g(\dot\gamma,\dot\gamma)$ is
\begin{align*}
 \dot\ell
 &=\tfrac12\partial_\phi\sigma_{ij}v^iv^j
               +\ell\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi v^i\\
 &=-\ell\alpha_i v^i.
\end{align*}
This momentum varies because $Y$ is only intrinsically parallel.
To compute the transverse equation, write
$w^A=\dot y^A+\omega_i^Av^i=G^{A\phi}\ell$. Then
\[
 -\partial_iG_{AB}w^Aw^B
 =-\ell^2G^{A\phi}(\partial_iG_{AB})G^{B\phi}
 =\ell^2\partial_iG^{\phi\phi}.
\]
The $x^i$ Euler--Lagrange equation is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{local:transverse-euler-lagrange}
\begin{aligned}
 0&=\frac d{d\tau}(\sigma_{ij}v^j+\ell\omega_i^\phi)
    -\frac12\partial_i\sigma_{jk}v^jv^k
    -\frac12\partial_iG_{AB}w^Aw^B
    -\ell\partial_i\omega_j^\phi v^j\\
 &=\sigma^0_{ij}(\nabla^{\sigma^0}_v v)^j
    +\frac{\ell^2}{2}\partial_iG^{\phi\phi}
    +\dot\ell\omega_i^\phi
    +\ell(\partial_j\omega_i^\phi-\partial_i\omega_j^\phi)v^j
    +\ell\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi\dot\phi\\
 &=\sigma^0_{ij}(\nabla^{\sigma^0}_v v)^j
    +\frac{\ell^2}{2}\partial_iG^{\phi\phi}
    -\ell\Omega_{ij}^\phi v^j
    +\ell^2\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi G^{\phi\phi}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The cancellation in the last line is
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \dot\ell\omega_i^\phi
    +\ell\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi\dot\phi
 &=\ell\omega_i^\phi\partial_\phi\omega_j^\phi v^j
   +\ell\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi
           (G^{\phi\phi}\ell-\omega_j^\phi v^j)\\
 &=\ell^2\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi G^{\phi\phi}
   +\ell(\omega_i^\phi\partial_\phi\omega_j^\phi
          -\omega_j^\phi\partial_\phi\omega_i^\phi)v^j.
\end{aligned}
\]
Together with
$\ell(\partial_j\omega_i^\phi-\partial_i\omega_j^\phi)v^j$,
the last term is precisely $-\ell\Omega_{ij}^\phi v^j$.
It vanishes by circularity. Substituting
\eqref{local:boundary-coefficients} leaves the smooth system
\begin{equation}\label{local:mechanical-reflection}
 \begin{aligned}
 \nabla^{\sigma^0}_v v
 &=\frac{\ell^2}{2}\operatorname{grad}_{\sigma^0}
                 \left(\frac{x^1}{W[\mathbf T,Y]}\right)
   -\frac{\ell^2x^1}{W[\mathbf T,Y]}\alpha^\sharp,\\
 \dot\ell&=-\ell\alpha(v).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here $\alpha^\sharp$ is taken with respect to $\sigma^0$.

At contact the initial data are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 x(0)&=x(p_0),\\
 v(0)&=0,\\
 \ell(0)&=\lambda\mathbf g(\mathbf T,Y)(p_0)\ne0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The last inequality follows because $\mathbf T,Y$ span a Lorentzian
plane and $\mathbf T$ is null there. The equations
in~\eqref{local:mechanical-reflection}, together with $\dot x=v$,
form a smooth first-order system for $(x,v,\ell)$ with $x\in B$. The coordinate
equation for $\dot v^i$ has right-hand side
\[
 -\Gamma(\sigma^0)^i_{jk}(x)v^jv^k
 +\frac{\ell^2}{2}(\sigma^0)^{ij}
               \partial_j\left(\frac{x^1}{W[\mathbf T,Y]}\right)
 -\frac{\ell^2x^1}{W[\mathbf T,Y]}(\sigma^0)^{ij}\alpha_j,
\]
which is even in $v$, whereas $\dot\ell=-\ell\alpha_i(x)v^i$
is odd in $v$. Thus $(x(-\tau),-v(-\tau),\ell(-\tau))$
solves the same first-order system with the same data at zero.
Uniqueness gives
\begin{equation}\label{local:brake-base}
 \begin{aligned}
 x(\tau)&=x(-\tau),\\
 \ell(\tau)&=\ell(-\tau).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Solve~\eqref{local:brake-lift} with initial point $p_0$ and the
reflected $x,\ell$. For small $\tau$ the solution stays in
$B\times P$. Its base retraces the incoming boundary arc, and
\eqref{local:plaque-invariance} consequently places its lift in
$\pi^{-1}(\partial H)$. Thus the coefficient identities
\eqref{local:boundary-coefficients} hold along the lift.
We can now check the full acceleration in the frame
$(\mathbf T,Y,E_1,E_2)$:
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf g(\mathbf D_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma,\mathbf T)&=0,\\
 \mathbf g(\mathbf D_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma,Y)
   &=\dot\ell-\tfrac12(\mathcal L_Y\mathbf g)(\dot\gamma,\dot\gamma)
     =\dot\ell+\ell\alpha(v)=0.
\end{align*}
The two remaining contractions
$\mathbf g(\mathbf D_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma,E_i)$ vanish by
\eqref{local:transverse-euler-lagrange} and
\eqref{local:mechanical-reflection}. Hence
$\mathbf D_{\dot\gamma}\dot\gamma=0$. Its tangent at contact is
\[
 \dot\gamma(0)=\frac{\ell(0)}{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,Y)(p_0)}\mathbf T_{p_0}=v_0.
\]
Uniqueness for the spacetime geodesic equation identifies this
lift with the ambient continuation. Formula
\eqref{local:isolated-contact} proves the remaining assertion.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Suppose $H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}\ne\varnothing$. Choose a connected
component of $\mathcal D_0$ meeting this set. By
Proposition~\ref{prep:residual}, its positive part is path connected
and meets $C_+$. There is consequently a path
$\eta:[0,1]\to\mathcal D_0\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$ with
$\eta(0)\notin H_0$ and $\eta(1)\in H$. Its first intersection
with the closed set $H$ lies in
$\partial H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$.

Near this point, Proposition~\ref{discrete:stalled-boundary}
gives a smooth timelike surface $\Sigma\subset\partial H$
with $B(X,X)=0$ for every null tangent vector $X$.
By \eqref{local:null-surface-acceleration}, its null curves are
null geodesics of the Lorentzian stationary quotient $(Q,h)$
up to reparametrization. Since $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$ here,
\eqref{local:null-lift} lifts either null direction to a
zero-energy spacetime null geodesic with projection in $\partial H$.

Parametrize one lift $\gamma$ by $\mathbf g_+$-arclength, starting
at $s=0$. Let $s_+>0$ be the maximal forward time for which
\eqref{action:normalized-flow} has this property. If
$s_+<\infty$, compactness of $H_0$ and
Lemma~\ref{action:escape} give
\[
 \lim_{s\uparrow s_+}(\gamma(s),\dot\gamma(s))=(p_0,v_0).
\]
The limit satisfies
\begin{align*}
 |v_0|_{\mathbf g_+}&=1,\\
 \mathbf g(v_0,v_0)&=\mathbf g(\mathbf T,v_0)=0,\\
 \pi(p_0)&\in\partial H\subset H_0.
\end{align*}
If $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p_0)>0$, the smooth boundary theorem and
\eqref{local:null-surface-acceleration} continue the lifted null curve through
$(p_0,v_0)$. If $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p_0)=0$,
Lemma~\ref{local:brake-reflection} gives the same conclusion.
Uniqueness for the normalized equation contradicts the
maximality of $s_+$ in either case. Thus the curve is defined
for all $s\ge0$, with projection in $H_0$.

Let $C_{H_0}$ be the length bound of
Lemma~\ref{action:escape}. Applying it to the segment
$0\le s\le C_{H_0}+1$ gives
\[
 C_{H_0}+1
 =\int_0^{C_{H_0}+1}|\dot\gamma(s)|_{\mathbf g_+}\,ds
 \le C_{H_0},
\]
a contradiction. Hence $H\cap\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}=\varnothing$.
The definition~\eqref{discrete:exact-domain} of $U_H$ and
Lemma~\ref{discrete:maximal-action} now give the asserted
extension of the rotational action.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{exit:global}
Assume GR, SBS, NT, and $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$.
The rotational field of Theorem~\ref{prop:continuation} extends
to $\mathbf Z\in C^\infty(T\mathbf E)$, with
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal L_\mathbf Z\mathbf g&=0,\\
 [\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]&=0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Its flow is complete, has effective period $2\pi$, and together
with the stationary flow generates an effective isometric
$\mathbb R\times U(1)$ action with nonempty rotation axis.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Fix $p\in\mathbf E$ with $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)(p)=0$. The field $\mathbf Z$ is already
Killing and commutes with $\mathbf T$ on the entire local side
$\{\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0\}$. The strict inequality~\eqref{local:ergosurface-convexity}
and Proposition~\ref{local:extension} therefore
extend this same field to a connected neighborhood $V$ of $p$.

We claim that $\mathbf T,\mathbf Z$ remain independent at $p$.
By \eqref{canon:commuting-twist}, $d(\mathbf Z\psi)=0$ on $V$.
The side $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$ fixes this constant at zero, so $\mathbf Z\psi=0$
throughout $V$. Also $\mathbf Z[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]=0$ by the Killing
equation and commutation. Suppose $\mathbf Z(p)=b\mathbf T(p)$ for a real number $b$.
Set $Y=\mathbf Z-b\mathbf T$ and $A(X)=\mathbf D_XY|_p$. Since $Y(p)=0$, we have
\begin{align*}
 A\mathbf T&=[\mathbf T,Y]|_p=0,\\
 0=X\bigl(Y[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]\bigr)|_p
   &=\mathbf g\bigl(AX,\operatorname{grad}_\mathbf g\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)\bigr)|_p,\\
 0=X(Y\psi)|_p&=\mathbf g(AX,\operatorname{grad}_\mathbf g\psi)|_p.
\end{align*}
The endomorphism $A$ is skew-adjoint. It therefore annihilates
$\mathbf T$, $\operatorname{grad}_\mathbf g\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)$ and
$\operatorname{grad}_\mathbf g\psi$. These vectors are independent by
Lemma~\ref{canon:regularity}, so $\operatorname{rank}A\le1$.
The two-form $\mathbf g(A\cdot,\cdot)$ has even rank. Hence $A=0$,
and the Killing transport equations
\eqref{local:killing-connection} imply $Y=0$ on $V$. This
contradicts $W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]>0$ on its positive side, proved in
Lemma~\ref{action:area}.

At $p$, both $\mathbf T$ and $\mathbf Z$ belong to the Lorentzian plane
$\ker d[\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)]\cap\ker d\psi$. Since $\mathbf T$ is nonzero
and null, their independence gives $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)(p)\ne0$.
Choose a sufficiently small constant $\varepsilon$ with sign
opposite to this number. The Killing field $\mathbf T+\varepsilon\mathbf Z$
satisfies
\[
 \mathbf g(\mathbf T+\varepsilon\mathbf Z,\mathbf T+\varepsilon\mathbf Z)(p)
 =2\varepsilon\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)(p)
       +\varepsilon^2\mathbf g(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)(p)<0.
\]
Thus a timelike Killing field exists near each ergosurface
point. At a point $p$ in the open ergoregion,
Lemma~\ref{action:area} instead gives the timelike Killing field
with constant coefficients
\[
 \mathbf T-\frac{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)}{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}\mathbf Z.
\]
Indeed,
\[
 \mathbf g_p\left(\mathbf T-\frac{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)}{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}\mathbf Z,
           \mathbf T-\frac{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf T,\mathbf Z)}{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}\mathbf Z\right)
 =-\frac{W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z](p)}{\mathbf g_p(\mathbf Z,\mathbf Z)}<0.
\]
Where $\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)<0$, we use $\mathbf T$ itself.

The stationary vacuum analyticity theorem of
M\"uller zum Hagen~\cite{MH70}, in the form discussed by
Tod~\cite{Tod07}, supplies analytic metric
coordinates in each of these neighborhoods. Their transition
maps are analytic: a smooth local isometry $\varphi$ has the
normal-coordinate expression
\[
 \varphi=\exp_{\varphi(p)}\circ d\varphi_p\circ\exp_p^{-1}.
\]
Consequently $\mathbf E$ is an analytic vacuum spacetime.

We apply Chru\'sciel~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{Chrusciel97}
to $\mathbf E$ and the original rotational field near the horizon.
GR and Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient} give the complete
stationary field and the globally hyperbolic simply connected
domain of outer communications. Computed inside $\mathbf E$, the
domain of outer communications is again $\mathbf E$, since a causal
curve between points of the asymptotic end lies in the original
domain of outer communications. The asymptotic estimates
\eqref{exit:asymptotic-data} imply the decay required in
that theorem: one may take regularity order $3$ and decay
exponent $3/4$. They also give zero spatial
ADM momentum:
\[
 \frac1{8\pi}\lim_{R\to\infty}
 \int_{|x|=R}(k_{ij}-\operatorname{tr}_\gamma k\,\gamma_{ij})
                    \nu^j\,dS
 =\lim_{R\to\infty}O(R^{-1})=0.
\]
Here $\gamma,k$ are the induced asymptotic data,
$\nu$ is the Euclidean unit normal, and $dS$ is Euclidean area.
The ADM four-momentum is $(M,0,0,0)$, with $M>0$ by GR.
Since $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$ and $S_0$ is spacelike,
$\mathbf g(\mathbf T,\mathbf T)>0$ at some point of $S_0$ and hence at nearby
points of $\pi^{-1}(C_+)$. At any such exterior point,
Lemma~\ref{action:area} gives $W[\mathbf T,\mathbf Z]>0$, so the original
field is not a constant multiple of $\mathbf T$.

The proof of Chru\'sciel's theorem gives a complete periodic
Killing field $Y$, commuting with $\mathbf T$, whose restriction to
$\pi^{-1}(C_+)$ has the form
\[
 Y=a\mathbf T+b\mathbf Z,
 \qquad a,b\in\mathbb R,
 \qquad b\ne0.
\]
Let $P>0$ be a period of $Y$ and fix $q\in\pi^{-1}(C_+)$.
The complete commuting flows on $\pi^{-1}(C_+)$ give
\[
 \Phi_{nPa}^\mathbf T(q)=\Phi_{-nPb}^\mathbf Z(q)
                    \qquad(n\in\mathbb Z).
\]
Let $C=\{\Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z(q):0\le\theta\le2\pi\}$.
The equivariant function $\vartheta$ of
Proposition~\ref{prep:quotient} then gives
\[
 |nPa|
 =|\vartheta(\Phi_{-nPb}^\mathbf Z q)-\vartheta(q)|
 \le\max_C\vartheta-\min_C\vartheta
                    \qquad(n\in\mathbb Z).
\]
Letting $|n|\to\infty$ yields $a=0$. Thus $Y/b$ is a complete
global extension of the original $\mathbf Z$. Killing transport
identifies it with the field on the connected domain $U_H$.
Its time-$2\pi$ map is an isometry equal to the identity near
the horizon and hence everywhere, and effectiveness follows
by restriction to the original action.

If $\Phi_s^\mathbf T=\Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z$, iteration and the same function give
$|ns|\le\max_C\vartheta-\min_C\vartheta$ for all integers $n$.
Hence $s=0$, and effectiveness of the rotations
gives $\theta\in2\pi\mathbb Z$. The product action is therefore
effective. Finally, choose a zero $q$ of $\mathbf Z|_{S_0}$ and the
outward normal $e_1$ fixed in Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}. Its
rotation invariance gives
\[
 \Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z(\exp_q(re_1(q)))
 =\exp_{\Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z q}(r\,d\Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z e_1(q))
 =\exp_q(re_1(q))
                  \qquad(0<r<\delta).
\]
These exterior curves belong to the rotation axis.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{exit:uniqueness}
Let a smooth four-dimensional stationary vacuum spacetime be
asymptotically flat and $I^+$-regular, with a connected
nondegenerate future horizon. Suppose its stationary and
rotational Killing fields generate an effective isometric
$\mathbb R\times U(1)$ action with nonempty rotation axis.
Then its domain of outer communications is isometric to a
nonextremal Kerr exterior.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0$.
Theorem~\ref{prop:continuation} and Proposition~\ref{exit:global}
give the global rotational action. By Killing transport it
agrees with the original action on the horizon neighborhood
of Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}. The stationary extension of that
neighborhood in Lemma~\ref{exit:regular-completion} therefore
extends the same action to its $I^+$-regular completion.
Theorem~\ref{exit:uniqueness} applies and gives a nonextremal
Kerr exterior.

If $\mathbf T|_{S_0}=0$, Lemma~\ref{exit:regular-completion} supplies
an $I^+$-regular completion with connected nondegenerate
horizon generated by $\mathbf T$. We use the smooth staticity
argument in Chru\'sciel, Costa, and
Heusler~\cite{CCH12}. Its global preparation,
carried out by Chru\'sciel and Costa~\cite{CC08},
places the same exterior in a spacetime with a bifurcate
Killing horizon and a Cauchy section ending at the
bifurcation surface. The maximal-hypersurface theorem of
Chru\'sciel and Wald~\cite[Theorem~4.2]{CWMax94} then gives a
maximal asymptotically flat hypersurface with that boundary.
The exterior-preserving construction applies to the smooth
Killing horizon with nonzero surface gravity and future section
supplied by Lemma~\ref{exit:regular-completion}. It supplies the
strongly causal spacetime and closed acausal section required
by the maximal-hypersurface theorem: the section has compact
boundary where $\mathbf T=0$, one asymptotically flat end, and interior
domain of dependence $\mathbf E$. This domain contains no black or
white hole relative to that end. Vacuum supplies the timelike
convergence condition, so the stationary translates of the
maximal hypersurface foliate $\mathbf E$.

We use the vacuum divergence identity in the staticity argument
of Sudarsky and Wald~\cite[Theorem~1]{SW93}. Denote the resulting
maximal hypersurface by $\Sigma$ and its bifurcation boundary by $S$.
On $\Sigma$ write
$\mathbf T=N\mathbf n+X$, where $\mathbf n$ is the future unit normal,
$X$ is tangent, and $k_{ij}=-\mathbf g(\mathbf D_i\mathbf n,\partial_j)$.
Let $\gamma$ be the induced metric and $\nabla^\gamma$ its
Levi--Civita connection. In the integrals
below, $d\mu$ and $dS$ are the induced volume and area measures.
The lapse satisfies
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Delta_\gamma N&=|k|^2N,\\
 N|_S&=0,\\
 N&\longrightarrow1\quad(r\to\infty).
 \end{aligned}
\]
For sufficiently large $R$, the negative part
$N_-:=\max\{-N,0\}$ vanishes on $\{r=R\}$ and has zero trace
on $S$. Testing the lapse equation with $N_-$ on the portion
$\Sigma_R$ bounded by these two surfaces gives
\[
 \int_{\Sigma_R}|\nabla^\gamma N_-|^2\,d\mu
 =\int_{\Sigma_R}N_-\Delta_\gamma N\,d\mu
 =-\int_{\Sigma_R}|k|^2N_-^2\,d\mu.
\]
Thus $N\ge0$. The strong maximum principle and $N\to1$ give
$N>0$ in the interior. The stationary metric equation and the
vacuum momentum constraint give
\begin{align*}
 \nabla^\gamma_iX_j+\nabla^\gamma_jX_i&=2Nk_{ij},\\
 \nabla^\gamma_i k^{ij}&=0.
\end{align*}
Using symmetry of $k$, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \nabla^\gamma_i(k^{ij}X_j)
 &=(\nabla^\gamma_i k^{ij})X_j
   +\tfrac12k^{ij}(\nabla^\gamma_iX_j+\nabla^\gamma_jX_i)\\
 &=N k^{ij}k_{ij}.
\end{aligned}
\]
If $\nu_R$ and $\nu_S$ are the outward unit normals of
$\Sigma_R$ along its two boundary components, integration gives
\[
 \int_{\Sigma_R}N|k|^2\,d\mu
 =\int_{r=R}k(X,\nu_R)\,dS+\int_S k(X,\nu_S)\,dS.
\]
On $S$, $\mathbf T=0$ implies $N=X=0$, so the second integral
vanishes. The asymptotics of the maximal hypersurface used in
Chru\'sciel and Costa~\cite{CC08} give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |k|&=O(r^{-2}),\\
 |X|&=O(r^{-1}),
 \end{aligned}
\]
and consequently
\[
 \left|\int_{r=R} k(X,\nu_R)\,dS\right|
 \le CR^2\sup_{r=R}|k|\sup_{r=R}|X|
 \le C R^{-1}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Since $N|k|^2\ge0$, exhaustion by $\Sigma_R$ yields
\[
 \int N|k|^2\,d\mu=0.
\]
It follows that $k=0$, and the stationary metric equation makes $X$ Killing
for the induced metric. On the bifurcation boundary, $X=0$ and
$\nabla^\gamma_VX=0$ for all tangent vectors $V$. The skew endomorphism $\nabla^\gamma X$
therefore has a two-dimensional kernel in a three-dimensional
space, so $\nabla^\gamma X=0$. Killing transport gives $X=0$ on the connected
maximal hypersurface. Its stationary translates cover $\mathbf E$ by
the maximal-hypersurface theorem, and hence
\[
 \mathbf T^\flat\wedge d\mathbf T^\flat=0\qquad\hbox{on }\mathbf E.
\]
Static uniqueness, due to Bunting and
Masood-ul-Alam~\cite{BuntingMasood87}, in the global form of
Chru\'sciel, Costa, and Heusler~\cite[Theorem~3.1]{CCH12},
identifies this exterior with Schwarzschild.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{prep:collar} and \eqref{prep:nonrotating-normalization}
give $\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$ in the rotating case and
$\mathbf K=\mathbf T$ in the nonrotating case, with
\[
 \mathbf K=\kappa(V\partial_V-U\partial_U),
 \qquad \kappa>0.
\]
The exterior is $U,V>0$, and its future horizon is
$U=0,V>0$ in this local normal chart.

Use the temporal function $\tau$ constructed with these normal forms, choosing an invariant normal frame
in the rotating case and any oriented orthonormal normal frame
when $\mathbf K=\mathbf T$. Recall that $\tau$ is temporal, meaning
that its gradient is everywhere past timelike, and that
$\tau=(V-U)/2$ near $S_0$.
Outside a compact neighborhood its zero level is the
hypersurface $\Sigma^0$ chosen in the proof of Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}.
We deform the zero level by choosing a smooth cutoff $\eta=1$
near $S_0$, supported where $\tau=(V-U)/2$, and replacing
$\tau$ by $\tau-\varepsilon\eta$.
For small $\varepsilon>0$ this function is still temporal.
Indeed, choose $c>0$ with $\mathbf g^{-1}(d\tau,d\tau)\le-c$ on
the compact support of $d\eta$. Then
\[
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d\tau-\varepsilon d\eta,d\tau-\varepsilon d\eta)
 \le-c+2\varepsilon|\mathbf g^{-1}(d\tau,d\eta)|
                   +\varepsilon^2|\mathbf g^{-1}(d\eta,d\eta)|
 \le-c/2.
\]
The gradient is unchanged off this support. On a fixed
neighborhood of the compact portion being deformed, choose a
smooth future timelike field $Y$ with $Y\tau=1$. Its flow
identifies the nearby levels, and
\[
 Y(\tau-\varepsilon\eta)=1-\varepsilon Y\eta>0
\]
for small $\varepsilon$. The implicit function theorem therefore
identifies the new section with a compact deformation of the
old one. We take its connected exterior portion to be
$\mathcal S$. Its temporal construction makes it spacelike and acausal
in the causally convex development of $\Sigma^0$, and hence acausal
in $\mathbf E$. Its asymptotic end is unchanged, and its closure is diffeomorphic to
$[1,\infty)\times S^2$, with boundary
\[
 C=\{(0,2\varepsilon,p):p\in S_0\}.
\]
Figure~\ref{fig:future-horizon-section}(a) shows the exterior
portion of this level, with boundary $C$, and its return to
the unchanged asymptotic slice.

The deformed section will parametrize a neighborhood of the
horizon by the stationary flow. In the rotating case the section
is invariant under the local rotations near $C$. If $\mathbf T$ were
tangent there, then $\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$ would also be
tangent. In the nonrotating case $\mathbf K=\mathbf T$.
Both cases contradict spacelikeness because $\mathbf K$ is nonzero
and null on $C$. Thus $\mathbf T$ is transverse there, and hence nearby.
At $C$, the null covector $d(UV)$ has
nonzero restriction to a spacelike three-plane, so we may use
$UV$ as a coordinate on the section near $C$.

In the rotating case the images of its small exterior portion under
the stationary flow are disjoint by Lemma~\ref{prep:collar}, using timelikeness
of $\mathbf K$ and periodicity of $\mathbf Z$. In the nonrotating case this follows
directly from acausality, since $\mathbf T=\mathbf K$ is timelike there.
Write $\iota:(-\delta,\delta)\times S_0\to\mathcal O$
for this part of the deformed section, using $UV$ as its first
coordinate and $\iota(0,p)=(0,2\varepsilon,p)$.
In the rotating case we choose $\iota$ equivariant under the
circle action on $S_0$: the section is $V-U=2\varepsilon$
near $C$, and both $U,V$ are rotation invariant.
On the product below, $UV$ denotes
this first section coordinate, independent of the stationary
parameter $t$; the separate functions $U,V$ belong to the original
normal chart.
On $UV>0$, the stationary flow gives an embedding into $\mathbf E$:
\[
 F(t,q)=\Phi_t^\mathbf T(\iota(q)),\qquad
 q\in(0,\delta)\times S_0.
\]
On $\mathbb R\times(-\delta,\delta)\times S_0$, for
$X,Y\in T_q(( -\delta,\delta)\times S_0)$ and $a,b\in\mathbb R$, define
\[
 \widehat{\mathbf g}_{(t,q)}((a,X),(b,Y))
 =\mathbf g_{\iota(q)}(a\mathbf T+d\iota(X),b\mathbf T+d\iota(Y)).
\]
Transversality makes the local maps
$F_{t_0}(t_0+s,q)=\Phi_s^\mathbf T(\iota(q))$ diffeomorphisms for
small $s$, on both sides of $UV=0$. Stationarity gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
F^{\ast}\mathbf g&=\widehat{\mathbf g}\quad(UV>0),\\
F_{t_0}^{\ast}\mathbf g&=\widehat{\mathbf g},\\
\mathcal L_{\partial_t}\widehat{\mathbf g}&=0,\\
\operatorname{Ric}(\widehat{\mathbf g})&=F_{t_0}^{\ast}\operatorname{Ric}(\mathbf g)=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus $\widehat{\mathbf g}$ is a smooth Lorentzian vacuum metric,
agreeing with the exterior metric on the identified region.
The field $\partial_t$ corresponds to $\mathbf T$ under $F$; we use
$\mathbf T$ for the resulting stationary field after gluing.
In the rotating case the maps
$(t,UV,p)\mapsto(t,UV,\Phi_\theta^\mathbf Z(p))$ preserve this metric
and agree with the original rotations on the overlap.
They define the lifted field $\mathbf Z$ on the product, with
$\mathbf Z t=\mathbf Z(UV)=0$, and extend the Hawking field by
$\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$. In the nonrotating case we extend
$\mathbf K$ by $\mathbf T$.

For the gluing to be Hausdorff, we need to separate the added
horizon from compact subsets of $\mathbf E$. Suppose, to the contrary,
that $p_j$ in the deformed section tends to $C$ while
$q_j=\Phi_{t_j}^{\mathbf T}p_j$ remains in a compact subset of $\mathbf E$.
Apply \eqref{prep:acausal-time-bound} with the acausal section
$\mathcal S$ and a compact set containing all $q_j$.
Since $p_j=\Phi_{-t_j}^{\mathbf T}q_j\in\mathcal S$, the sequence
$t_j$ is bounded. Taking subsequences with
$t_j\to t$ and $q_j\to q\in\mathbf E$
would give $p_j\to\Phi_{-t}^{\mathbf T}q\in\mathbf E$, contrary to $p_j\to C$.

We now identify the domain of outer communications by proving
that $UV\le0$ is preserved along future causal curves in the
added neighborhood. We denote the glued metric by $\mathbf g$. On the
added product, the translates of the spacelike section have conormal $dt$,
so $\mathbf g^{-1}(dt,dt)<0$ on the added neighborhood. In the rotating
case $\mathbf Z t=0$, and $\mathbf K=\mathbf T+\Omega_H\mathbf Z$ gives
$\mathbf Kt=1$. This identity also holds when $\mathbf K=\mathbf T$.
Since $\mathbf K$ is future null on $C$, the metric gradient
of $t$ is past timelike there, and hence throughout a smaller
stationary product neighborhood.
In the original normal coordinates $(U,V,x^1,x^2)$, where
$x^A$ are local coordinates on $S_0$, the generator $\partial_V$
on $U=0$ is orthogonal to $\partial_V,\partial_{x^A}$ and
$\mathbf g(\partial_U,\partial_V)>0$. Inverting that row of the
metric gives
$\mathbf g^{UU}=\mathbf g^{UA}=0$ and $\mathbf g^{UV}=\frac1{\mathbf g(\partial_U,\partial_V)}$.
It follows that
\[
 \mathbf g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_\beta(UV)\partial_\alpha
       =\frac{V}{\mathbf g(\partial_U,\partial_V)}\partial_V
       =\frac{\mathbf K}{\kappa\mathbf g(\partial_U,\partial_V)}
       \quad\hbox{on }C.
\]
Since $\mathbf Kt=1$,
\[
 \mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),dt)
       =\frac1{\kappa\mathbf g(\partial_U,\partial_V)}>0
                        \quad\hbox{on }C.
\]
Choose a closed interval $|UV|\le\delta_0<\delta$ in the
section. At $t=0$, the function
$\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),d(UV))$ vanishes on $C$, and its derivative
in the $UV$ coordinate is bounded on this compact interval
times $S_0$. The fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\[
 |\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),d(UV))|
 \le |UV|\sup_{|u|\le\delta_0,\,p\in S_0}
       \left|\partial_u\bigl[\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),d(UV))\bigr](0,u,p)\right|.
\]
The positive function $\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),dt)|_C$ has a positive
minimum. Shrinking $\delta_0$ and using stationarity therefore
gives constants $c_0,C_0>0$, independent of $t\in\mathbb R$, such that
\[
\begin{aligned}
|\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),d(UV))|&\le C_0|UV|,\\
\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),dt)&\ge c_0,\\
\mathbf g^{-1}(dt,dt)&<0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Choose $C_1>C_0/(2c_0)$. Then
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV)-C_1|UV|dt,d(UV)-C_1|UV|dt)\\
 &\quad=\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),d(UV))
       -2C_1|UV|\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV),dt)
       +C_1^2(UV)^2\mathbf g^{-1}(dt,dt)\\
 &\quad\le(C_0-2C_1c_0)|UV|\le0,\\
 &\mathbf g^{-1}(d(UV)-C_1|UV|dt,dt)\ge c_0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The metric dual of $d(UV)-C_1|UV|dt$ is future causal. Recall that $(UV)_+=\max\{UV,0\}$.
Every future causal curve in the added neighborhood, parametrized
by $t$, consequently satisfies
\begin{align}
 \frac{d(UV)}{dt}&\le C_1|UV|,\notag\\
 \shortintertext{and hence}
 \frac d{dt}(UV)_+&\le C_1(UV)_+
       \quad\hbox{almost everywhere}.\label{app:causal-barrier}
\end{align}
Integrating the inequality for $(UV)_+$ gives, for $t\ge t_0$,
\[
 (UV)_+(t)\le e^{C_1(t-t_0)}(UV)_+(t_0).
\]
Thus $UV\le0$ is preserved in the future direction. Since a
future causal path from the added region to the asymptotic end would have to
cross $UV=0$ toward $UV>0$, the domain of outer communications
remains $\mathbf E$. The same estimate keeps every causal path between
exterior points in the exterior, so the deformed section remains
acausal after gluing. A closed causal curve would have to stay
either in $\mathbf E$, where global hyperbolicity excludes it, or in
the added region, where $t$ is temporal. Thus the extended spacetime
is causal as well.
Figure~\ref{fig:future-horizon-section}(b) depicts this causal
property in the stationary coordinates $(t,UV)$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=.92cm,y=.92cm,font=\normalsize,
                    line cap=round,line join=round]
 \begin{scope}
  \draw[densely dotted,black!60,line width=.55pt]
              (-.53,-.67) rectangle (3.0,1.48);
  \draw[line width=.85pt] (-.43,-.43)--(1.98,1.98);
  \draw[line width=.75pt] (-.67,.67)--(.82,-.82);
  \node[above left] at (1.78,1.78) {$\mathcal H^+$};
  \node[right=3pt,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (.70,-.82) {$\mathcal H^-$};
  \draw[dashed,line width=.75pt] (0,0)--(4.65,0);
  \draw[densely dashed,black!60,line width=.6pt] (-.34,.70)--(.70,.70);
  \draw[line width=1.2pt] (.70,.70)--(2.52,.70)
         .. controls (3.2,.70) and (3.45,0) .. (4.65,0)--(6.5,0);
  \filldraw[fill=white,line width=.65pt] (0,0) circle[radius=2pt];
  \fill (.70,.70) circle[radius=2pt];
  \node[anchor=east,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (-.19,-.17) {$S_0$};
  \node[above left=2pt] at (.70,.70) {$C$};
  \node[above=3pt,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (1.9,.70) {$V-U=2\varepsilon$};
  \node[below=3pt,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (2.0,0) {$V=U$};
  \node[align=center] at (5.25,.87) {unchanged\\asymptotic end};
  \node at (4.0,1.65) {$\mathbf E$};
  \node at (2.85,-1.47) {(a) Deformation of the section};
 \end{scope}
 \begin{scope}[xshift=10.2cm]
  \fill[black!13] (-1.30,-.83) rectangle (0,1.8);
  \fill[black!3] (0,-.83) rectangle (2.15,1.8);
  \draw[->,line width=.9pt] (0,-.83)--(0,1.92);
  \node[above=3pt] at (0,1.92) {$\mathcal H^+:\ UV=0$};
  \draw[densely dashed,black!60,line width=.6pt] (-1.30,0)--(0,0);
  \draw[line width=1.2pt] (0,0)--(2.15,0);
  \fill (0,0) circle[radius=2pt];
  \node[above left=2pt] at (0,0) {$C$};
  \node[above=4pt] at (1.52,0) {$t=0$};
  \draw[->,line width=.9pt] (0,.35)--(0,.86);
  \node[anchor=west] at (.13,.70) {$\mathbf K$};
  \node[align=center] at (-.71,-.55) {$UV<0$};
  \node at (1.14,1.48) {$UV>0$};
  \draw[->,line width=.8pt] (1.6,-.67)
          .. controls (.90,-.45) and (.10,.39) .. (-.97,1.31);
  \draw[->,line width=.65pt] (-1.76,.44)--(-1.76,1.28);
  \node[above] at (-1.76,1.28) {$t$};
  \node at (.22,-1.47) {(b) Future causal crossing};
 \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The extension across the future horizon in Lemma~\ref{exit:regular-completion}.
In (a), the thick exterior section begins at
$C=\{U=0,V=2\varepsilon\}$ and rejoins the original section
outside the local chart (dotted box). Its dashed continuation
across the horizon is auxiliary. In the stationary coordinates
of (b), future causal curves may enter the added region $UV<0$
but cannot return to the exterior $UV>0$, by \eqref{app:causal-barrier}.}
\label{fig:future-horizon-section}
\end{figure}

\Needspace{4\baselineskip}
It remains to verify the horizon conditions in $I^+$-regularity.
The stationary field is complete by construction, and the
deformed section consists of a compact part and its original end,
with boundary $C$. On $U=0$, the original normal exponential
coordinates make $V$ affine. Hence
$\mathbf K=\kappa V\partial_V$ and $\mathbf D_{\partial_V}\partial_V=0$,
which imply
\[
 \mathbf D_{\mathbf K}\mathbf K=\kappa^2V\partial_V=\kappa\mathbf K.
\]
Stationary translation preserves this identity on the added
horizon. Together with $\mathbf Kt=1$, it gives
\[
 \mathbf D_{e^{-\kappa t}\mathbf K}(e^{-\kappa t}\mathbf K)
 =e^{-2\kappa t}
   (\mathbf D_{\mathbf K}\mathbf K-\kappa\mathbf K)=0.
\]
In the product coordinates, we identify $\{0\}\times\{0\}\times S_0$
with $C$ by $\iota$. The generator through $(0,0,p)$ is
\[
 \Phi_t^{\mathbf K}(0,0,p)
   =\begin{cases}
     (t,0,\Phi_{\Omega_Ht}^{\mathbf Z}p),&\mathbf T|_{S_0}\not\equiv0,\\
     (t,0,p),&\mathbf T|_{S_0}=0,
    \end{cases}
    \qquad p\in S_0.
\]
The rotations in the first case are complete, so the
generators are defined for all $t\in\mathbb R$ and each meets $C$
exactly once.
Their future affine parameter is unbounded since
\[
 \int_0^t e^{\kappa s}\,ds=\frac{e^{\kappa t}-1}{\kappa}
       \longrightarrow\infty\qquad(t\to\infty).
\]
In the original normal chart, the short curves with tangent
$\partial_V-\partial_U$ are future timelike. For $p\in S_0$
and sufficiently small $s>0$, we therefore have
\[
 (s,2\varepsilon-s,p)\ll(0,2\varepsilon,p).
\]
The first point lies in $\mathbf E$, so it is chronologically preceded
by a point of the asymptotic end. Hence
\[
 C\subset\partial\mathbf E\cap I^+(\mathbf M^{(\mathrm{end})}).
\]
The asymptotic data are unchanged and satisfy
\eqref{exit:asymptotic-data}.
Together with global hyperbolicity of $\mathbf E$ from GR, we have
verified $I^+$-regularity as defined by Chru\'sciel and
Costa~\cite[Definition~1.1]{CC08}. The horizon is connected with
surface gravity $\kappa>0$. In the rotating case, stationary
translation extends the local rotational action to its added neighborhood.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
